Liber Exodus
by Anselm of Laon and his school

----------------------------------------

In Hebrew VEELLE SEMOTH ( ואלה שמות ), that is, THESE ARE THE NAMES; in Greek ΕΞΟΔΟΣ, that is, THE GOING OUT.

## PROTHEMATA ON EXODUS.

[0183A]

[n: (RAB.)] In the Pentateuch Exodus excels, in which nearly all the mysteries by which the Church is instructed are figuratively expressed. For by the bodily going out of the children of Israel from Egypt our bodily going out [cj: *corporalis*; read *spiritualis*, "our spiritual going out"] from spiritual Egypt is signified. By the Red Sea, and the drowning of Pharaoh and of the Egyptians, the mystery of baptism and the destruction of spiritual enemies. By the immolation of the typical lamb, and of the deliverances of the Hebrews, the passion of the true Lamb and our redemption. From heaven manna is given, and water from the rock. *This is the bread which came down from heaven [n: (Joan. VI)]* and the teaching of Christ. On the mountain precepts and judgments are given to the people of God, that we may be made subject to heavenly disciplines. [0183B] The tabernacle and its vessels are constructed, worship and sacrifices are commanded, by which the adornment of the Church and spiritual sacrifices are signified. The mystical unction and the fragrant compounds are prepared, by which the sanctification of the spirit and the perfumes of prayer are commended. Hence Paul says: *All ate the same spiritual food, and all drank the same spiritual drink,* etc.

[n: (I Cor. X.)] Hence John also: *These things were done,* he says, *that the Scripture might be fulfilled: You shall not break a bone of him [n: (Joan. XIX)]*.

[n: (BED.)] *Veelle Semoth* ( ואלה שמות ) in Hebrew; ἒξοδος in Greek; *exodus, exitus* or *egressus* in Latin.



## CHAPTER ONE.

VERS. 5.-- *There were therefore all the souls of them that [0183C] came out of the thigh of Jacob, seventy,* etc. Seventy souls, that is, seventy disciples, together with Jacob, that is, with Christ, enter into Egypt, that is, the world, being sent, namely, to preach. Otherwise: the seventy souls that entered Egypt are taken mystically in the number of remission, so that to this age, which is figured by Egypt, after so many sins the remission of sins may be granted.

VERS. 6, 7.-- *And when he was dead,* etc. [n: (ISID. in Exod. )] So, after the true Joseph had tasted death for all, and had destroyed him who had the empire of death, the people of the faithful was multiplied. For unless the grain had died in the earth, it would have remained alone.

[n: (ORIG., hom. 1, in Exod.)] If Joseph die in thee, [0183D] that is, if thou take upon thee the mortification of Christ, and mortify thy members to sin, the children of Israel are multiplied in thee, namely, good and spiritual senses. For if the senses of the flesh be mortified, the senses of the soul grow, and as the vices die in thee the virtues are increased, and the earth multiplies thee in good works, which are ministered through the office of the body. The earth had multiplied Paul, who said: *But to remain [0184A] in the flesh is more needful for your sake.* [var: Vulg. *permanere autem in carne, necessarium propter vos*, without *magis*, "to remain in the flesh is needful for you"] For while he remains on earth, that is, in his own flesh, the Church is multiplied by believing, by winning a people for God, and by preaching the Gospel from Jerusalem as far as Illyricum.

VERS. 8-10.-- *Meanwhile there arose a new king over Egypt,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] *Allegorically.* This is the devil, who *said in his heart: There is no God;* who says to his own nation, that is, to the apostate angels: *Behold the people of the children of Israel,* those, namely, who are able to see God with the mind, is mightier than we: *Come, let us oppress them wisely.* The devil perceives that the people of Israel is great, and stronger than himself, because he has often engaged them and is often overcome. He knows that Jacob wrestled, and by the help of an angel prevailed against his adversary, and was strong [0184B] with God. He has felt the wrestlings of the other saints also, he has endured spiritual contests: he fears lest at some time war befall, and they consent to his adversaries, and, these being overcome [devicto eo], depart out of his land. It seems to me, from those things which had been shown to the patriarchs and prophets concerning the coming of Christ, that he perceives that One will come who will strip off his principalities and powers, and with confidence triumph over them and afflict them on the wood of the cross.

[n: (RAB. in Exod., tom. II.)] In the Chronicles of Eusebius and Jerome we read, etc., *as far as* and he perished in the Red Sea; there were many Pharaohs.

*[n: (ORIG. homil. 1.)] Morally.* First I wish to enquire who is the king in Egypt who knows Joseph, and who is he that knows him not. For while he was reigning who knew Joseph, the children of Israel are not said to be [0184C] afflicted, nor consumed in clay and brick; nor were their males slain and their females kept alive; but only when he began to reign who had not known Joseph. If the Lord rules us, and the sense of our mind or soul is enlightened by God, remembering that Christ has risen from the dead, then in Egypt, that is, in our flesh, our spirit holds the kingdom with justice, and does not consume the children of Israel, that is, the rational senses or the virtues, in clay and brick, nor wear them away with earthly cares. If our sense should lose the memory of these things, and should not know Christ, then the wisdom of the flesh, which is an enemy to God, succeeds to the kingdom; it addresses its own nation, that is, the bodily pleasures, and, the leaders of the vices being called to council, deliberates against the children of Israel, how they may be circumvented, oppressed [0184D] with clay, and afflicted with brick; how the Egyptians may expose the males, rear the females, and build cities and fortified cities. Nor is it to be thought that the divine books relate the deeds of the Egyptians without cause; but *the things that were written were written for our instruction [n: (Rom. XV)]*, so that thou who hearest these things, if perchance thou hast been baptized and numbered among the children of Israel, and hast received the Lord as king within thee, and after this hast willed to turn aside and to do the works [0185A] of the world, to perform the doings of earth and to fulfil the offices of clay: know that there has arisen in thee another king, who knows not Joseph, the king of Egypt; he himself compels thee to his own works, to labour at brick and clay; he himself, with taskmasters and drivers set over thee, drives thee with scourges to earthly works, that thou mayest build him cities; he makes thee run about through the world and, out of covetousness, throw into confusion the elements of sea and of land; to beat upon the court with lawsuits and to weary thy kinsmen with quarrels over a scanty clod of earth; to lie in wait against chastity, to deceive innocence, to commit things foul at home, cruel abroad, and shameful within the conscience: in things of this kind know that thou art soldiering for the king of Egypt, which is to be driven by the spirit of this world.

VERS. 11-13.-- *He set therefore over them,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] The devil, having called together his own nation, desires to oppress [0185B] the rational sense, which is now figuratively called Israel: he sets over them masters of works, who may compel them to learn the works of the flesh, according to that of Psalm CV: *They were mingled among the heathen, and learned their works.* He teaches them to build cities for Pharaoh: Phithom, which signifies *the mouth of failing,* or *of the abyss;* and Ramesses, which is *the commotion of the moth,* and On, that is, Heliopolis, which is called the city of the sun. The mouth fails when it speaks a lie apart from truth and from proofs. He was a liar from the beginning, and therefore he wills such cities to be built for himself: *the mouth of the abyss,* because the abyss of perdition is his place; his other city is *the commotion of the moth:* for he who follows him lays up treasure there where the moth destroys, and thieves dig through and steal [n: (Matth. VI)]. He builds also the City of the Sun, under a false name *transfiguring himself [0185C] into an angel of light.* In these he occupies the minds which were made to see God. Yet he foresees that wars threaten him and the ripe destruction of his nation; therefore he says, The nation of Israel prevails over us, would that it might also perceive that we prevail over it: that is to say, that I may not receive the thoughts and the evil concupiscences which he sends in, but may repel the fiery darts with the shield of faith, and in all things that he shall suggest may say: *Get thee behind me, Satan: for it is written: Thou shalt adore the Lord thy God, and him only shalt thou serve [n: (Matth. IV)]*. [var: Vulg. *Vade Satana*, without *retro*] But this also which he says, Lest perchance war befall us, and these consent with our adversaries — out of the prophetic voices he foresees that war will come upon him, and that he will be forsaken by the children of Israel, and that they will consent to his adversary and be added to [0185D] the Lord. This is what Jeremias says [n: (cap. XVII)]. *The partridge has cried out, she has gathered what she did not bring forth, she has made riches, and not in judgment: in the midst of his days they shall forsake him, and in his last days he shall be a fool.* [var: Vulg. *Perdix fovit quae non peperit… in dimidio dierum suorum derelinquet eas, et in novissimo suo erit insipiens*, "The partridge hath hatched what she did not bring forth… in the midst of her days she shall leave them, and in her latter end she shall be foolish"] He understands therefore that he himself is the partridge who gathered what she did not bring forth; and that those whom he gathered without judgment will forsake him and will follow Christ, who begot them. He himself will remain a fool, when the whole creation shall have fled for refuge to its Maker and Parent; therefore he is indignant, saying: Lest they that fight against us go forth out of our land. He does not wish us to go out of his land, that we may always bear the image of the earthly: lest, having left him, we flee to him who has prepared the kingdom of heaven, leaving the image of the earthly and taking up the image of the heavenly. Therefore he appoints masters of works, who may teach [0186A] his arts and make us craftsmen of malice. And because there are many masters of malice whom Pharaoh has set over us, who command, exact, and extort earthly works: therefore, when Christ came, he made other teachers fighting against them, who, subjecting all their principalities and powers and virtues, might defend the children of Israel from violence, and might teach them Israelite works, and to see God with the mind; to leave the works of Pharaoh, to go out of the land of Egypt, to put off Egyptian manners and the whole old man with his deeds, and to put on the new, who is created according to God.

[n: (ISID.)] Israel signifies the Christian people, Pharaoh the devil: who lays upon them the most grievous yoke of clay and brick, that is, the servitude of earthly and miry work; with straw mingled in, that is, with light and irrational [0186B] deeds: so that, all being oppressed by the burden of sins, there may be none to destroy or to overcome his kingdom.

VERS. 15.-- *And the king of Egypt spoke to the midwives,* etc. [n: (ORIG., hom. 2.)] These things we detect not only in the world, which is spiritually called Egypt, but in each one of us: the prince of this world does not wish the males to be kept alive, but the females; by the female the flesh and the affection of the flesh is designated; but the man is the rational sense, and the intellectual spirit, which can lay hold of heavenly things, understand God, and seek the things that are above. Him the prince of Egypt hates and desires to slay; but he desires that the things which are of the flesh should live, and that those which pertain to bodily matter should not only live but be increased and cultivated: for he wills that all should savour of carnal things, should seek things temporal and those that are upon the earth; that no one [0186C] should lift up his eyes to heaven; that no one should ask whence he came hither; that no one should remember paradise his fatherland. When therefore thou shalt see men leading their life in pleasures, floating in luxury, giving their labour to banquets and unchastities, know that in these the king of Egypt slays the males and keeps the females alive. But if thou see one of a thousand turned to God, lifting his eyes upward, seeking things eternal, hating delights and luxury, loving continence: him, as a male, Pharaoh desires to slay, persecutes, and assails with a thousand engines; such men he hates, he does not suffer them to live in Egypt. Hence it is that in this world the servants of God are held in contempt, exposed to insults; hence hatreds and persecutions are stirred up against them.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

*To the midwives. [n: (ORIG., ibid.)]* He strives also to corrupt the midwives. *Of whom one was called Sephora,* [0186D] that is, *sparrow; the other Phua,* namely, *ruddy* or *modest;* through these he seeks that the males be slain and the females kept alive: but the midwives feared God, and did not do as he had commanded them. The midwives have taught us, before our time, to hold a certain form of rational instruction. For midwives are physicians, and they cherish both males and females at their birth. Instruction therefore is the common rational knowledge, and it reaches almost every understanding, it instructs all and cherishes all. If anyone in it be of manly spirit, one who would seek heavenly things and follow after things divine, he would come, as one healed and made strong, the readier through these instructions to the understanding of things divine. For one of them is, as it were, a *sparrow,* which teaches loftier things and calls upward on the wings of rational teaching; [0187A] *the other, ruddy* or *modest,* is moral, it composes manners, teaches modesty and honourableness. To me, however (since Scripture says of them that *they feared God, and did not act according to the precept of the king of Egypt* ), they seem to preserve the figure of both Testaments, and Sephora, who is interpreted *sparrow,* to suit the law, which is spiritual; but Phua, who is *ruddy* or *modest,* to indicate the Gospels, which are red with the blood of Christ and glow throughout the whole world with the gore of the passion. By these, therefore, the souls that are born in the Church are tended as by midwives, because from the Scriptures all the medicines of instruction are supplied. Yet Pharaoh attempts through these to slay the males, when he suggests heresies and depraved doctrines to those who are studious in the divine Scriptures. But the foundation of God stands [0187B] unmoved. For the midwives fear God, that is, they teach the fear of God, which is the beginning of wisdom. Thus what follows is the more fittingly adapted, that because the midwives feared God, they made themselves houses. Which according to the letter has no consequence: for a house is not made for the reason that God is feared. But if thou see how the Scriptures of the New and the Old Testament, teaching the fear of God, make houses for the Church, and fill the whole world with houses of prayer, it will seem reasonably written. Thus therefore these midwives, because they fear God, that is, teach the fear of God, do not do the precept of the king of Egypt, but keep the males alive; nor yet is it said that they obeyed by keeping the females alive. But I say confidently, they do not keep the females alive. For neither are vices nor [0187C] luxury taught in the Church, nor are sins nourished, which is what Pharaoh wishes when he bids the females be kept alive; but virtue alone is cultivated and nourished. But let us refer these things to each single person. If thou fearest God, thou wilt not do the precept of the king of Egypt: for he commands that thou live in delights and covet things present; thou, if thou fearest God and dost exhibit the office of a midwife, if thou desirest to profit thine own soul, keepest the males alive, that is, thou dost heal and cherish the inner man who is in thee, and through good acts and understandings dost gain eternal life.

VERS. 16-21.-- *If it be a male,* etc. [n: (ISID.)] *Allegorically.* Pharaoh bids the males be killed, the females live: so the devil, lest the strength of faith prevail, the virtues being slain, wills the vices to live, and strives to kill the strong and manly senses of the mind by which we savour of things divine, that is, [0187D] reason, prudence, constancy, innocence, and faith; and that which is womanish, weak, frail and prone to vices he desires to survive, that is, ambition, violence, lust, wrathfulness, and things like these, which are the figure of women.

VERS. 19.-- *Who answered,* etc. [n: (AUG. in Gen., tom. III.)] It is asked whether such lies are approved by divine authority, etc., *as far as* but those whose conversation is already in heaven, I do not think ought to shape their measure of speech concerning truth and falsehood by the example of the midwives.

[n: (GREG., Moral., lib. XXXVIII, cap. 2, 3.)] A lie is above all things to be guarded against, etc., *as far as* for by sparing [0188A] they endeavoured to cover the life of the infants, by lying, their own.

[n: (AUG., in lib. de Locutionibus in Exod., tom. III.)] And because the midwives feared God, etc., *as far as* so that what is consequently set forth concerning the wages to be fixed by Laban may seem to pertain to the making of that very house.

VERS. 22.-- *Pharaoh therefore commanded,* etc. [n: (ORIG., homil. 2.)] The Egyptians, whom Pharaoh here commanded to keep only the females alive, hated the males. For they hated the virtues, they nourished the vices. Now also the Egyptians lie in wait, if a male be born to the Hebrews, that they may slay him at once, unless they beware, keep watch, and hide him. Scripture relates that someone of the tribe of Levi begot a male, and saw that the infant was goodly [0188B] and hid him. Let us not, therefore, do our justice before men, but with the door shut let us pray to our Father in secret, and let not the left hand know what the right hand does, lest it be snatched away by the Egyptians, be set upon, be drowned in the waves. If I give alms because it is the work of God, I beget a male; but if I seek praise from men, and do not hide it, it is seized by the Egyptians, it is plunged into the river, and with so much labour and so much zeal I have begotten a male for the Egyptians.

*Whatsoever is of the female [n: (HIERON., in Ecclesiasten., c. 3)]*. Wherever in the Scriptures a woman is read of, and the frailer sex, let us transfer it to the understanding of matter. Whence Pharaoh too does not wish males to be born, but females, who are near to matter; and, on the contrary, none of the saints is related, save very rarely, to have begotten daughters. And [0188C] Salphaat alone, who died in his sins, begot all daughters: Jacob is the father of one daughter, and on her account is brought into peril.



## CHAPTER II.

VERS. 1-5.-- *He went out,* etc. [n: (RAB. in Exod., tom. II.)] Hence Josephus says: But Amram, fearing lest, being seized, he should fall under the king's anger, etc., *as far as* or to lead out, as Moses was led out of the river.

*Alleg.* The parents of Moses, whose faith and works the natural law adorned [n: (ID., ibid.)], *as far as* she herself also is taught to blush to worship idols.

*A man of the house,* etc. [n: (DIOD.)] Not without design is the father of Moses passed over: for Christ also, whose type Moses bore, is without a father.

[0188D]VERS. 2.-- *And seeing,* etc. [n: (CHRYSOST., hom. 54 in Act. apost.)]. It is wonderful that not through those things which seem to be of safety, but plainly through their contraries, all things are done for us. Pharaoh ordered the boys to be cast into the river; unless the boys had been cast in, Moses would not have been saved: when he was being kept safe, he was not in honour; when he was exposed, then he was made to be in honour. A Jew threatened him, saying: *Wilt thou kill me?* and this profited him, that he might philosophize in solitude and live in safety.

*[n: (ORIG., hom. 2.)] Allegorically.* Pharaoh's daughter is the Church gathered from the Gentiles, to whom it is said by the prophet: *Forget thy people, and thy father's house, for the king hath desired thy beauty [n: (Psal. XLIV)]*. [var: Vulg. *Et concupiscet rex decorem tuum*, "And the king shall desire thy comeliness"] [0189A] This, therefore, is she who went out of her father's house and came to the waters, that she might be washed from the sins which she had contracted in her father's house: and at once she took on bowels of mercy, and had pity on the infant; and she, coming to the marsh, found Moses lying exposed by his own, and caused him to be nursed. He is nursed among his own, there he passes his infancy; being made stronger, he is brought to her and is adopted as a son. Moses signifies the law. The Church, therefore, coming to the waters of baptism, received the law shut up within the tibin and smeared with bitumen. Now the tibin is a kind of covering woven out of papyrus, and formed of rods or of the bark of trees. The law therefore lay shut up within coverings of this sort, smeared with pitch and bitumen, that is, with the base and foul understandings of the Jews; it lay soiled and forgotten until the Church should come from the Gentiles and take it up [0189B] out of the miry and marshy places into the halls of wisdom and the royal roofs. Yet it passes its infancy among its own: for among those who do not understand it spiritually it is little, and an infant, and has the food of sucklings; when it comes to the Church, Moses is stronger and more robust; for the veil of the letter being removed, perfect food is found in the reading of him. The wages of the nursing she receives from Pharaoh's daughter, with whom the law is born and nursed. But what is it that the Synagogue receives from the Church? I think it is that which Moses says: *I will lead you into emulation by that which is not a nation: by a foolish nation I will stir you up to wrath.* [var: Vulg. *Ego ad aemulationem vos adducam in non gentem : in gentem insipientem, in iram vos mittam*, "I will bring you to emulation… I will send you into wrath"] The Synagogue therefore receives this wage from the Church, that it should no longer worship idols: for seeing the Gentiles so turned to God that they no longer know idols, it blushes any longer [0189C] to worship idols. We also, if we have had Pharaoh for a father, if he begot us in evil works, when we shall have come to the waters, let us take up the law of God: let not the letters be foul to us as an obscure covering; let us eat those things of it which are small and of milk; let us take up those which are perfect, and let us store them within the royal roofs of the heart. We have a great and strong Moses: let us think nothing small of him, nothing base, but all magnificent, all excellent, all elegant: for all is great that is spiritual and of a lofty understanding.

VERS. 6-8.-- *And seeing in it a little one,* etc. [n: (THEOD.)] *Mystically.* This above all could betray him to her, for the Egyptians had not yet admitted such a mystery, although in a later time they observed the law of circumcision [0189D] in imitation of the Hebrews. Whence Jeremias: *I will consider all that cut round the foreskin, the Egyptians and the sons of Edom. [n: (Jer. IX)]* [var: Vulg. *visitabo super omnem qui circumcisum habet praeputium, super Aegyptum, et super Juda, et super Edom*, "I will visit upon everyone that hath the foreskin circumcised, upon Egypt, and upon Juda, and upon Edom"]

[n: (ISID.)] Moses, that is, Christ, is found by the faithful at the river of the laver and the water of baptism, etc., *as far as* and receiving him with religious observance they guard him.

VERS. 9-11.-- *And when he was grown she delivered him,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] Josephus relates that Pharaoh's daughter brought Moses, when grown, to her father, who set his own crown upon him, which Moses at once threw down, because he saw a graven image sculptured there. Whence the priest who was present wished to kill him, saying that Egypt was to be overthrown by him, as he had [0190A] received from the oracle of the demons. But Pharaoh's daughter delivered him. And when he was of full age, he was sent by Pharaoh against the Ethiopians. And since he knew that he would journey through deserts full of serpents, he took ibises, that is, Egyptian storks, and, having measured out his camp, brought them forward, that they might devour the serpents and put them to flight: and thus the army passed the night in safety. When therefore he had come to the city which he was to storm, the queen of the Ethiopians fell in love with him, and according to the agreement delivered the city to him and married him: hence it is that Aaron and Mary quarrelled against Moses on account of his Ethiopian wife.

VERS. 12.-- *And when he had looked about this way and that.* [n: (Aug. Quaest. in Exod. )] Concerning this deed of Moses we have disputed sufficiently in that work which we wrote concerning the life of the patriarchs [0190B] against Faustus: was the natural disposition in him praiseworthy, by which he committed this sin, as rich land is praised, before useful seeds are sown, by a certain wildness of useless herbs: or is the deed itself to be justified? Which does not seem so, because he as yet bore no lawful power, neither received from God nor ordained by human society: and yet, as Stephen says in the Acts of the Apostles, he thought his brethren understood that by him God would give them salvation; so that by this testimony Moses may seem to have been able to dare this, having been already divinely admonished (which Scripture in that place passes over in silence).

[n: (ISID.)] *Allegorically.* Moses, that is, Christ, slew the Egyptian, that is, the devil, who is injurious to us in this pilgrimage, by defending us, and in the sand [0190C] buried him slain. For he lies hidden in those who have no stable foundation. But he builds the Church upon a rock, and likens those who hear his word and do it to a prudent man, who built his house upon the firm rock. But others he likens to a fool, who builds upon the sand.

VERS. 13.-- *And going out the next day,* etc. In this Moses bore the figure of Christ, who as the corner stone strove to make peace in himself between the discord of the two peoples. He who did the injury and despised the reconciler is the Jewish people, which rejected the preaching of Christ reconciling, and persisted an enemy of the believing people. Whence John IX: *Be thou his disciple, we are the disciples of Moses.*

VERS. 14-21.-- *Moses feared,* etc. [n: (AUG.)] If thus [0190D] this word has been divulged. Two things are to be noted in this expression: namely, that the sentence hangs and is so left; and then, that he put the word for the deed.

VERS. 22.-- *Who bore,* etc. *Allegorically.* Christ joined to himself the Church from the Gentiles, which by the gift of the sevenfold Spirit is one in the catholic faith. Whence: *One is my dove;* which bears a son who is called *a stranger,* and another who is *God's help:* for the saints are strangers upon the earth, but they never lack the help of God.

VERS. 23-25.-- *But after much time,* etc After Moses had taken a wife in the land of Madian, the king of Egypt died: because when Christ converted the nations throughout the world [0191A] to the faith, the principality of the devil was destroyed.

*Groaning.* Unless the king of Egypt die in thee, thou dost not know thyself to be oppressed by the servitude of Egypt, and by the works of clay and brick; nor dost thou groan in heart, nor cry out to the Lord that he may free thee.



## CHAPTER III.

VERS. 2, 3.-- *And there appeared to him.* [n: (ISID.)] *Allegorically.* There was a flame in the bramble, and it was not burnt up. The bramble, the thorns of the sins of the Jews: the flame in the bramble, the word of God, that is, the law given to that people. But the flame does not burn up the bramble, because the law that was given does not consume their sins. Others understand by the flaming bramble the Church, which is set aflame by persecutions, and which, [0191B] when the Lord speaks to her, is not burnt up. That the Lord appeared to Moses in the bramble shows that he appears to the faithful in the Church, and nowhere else. The Hebrews say that God appeared to Moses in a bramble for this reason, that the Jews might not be able to carve themselves an idol out of it. For God always cuts away the occasion of idolatry.

[n: (GREG. lib. XXII Moral., cap. 20.)] Moses, mingled with the Egyptians, was as it were awake to the world, and therefore did not hear the voice of the Lord: but when the Egyptian had been slain, after he fled into the desert, while he dwelt there forty years, he fell asleep to earthly tumults, and therefore he perceived the divine voice. For when the mind is lulled from outward things, the word of God penetrates more truly. For the throng of earthly thoughts, clamouring about it, closes the ear of the heart. For a man does not perfectly suffice for both things [0191C] when he is divided, because if he opens his hearing outwardly, he grows deaf within.

VERS. 4.-- *And the Lord seeing,* etc. [n: (GREG., praef. in Moral., c. 2.)] The angel who is described as having appeared to Moses is recorded now as the Lord, now as an angel: an angel, because by speaking outwardly he serves; the Lord, because presiding inwardly he bestows the efficacy of speaking. For when he who speaks is governed from within, he is recorded both as an angel through his service, and as the Lord through his inspiration.

*He called him out of the midst,* etc. [n: (GREG. lib. XXVIII Moral., c. 5.)] Often God speaks through angels to earthly and to heavenly substances at once: as here, when he joined the fire and the bramble, and united one thing above and another below. Which is then only done, when something [0191D] great is signified by the conjunction. For in addressing Moses through the kindled bramble, he showed that he would become a teacher of the people, one who should both receive the flame of the law and not shun the thorn of sin: so that from that people should come forth one who by the fire of the Godhead should take the sins of our flesh as it were the thorn of the bramble, and should preserve the substance of his humanity unconsumed in that very flame of the Godhead.

VERS. 5-7.-- *Loose thy shoe,* etc. [n: (THEOD.)] For two reasons he bids him loose his shoes: first, that he may render him more and more religious and reverent, so that he may receive his commands with trembling; then also he is taught by what reason the priests should minister in the tabernacle of God: for they discharged the priestly office with bare feet.

[0192A] [n: (ISID.)] Because, namely, no one can worthily stand, or see God, unless he lay aside all things earthly and mortal. Otherwise, it was the custom of the ancients that if a bridegroom repudiated his bride, in sign of the repudiation he should be unshod. Therefore Moses is bidden to be unshod, and not to approach shod, as a bridegroom, to the Church, which is signified in the bramble; for this is to be reserved for Christ, who is the true bridegroom. Of whom John says: « Whose shoe's latchet I am not worthy to loose [n: (Joan. I)].

VERS. 6.-- *I am the God,* etc. [n: (HILAR. lib. IV de Trinitate.)] We must not speak of God otherwise than as he himself has spoken of himself to our understanding. It is an angel of God who appeared in the fire out of the bramble: and out of the bramble in the fire God speaks; thou hast the dispensation [0192B] in the angel, because in the angel it is an office, not a nature; thou hast God in the name of nature, because the angel of God is God.

VERS. 7.-- *I have seen the affliction,* etc. [n: (GREG., Moral. )] For minds scattered abroad it is hard for each to return to himself: because evil ways hold those once taken so delightfully, in proportion as what has pleased is not lawful. Because, when the gift of discipline does not stand in the way, no penalty of retribution is foreseen to terrify. But with the eyes of the heart closed the soul is cast headlong, and penetrates temporal evils the more securely, the more harshly it despairs of eternal goods. But this wickedness of the reprobate separates the life of the elect, as grains from chaff, and while it afflicts, it sets free: because while it presses violently, it compels men to hasten to things above; which in the Israelite [0192C] people, when Moses called and Pharaoh raged, is signified. For Moses was sent to call, when Pharaoh pressed with hard works, so that the one by calling might draw, the other by raging might drive, that the people, basely fixed in servitude, might be moved either by being called or by being driven; this is done daily while, the heavenly rewards being preached, the reprobate are permitted to rage against the elect: so that those whom Egypt, that is, the present life, has oppressed by flattering, it may help by pressing; so that while God calls, torment may drive.

VERS. 8-13.-- *And I will bring out of the land,* etc. [n: (GREG., homil. 32.)] To a rude people something had to be promised concerning the present life, that it might be able to be more sturdily strengthened in the future. For if it did not receive small things, it would not believe great ones. God therefore by bestowing earthly things persuades to heavenly things also, [0192D] so that receiving what it saw, it might hope for what it did not see.

*Into a good and spacious land, in a land which flows with milk,* [var: Vulg. *in terram quae fluit lacte et melle*, accusative *in terram*, "into a land which floweth with milk and honey"] etc. [n: (Ibid.)] Present prosperity is sometimes given that it may call to a better life, sometimes that it may more fully condemn for ever. Hence to the Israelites the land of Chanaan is promised, that they may at some time be called to hope for things eternal: because a rude people would not believe promises, unless it received something near at hand from the promiser. Not therefore by hope alone is it drawn to things, but sometimes by things to hope. Whence Psalm CIV: *And he gave them the regions of the nations, and they possessed the labours of the peoples, that they might keep his justifications and seek after the law.*

[0193A]VERS. 14-16.-- *He who is hath sent me to you,* etc. Moses is sent to punish Pharaoh and at last to slay him: the law was sent to destroy the devil.

Jerome, writing to Marcella concerning the ten names of God, puts in the sixth place the name *ehieh* ( אהיה ), which is read in Exodus: *He who is hath sent me to you.* For God alone, who has no beginning, holds the name of true essence: which was spoken by an angel to Moses when he asked his name, because in comparison with him who truly is, because he is unchangeable, the things that are changeable are as though they were not; for what is said to have been, is not: and what is said will be, is not yet. But God only is, who knows not to have been, or to be about to be. And the Father alone with the Son and the Holy Spirit [0193B] truly is. Compared with whose essence, our being is not; whence also we say: God lives, because the divine essence lives by a life which death does not possess.

VERS. 17-20.-- *To a land flowing with milk and honey,* etc. [n: (AUG., Quaest. 4 in Exod.)] Ought we to take this spiritually, because according to propriety this was not the land which was given to the Hebrews? Or is it a manner of speech by which the richness and sweetness of the land is praised?

VERS. 21.-- *You shall not go out,* etc. [n: (AUG., ibid.)] That the Lord commanded the Hebrews to take from the Egyptians vessels of silver and of gold, and raiment, and added, *and you shall make spoil of them,* [var: Vulg. *et spoliabitis Aegyptum*, "and you shall spoil Egypt"] the judgment of this command cannot be unjust. For it is a command of God, concerning which there was no judging, but obeying. For he himself [0193C] knows how justly he commanded; it belongs to the servant to do obediently what he commanded.

VERS. 22.-- *Vessels of silver.* We ask silver and gold from the Egyptians, whereof to fashion a tabernacle for God, when we read the poets and the philosophers, that we may expound the divine Scripture more wisely and more eloquently.



## CHAPTER IV.

VERS. 2-5.-- *What is that which,* etc. [n: (GREG.)] Moses is the Jewish people; the rod, the power of the Godhead; the serpent, Christ's mortality: for since death came by a serpent, by a serpent mortality is signified. Or because it is written in Matthew X: *Be ye prudent as serpents;* [0193D] and because the highest prudence, that is, the wisdom of God, was made flesh, his mortality is rightly figured in the serpent. Whence John IV: *As Moses lifted up the serpent in the desert, so must the Son of man be lifted up.* Moses held the rod, because the Jewish people, before the coming of the Redeemer, trusted in the power of God; he cast it upon the ground, because through the patriarchs and prophets it announced that God was to be made flesh; the rod is turned into a snake, because Christ, remaining God in himself, was made mortal in man. But Moses also, beholding the snake, was afraid and fled, because the Jewish people, when it saw Christ mortal, was terrified to believe him God. He is bidden to hold its tail, because the people which now refuses to believe will gather itself to the faith in the uttermost part of the Lord's body, that is, in the later time of the Church, [0194A] namely at the end of the world; holding the tail of the snake, because him whom it had before despised as mortal, it will confess in the last part of the Church to be its own Redeemer. Presently the serpent returns into a rod, because as soon as the Jewish people shall have believed in Christ, he will presently appear unto judgment in the power of his Godhead: so that now the serpent is a rod, because he who on earth was a man despised will, coming from heaven, be seen as God above the angels.

[n: (ISID.)] The serpent persuaded man to death. Death therefore comes from the serpent; the rod in the serpent is Christ in death. Moses was afraid and fled: for when Christ was dead, the disciples were afraid, falling back from the hope in which they had been. He took hold of the tail, that is, of the hinder parts, and it was made a rod: because, first slain, afterwards, when all was accomplished, [0194B] he returned by rising again to that which he had been, where, death being swallowed up by life, nothing of the serpent appeared in him. Or the tail of the serpent is the end of the world, because thus the mortality of the Church is rolled onward through slippery times; some go, others come, by death, as it were by the serpent, through whom death was sown: but at the end of the world, as at the tail, we return to the hand of God, and being laid hold of we shall be restored; and when the last enemy, death, has been destroyed, we who rise again shall be at the right hand of God a rod of the kingdom.

VERS. 6, 8.-- . . . *Put forth thy hand,* etc. [n: (PROCOP.)] Some assert that that portent came to pass in order to show that the law is not laid down for the just man. Although afterwards he ratifies laws by what manner the disease of leprosy is to be examined and the unclean man declared, yet very many [0194C] laws are so delivered as though they were given to fools and to little children. So far does God accommodate himself to our weakness!

[n: (ISID.)] The hand is white, that is, unclean, for whiteness in the skin is leprosy, not fairness. For the very inheritance of God, that is, the people, being sent forth from him, was made unclean. Whence Psalm LXXIII: *Why turnest thou away thy hand and thy right hand,* etc. But the hand was called back into the bosom, and returned to its own colour: so the Jewish people, estranged from the bosom of God, remained unclean without; but being called back, it will return to its former colour when it shall have acknowledged the Saviour. *For blindness in part hath happened in Israel [n: (Rom. XI)]*.

VERS. 9, 10.-- *Take water,* etc. [n: (ISID.)] The water signifies the people; whence Apocalypse XVII: *The waters which thou sawest are peoples and nations.* The people therefore is turned into [0194D] blood, that is, into faith in the blood of Christ.

VERS. 10.-- *I beseech thee, Lord,* etc. [n: (AUG. Quaest. in Exod. )] As to what Moses says to the Lord: *I pray thee, Lord, I am not eloquent since yesterday nor since the day before, nor since thou hast begun to speak to thy servant,* it is understood that he could believe himself to be made eloquent suddenly by the will of God, when he says: *nor since thou hast begun to speak to thy servant,* as though showing that it could have come to pass that one who had not been eloquent before yesterday and the day before should suddenly be made so from the time when the Lord began to speak with him.

[n: (ORIG. homil. 3 in Exod.)] While Moses was in Egypt, and was being instructed in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, he was not of a slender voice, nor slow of tongue, nor did he declare [0195A] himself uneloquent. For he was, as regards the Egyptians, of a sonorous voice and of incomparable eloquence. But when he heard the voice of God and received the utterances of the Lord, he perceives his voice to be slender and thin and slow, and his tongue hindered, and he pronounces himself dumb, when he begins to know the true Word, which was in the beginning with God [n: (Joan. I)]: for though an untaught man be compared with dumb animals, he will seem eloquent; but if with the learned and the eloquent, foolish and dumb. But if anyone should regard the word of God and the divine wisdom, weighing it far more highly than it is weighed among us, before God he will profess himself a dumb animal. Whence Psalm LXXII: *As a beast of burden was I driven before thee.* [var: Vulg. Ps 72:23 *ut jumentum factus sum apud te*, "as a beast of burden was I made before thee"] Since therefore he advanced so far in understanding as to know himself, which is a great part of wisdom, [0195B] the divine condescension rewarded him, saying: *I will open thy mouth,* etc. Blessed are they whose mouth God opens that they may speak. Whence Psalm LXXX: *Open wide thy mouth, and I will fill it.* Likewise Paul says to the Ephesians VI: *That speech may be given me in the opening of my mouth.* The mouth, then, of those who speak true things God opens; the mouth of those who speak a lie, false witness, buffooneries, filthiness, and of whisperers too and detractors, and of those who speak idle things, the devil opens. Of Judas it is related in John XIII, *that Satan entered into him, and put it into his heart to betray him.* His mouth therefore he opened, that he might speak with the Pharisees how he might betray him for money received. Now it is no small grace to discern the mouth which God opens and that which the devil opens. God also opens the ears [0195C] of the saints to hear the divine words; whence: The Lord will open my ear. He opens the eyes, as he opened the eyes of Agar, and she saw a well of living water; whence Eliseus: *Open, Lord, the eyes of thy servant, that he may see, for there are more with us [n: (IV Reg. VI)]*. But the ear which is opened by the instruction of the Lord is sometimes open, sometimes shut; whence: Thou shalt not receive a vain hearing [n: (Eccli. XLV)]. If vain, profane, filthy things are spoken, he who knows the instruction of the Lord shuts his ear and says: *But I was as one dumb, who opened not his mouth, and as one deaf I heard not [n: (Psal. XXXVII)]*. But if the things spoken pertain to the profit of the soul, if the discourse is of God, if it teaches morals, invites to virtues, cuts away vices, let the ears lie open and the whole door of the soul. And it is to be noted that the law used the greatest moderation [0195D] in saying: *Thou shalt not receive a vain hearing;* it did not say, Thou shalt not hear; but, *Thou shalt not receive.* For we often hear vain things: for Marcion and Valentinus, and all who dispute against the Creator, speak vain things; yet we frequently hear them that we may contradict them, but we do not receive them, because the devil opens their mouth. May the Lord vouchsafe to open our mouth, that we may be able to refute the gainsayer, and to stop the mouth which the devil opens.

VERS. 11.-- *Who made the mouth of man?* etc. [n: (AUG., Quaest. 8.)] There are those who calumniate God, or rather the Scripture of the Old Testament, because God said that he made the dumb. What then do they say of Christ, who says openly in John X: *I am come, that they who see not may see; [0196A] and they who see may be made blind.* And who, save one who does not know, would believe that anything can befall a man in respect of bodily defects which God does not will? But that he wills the whole justly no one doubts.

VERS. 12, 13.-- *Go therefore,* etc. [n: (AUG.)] *Allegorically.* As to what the Lord says to Moses: *But now go thou, and I will open thy mouth and instruct thee what thou art to speak,* [var: Vulg. Ex 4:12 *Perge igitur, et ego ero in ore tuo: doceboque te quid loquaris*, "Go therefore, and I will be in thy mouth, and I will teach thee what thou shalt speak"] it appears that not only the instruction of the mouth, but the very opening of it also, belongs to the grace of God. For he did not say, Open thy mouth, and I will instruct thee; but he promises both, I will open, and I will instruct. And in Psalm LXXX: *Open wide thy mouth, and I will fill it up.* Where he signifies in man the will of receiving what God gives to him that wills, so that to the beginning of the will belongs, *Open wide thy mouth,* but to the grace of God, [0196B] *and I will fill it.*

VERS. 14.-- *The Lord being angry with Moses, said,* etc. [n: (AUG. Quaest. 10 in Exod.)] He seems, as to one distrusting, not to have given the fullest faculty, etc., *as far as* whose figure Moses bears as it were in the midst between God and Aaron, and Aaron between Moses and the people.

VERS. 15-18.-- *And I will be in thy mouth and in his mouth,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] Not only to Moses is it promised that his mouth shall be opened by the Lord, but to Aaron also. For it is said: I will open thy mouth and his mouth, and I will instruct you what ye are to do.

VERS. 19.-- *The Lord therefore said,* etc. [n: (AUG., Locut. in Gen., tom. III.)] Now after many days the king of Egypt died. And the Lord said to Moses in [0196C] Madian: *Go, proceed into Egypt,* [var: Vulg. Ex 4:19 *Vade, et revertere in Aegyptum*, "Go, and return into Egypt"] etc. Many kinds of expression are to be noted in these words. For first he says: *Go, proceed into Egypt,* as though one were not enough; then, *they are all dead who sought thy life,* whereas the Scripture had said that only the king of Egypt was dead: of whom alone it had before been said that he sought to kill Moses. Or did he himself die last after the other enemies? And if it is so, it is not a manner of speech, but the sense.

VERS. 20.-- *Moses therefore took,* etc. [n: (AUG., Quaest. 12 in Exod.)] As to what is said, that Moses set his wife and his little ones upon a beast, that he might go into Egypt, but that afterwards Jethro his father-in-law met him there with them, when he had led the people out of Egypt: it is to be understood that, after that slaying which was to come [0196D] from the angel, the wife of Moses or of the child returned with the little ones. For some have thought that the angel terrified him for this cause, lest the female sex should accompany him to the hindrance of the ministry divinely laid upon him.

VERS. 21-23.-- *I will harden his heart. [n: (AUG., Serm. 88.)]* What is, *I will harden his heart,* save that, when my grace shall have been absent, his own wickedness hardens him? Just as, whenever water is bound fast by exceeding cold, it is loosed when the heat of the sun comes upon it, and when the sun departs it is hardened again: so by the cold of sins the charity of many grows cold, and they are hardened as it were into ice; and when the warmth of the divine mercy shall have come upon them, they are loosed. This we recognize in Pharao, who, as often as [0197A] the scourges were removed from him, hardened himself and raised himself up against God; as often as he was afflicted, he made supplication. There is not therefore iniquity in God. Whence it was not so much the power as the patience of God that made Pharao to be hardened. Which thing we too are wont to practise with our home-born servants, whom, when they sin frequently, we indulge, saying: I have made thee such as thou art, I by sparing thee have nourished thy insolence and thy negligence.



## CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

VERS. 24.-- *The Lord met him,* etc. [n: (AUG., Quaest. 11 in Exod.)] Another reading: *And it came to pass on the way at the resting-place, an angel met him, and sought to kill him: and Sephora, taking a pebble, circumcised the foreskin of her son, and fell down at his feet and said: The blood of the circumcision of my child hath stood, and he departed from him because of this which she said: The blood of the [0197B] circumcision hath stood.* First it is asked whom the angel wished to slay, etc., *as far as* by a great mystery, if I am not mistaken.

VERS. 25, 26.-- *Thou art to me a spouse of blood,* etc. According to history, the Gentile mother, wondering at the newness of the thing, could say to Moses: *Thou art to me a spouse of blood;* as if to say, the rite of thy nation compels me to shed the blood of my son, which among other nations is not the custom. But mystically Sephora signifies the Church from among the Gentiles, which strips the foreskin of her son, that is, of the Gentile people, with a most sharp stone, that is, with the teaching of the Holy Spirit, or with that rock of which it is said in II Corinthians X: *Now the rock was Christ,* that it may be cleansed from all defilement of flesh and spirit: *And that he may put off the old man with his deeds [n: (Col. III)]*. For the blood of circumcision stands [0197C] when this corruptible shall put on incorruption.

VERS. 27-31.-- *And the Lord said,* etc. [n: (ORIG., hom. 32.)] Aaron met Moses, and he went out of Egypt. But it matters where he meets Moses, whose mouth is to be opened by the Lord: and he meets him in the mount of God. Deservedly is his mouth opened who meets him in the mount of God. Peter, James and John in the mount deserved both to see the Lord Jesus transfigured, and Moses and Elias with him in glory. Thou also, unless thou shalt have ascended the mount of God, and there met Moses, that is, unless thou shalt have ascended the lofty ascent of the law, unless thou shalt have seized the summit of spiritual understanding, there is no door opened to thee by the Lord. If thou shalt have stood in the lowly place of the letter, thou hast not met Moses in the mount of God; God has not [0197D] opened thy mouth, nor instructed thee what it behoves thee to speak; unless Aaron had met Moses in the mount, unless he had seen his understanding to be lofty, he would not speak to him the words of God, nor would he have delivered to him the power of the signs, nor have made him a sharer of so great a mystery.



## CHAPTER V.

VERS. 1.-- . . . . . *Let my people go. [n: (ORIG., hom. 3 in Exod.)]* Moses does not will that the people, being set in Egypt, should serve God, but that it should go out into the desert, and there serve him. For while a man remains in the dark deeds of the world, and is engaged in the obscurity of business, he cannot serve God. *For ye cannot serve God and mammon [n: (Matth. VI)]*. We must go out therefore [0198A] from Egypt: the world must be left behind, not in place but in mind, if we wish to serve the Lord. Whence John says: *Little children, love not the world, nor the things which are in the world [n: (I Joan. II)]*.

VERS. 2.-- *Who is the Lord?* He expresses the pride of the devil, who, scorning to be made subject to God, says: *I will be like the Most High [n: (Isa. XIV)]*; and as though doubting of the Lord's nativity, says:

*If thou be the Son of God, say that these stones be made bread [n: (Matth. IV)]*. But afterwards, as though under constraint, the demons cried out: *Why art thou come to torment us before the time? We know that thou art the Son of God [n: (Matth. VIII)]*. After torments they know what before they had refused to know. Pharao also, when he had been scourged, asks that supplication be made for him to the Lord. *Who is the lord, that I should hear his voice?* etc. Thou seest [0198B] what raw pride does; you will see a little later how much it profits under scourges; how much better a man is made when he has been beaten; he who now says, I know not the Lord, when he has been beaten will say: Pray to the Lord for me. Let no one therefore be so untaught in the divine discipline as to think the divine scourges a ruin and a penal destruction. Behold, even Pharao, most hardened, profits when he is beaten: for before the stripes he knows not the Lord, and when beaten he asks that supplication be made for him; and profiting under punishments, he knows how to acknowledge why he is punished. *I know not,* he says, *the Lord.* In the Gospel, being beaten, he amends this utterance, for the demons cried out: *We know thee who thou art: thou art the Son of the living God.* After the scourges he will let Israel go also, and will even urge it to depart. *For there is no fellowship of light with darkness, no part of the faithful with the unbeliever [n: (II Cor. VI)]*.

[0198C]VERS. 3.-- *The God of the Hebrews hath called us,* etc. [n: (AUG. in Exod. )] It is asked how it is said to the people, etc., *as far as* that he may obtain a just judgment.

[n: (ORIG.)] *Morally.* We set out on a three days' journey from Egypt, if we so keep ourselves from every defilement of soul and of body and of spirit, that our whole spirit, and soul, and body may be preserved unto the day of Christ.

[n: (ORIG.)] We set out on a three days' journey from Egypt, if, taking away rational, moral and natural wisdom from worldly things, we turn them to the divine ordinances. We set out on a three days' journey from Egypt, if, purifying in ourselves things said, done or thought (for these are the three by which men can sin), [0198D] we are made clean of heart, that we may be able to see God. But the prince of Egypt, when he sees himself the more vehemently pressed to let the people of God go, desires in the second place to obtain that they go not farther off, that they may not set out for the whole three days, saying: *Go not far away.* He does not will the people of God to be far from him. For he wills that, if not in deed, at least in speech they should sin; if not in speech, at least in thought. He does not will that they should set out from him for the whole three days. He wills to possess in some of us one day, in others two, in others the whole three days. Blessed are they who withdraw from him the whole three days.

*A journey of three days into the wilderness, and let us sacrifice to God,* etc. [n: (ISID.)] We walk a journey of three days going out of Egypt, that we may come to the place where [0199A] we ought to offer sacrifice to the Lord. This way is Christ, who says in John XIV: *I am the way, the truth and the life.* For whosoever shall have confessed Christ with the mouth, and believed with the heart that God raised him up on the third day, shall be saved, coming to the place in which a sacrifice of praise may be offered.

[n: (STRAB.)] *A journey of three days,* etc. At the third encampment, after they had crossed the Red Sea, they came to Ethan, which place is also called Mara, because of the bitter waters. Or of three days, that is, of three months, because in the third month after they went out of Egypt they came to Sina, where they sacrificed and received the law, where the tabernacle also was set up.

[n: (ORIG., hom. 3 in Exod.)] Now also Moses, whom we have with us (for we have Moses and [0199B] the prophets), that is, the law of God, wills to lead thee out of Egypt, if thou wilt hear it; it wills to set thee far from Pharao, to rescue thee from the work of clay and of straw: if only thou wilt hear, and understand spiritually; it does not will thee to remain in the deeds of the flesh and of darkness, but to go out to the desert, namely to a place empty of the disturbances and waves of the world, and to the quiet of silence. For the words of wisdom are learned in silence and quiet. In this place thou shalt be able to offer to the Lord the law of God, and to acknowledge the power of the divine voice. Therefore Moses desires to cast thee out of the waves of business and the noise of peoples, and out of Egypt, that is, out of the darkness of ignorance, that thou mayest hear the law of God and receive the light of knowledge.

VERS. 4-8.-- *Why, Moses and Aaron, do ye stir up,* [0199C] etc. [n: (ORIG., ibid.)] While the people is with him, and works clay or brick, while it is occupied with straw, he does not think it to be perverted, but to walk rightly. But if it should say, I wish to go a three days' journey, and to serve the Lord, he says the people is perverted through Moses and Aaron. Today also, if Moses and Aaron, that is, the prophetic and the priestly word, should stir up a soul to the service of God, to go out of the world, to renounce all that it possesses, to attend to the law and the word of God: straightway thou wilt hear the friends and confederates of Pharao saying: See how men are seduced, and youths are perverted, that they may not labour, may not serve as soldiers, may not do anything that profits; leaving necessary things behind, they follow after trifles and idleness. What is it to serve God? They are unwilling to labour and seek occasions of idleness: these were then [0199D] the words of Pharao, these his friends speak now also, and not only with words, but they persecute with stripes as well. He commands the scribes of the Hebrews to be scourged, straw not to be given, work to be exacted; these things the fathers suffered, and now the people of God suffers them. They who have wholly given themselves over to the prince of this world prosper; all things, as they think, fall out happily: but to the servants of God even a small and lowly sustenance does not come. For these are the straws which were given by Pharao; whence certain men, being wearied, say to Pharao: Why dost thou afflict thy people? For being overcome by stripes they fall from the faith, and confess themselves to be the people of Pharao, for not all who are of Israel, these are Israelites.

VERS. 9-13.-- *Let them be oppressed with works and let them fulfil [0200A] them,* etc. *Mystically.* From the time when Moses began to speak to Pharao the people of God is the more afflicted: from the time when the word of God comes into the soul, the devil rises up more sharply, and sends in greater vices. For before the word of God came, which should reprove vices, they endured in peace; but when it came, a great disturbance arises.

VERS. 14-20.-- *And they were scourged who,* etc. The children of Israel were more grievously worn down after they had determined to go out of Egypt; because they who wish to renounce the world and evil works are assailed by the devil with fiercer temptations.

VERS. 21.-- *Let the Lord see,* etc. [n: (GREG. l. XXIX, Moral., cap. 14.)] When the divine light illumines the human heart, *as far as* he grieves that he as it were stinks in the eyes of Pharao. [0200B]

*Because to stink,* etc. *[n: (ORIG. Hom. 3.)] Allegorically.* They speak truth, although they know it not, like Caiphas, who said: *It is expedient that one should die for the people [n: (Joan. XVIII)]*; he knew not what he was saying. For as the Apostle says: *We are the good odour of Christ, to some unto life, to others unto death,* so the prophetic word is a sweet odour to them that believe; but to the doubting and unbelieving, and to those who confess themselves the people of Pharao, it is made an execrable odour. Moses himself also says to the Lord: From the time when I spoke with Pharao, thou hast afflicted thy people; because before the word of God is heard there is no tribulation or temptation: but when the trumpet of preaching shall have shown the signal of war, the battle of tribulations arises. From the time therefore when Moses and Aaron began to speak to Pharao, [0200C] the people of God is afflicted. From the time when the word of God has been uttered into thy soul, a contest is stirred up within thee of the virtues against the vices, which before endured within thee in peace. But when the word of God has begun to make the distinction between one thing and another, a great disturbance arises, and without treaty a war is born: because injustice does not agree with justice, nor drunkenness with sobriety, nor lying with truth. Therefore let us not be troubled if we seem an execrable odour to Pharao. For the execration of vice is called virtue. But as Moses stood before Pharao, let us also stand; let us not be bent, *having our loins girt about with truth, and our feet shod with the preparation of the Gospel of peace [n: (Ephes. VI)]*. And we stand confidently, we beseech the Lord that he set our feet upon a firm rock, lest our feet be moved. But let us stand, that is, [0200D] let us withstand, according to that of I Peter V: *Whom resist ye, strong in faith: that Satan may quickly be crushed under our feet.* For by how much the more steadfastly and bravely we stand, by so much the more weakly Pharao. But if we shall have begun to be weak or doubting, he will become stronger. For when Moses lifted up his hands, Amalec was overcome; when he let them down as though weary, he grew strong. Let us therefore in the power of the cross of Christ lift up our arms, and raise in prayer holy hands, that we may deserve the help of God. Hence James says: *Resist the devil and he will fly from you [n: (Jac. III)]*. Let us act therefore that he may not only fly, but be crushed under our feet, extinguished in the depth of the abyss.

But if we withdraw from the Egypt of vices, [0201A] we escape the waves of the world as it were by a solid path, through Jesus Christ.

VERS. 22, 23.-- *Lord, why hast thou afflicted this people?* [n: (AUG.)] These are not words of contumacy or of indignation, but of inquiry and of prayer, as appears from those things which the Lord answered. For he did not reprove his unbelief, but disclosed what he was about to do.



## CHAPTER VI.

VERS. 3-6.--. . . *And my name Adonai I did not show them.* As to thee. For through the successions of the ages the increase of divine knowledge grew. The Lord appeared to Abraham; he spoke face to face with Moses; but David says in Psalm CXVIII: *I have understood above the ancients,* etc. The Apostles beheld the Son of God present, and asked whatever they wished, to whom [0201B] it is also said in Luke II: *Many kings and prophets,* etc.

VERS. 7, 8.-- *Out of the workhouse of the Egyptians.* Ergastulum and ergasterium, that is, a workshop: namely where the accused were bound fast for the doing of works.

VERS. 9-11.-- *They did not acquiesce in him,* etc. Note the clemency of God: he is not believed by the children of Israel, and yet he ceases not to admonish. He is contemned by Pharao, and long-sufferingly he waits.

VERS. 12, 13.-- *Behold the children,* etc. [n: (AUG. Quaest. 16 in Exod.)] The LXX. *Behold I am of a slender voice, and how shall Pharao hear me.* Yet he does not seem to excuse himself for the slenderness of his voice on account of the multitude of the people, but on account of one man also: it is a marvel if he was of so slender a voice that he could not be heard even by one. Or [0201C] perhaps the royal disdain did not permit them to speak from near at hand.

VERS. 14-19.-- *These are the princes,* etc. [n: (AUG. Quaest. 15.)] Scripture, wishing to show the origin of Moses, which his action required, began from the firstborn of Jacob, that is, from Ruben; thence to Simeon, thence to Levi: it did not go on further, because from Levi is Moses. And these are commemorated who had already been mentioned among those seventy-five with whom Israel had entered into Egypt. For God willed not the first, nor the second, but the third tribe, that is, the Levitical, to be the priestly one.

VERS 20.-- *And Amram took to wife Jochabed,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] Amram is interpreted *lofty father,* who signifies Christ; Jochabed, *the grace of God,* [0201D] who signifies the Church: from Christ and the Church is born Moses, that is, the spiritual law, and Aaron, namely the true priesthood.

*And he took,* etc. She was of the tribe of Juda, sister of Naason, who was prince of the tribe of Juda in the desert: for it had not yet been commanded that each man, should take a wife from his own tribe. And these tribes, that is, the Levitical and the royal, were even then joined together, that it might be understood that Christ was to be king and priest from them.

VERS. 21-26.-- *The sons also of Isaar,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] It is asked why Core, whom the earth swallowed alive for his iniquity, is written in the genealogy of the just? But it signifies that heretics will be with catholics in the Church

[0202A]VERS. 27-30.-- *This is Moses.* [n: (STRAB.)] This Esdras adds of his own, who restored the whole library after the Babylonian captivity, all the books having been burnt by Nabuchodonosor; or Moses himself speaks of himself as of another.



## CHAPTER VII.

VERS. 1-6.-- *And the Lord said to Moses: Behold I have appointed thee lord,* etc. [n: (AUG. Quaest. 17 in Exod., tom III.)] It is to be noted that when he was sent to the people, it was not said to him: Behold I have given thee as God to the people, and thy brother shall be thy prophet; but, thy brother shall speak for thee to the people. It was said also *shall be thy mouth, and thou to him in those things which are unto God;* it was not said, *thou to him God.* But to Pharao Moses is said to be given as god, and by analogy Aaron is given [0202B] as prophet to Moses, but towards Pharao. Here it is intimated to us that the prophets of God speak those things which they hear from him; and that a prophet of God is nothing else than one who announces the words of God to men, who either cannot or do not deserve to hear God.

[n: (GREG. hom. 8 super Ezech.)] Sometimes God is spoken of by appellation, sometimes essentially. By appellation, as here: *I have appointed thee god of Pharao.* And elsewhere: *Bring him unto the gods.* And elsewhere: *God hath stood in the synagogue of gods,* etc. Essentially, as here: *I am the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob,* which Paul, wishing to distinguish, says: *Of whom Christ was born according to the flesh, who is God blessed for ever.* [var: Vulg. Rom 9:5 *qui est super omnia Deus benedictus in saecula*, "who is over all things, God blessed for ever"] For by appellation God is said to be among all things, but essentially above all things; that therefore he might show [0202C] Christ to be naturally God, he recorded him to be not only God, but God above all things. For any just man whatever is God, but among all things, because by appellation: but Christ is above all things, because he is naturally God.

[n: (AUG. Quaest. 18 in Exod.)] God says continually, etc. *as far as I will harden,* that is, I will show how hard it is.

VERS. 7, 8.-- *Now Moses was eighty years old,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] On account of circumcision, namely, which was commanded to be celebrated on the eighth day: for the number eighty has the same signification as the number eight which is less than it. *Now Moses was eighty years old.* When Moses and Aaron spoke to Pharao, Moses was eighty years old, but Aaron [0202D] eighty-three years old: because the law offered by preachers to the Gentiles taught spiritual circumcision, and they themselves were zealous to be perfectly cleansed, and so they preached the faith of the holy Trinity.

VERS. 9.-- *When Pharao shall say to you,* etc. [n: (AUG. Quaest. 19 et 20.)] Here there was no need of a voice, for the slenderness of which Aaron seemed to have been given to Moses, but the rod was to be cast down that it might become a dragon. Why then did Moses not do this himself, save because the mediation of Aaron between Moses and Pharao bears the figure of a great mystery. This also is to be noted, that it is written: *Aaron cast down his rod:* perhaps if it had said, he cast down the rod, there would be no question; but that it adds *his own,* whereas Moses had given it, is perhaps not said in vain. Or [0203A] was that rod common to them both, so that if it were called the rod of either of them, it would be truly called so?

[n: (STRAB.)] The rod of Moses signifies Christ, who took flesh from a royal stock. Whence that same rod, turned into a snake, devoured all the rods of the sorcerers, because the death of Christ consumed our death.

[n: (ORIG. hom. 4 in Exod.)] The Apostle says in I Corinthians XIV: *Be emulous of spiritual gifts, but rather that ye may prophesy.* Let us also try to take up the emulation of good things, but to await from the Lord the fulness of the gift. For for the one it is said in Psalm LXXX: *Open thy mouth and I will fill it;* [var: Vulg. Ps 80:11 *Dilata os tuum, et implebo illud*, "Open wide thy mouth, and I will fill it"] but for the other: *Prick the eye, and it brings forth a tear; prick the heart, and it brings forth understanding.* I think therefore that Moses coming to Egypt, and bearing the rod [0203B] with which he chastises and beats Egypt with ten plagues, is the law of God, by which it may reprove and amend the world with ten plagues, that is, with the ten commandments of the decalogue. The rod by which these things are done, and Egypt is subdued, and Pharao overcome, is the cross of Christ, through which the world and the prince of the world is overcome, that is, the devil, with the principalities and powers. The rod when cast down becomes a dragon or a serpent, and devours the serpents of the Egyptian magicians. But the serpent is wont to be set for wisdom or prudence; whence Matthew X: *Be ye prudent as serpents,* etc. And Genesis III: *The serpent was more prudent than all the animals,* [var: Vulg. Gen 3:1 *serpens erat callidior cunctis animantibus terrae*, "the serpent was more crafty than all the living things of the earth"] etc. The cross therefore of Christ, whose preaching seemed foolish, which Moses, that is, the law, contains [n: (secundum illud Joan. V: De me enim ille scripsit )], after it was cast down upon the earth, that is, came to the faith of men, [0203C] was turned into wisdom, and into so great a wisdom as should devour all the wisdom of the Egyptians, that is, of this world. For behold how God made foolish the wisdom of this world, after he manifested Christ crucified to be the power and wisdom of God: and now the whole world has been laid hold of by him who said in I Corinthians III: *I will take the wise in their own craftiness.*

VERS. 10.-- *And Aaron took,* etc. [n: (ISID. in Gen., tom. V.)] And he took the rod before Pharao, etc., *as far as: I will be thy death, O death,* etc.

*Which was turned into a snake.* The law of Moses was as it were tasteless like a rod, but turned into a snake, that is, into spiritual understanding, it was made drinkable and [0203D] delightful.

VERS. 11.-- *And Pharao called the wise men,* etc. It is to be known that either by diabolical feignings they deluded the eyes of the beholders, so that things remaining in their own nature seemed to be something else; or, according to Augustine, the demons, although evil, have not lost their own nature: therefore while by their incantation the sorcerers attempt to effect something, they run through the world, and suddenly bring the seeds of those things of which the matter is in hand. And so from these, God permitting, they produce new species of things.

*Similarly.* In the first plague he says, when the water is turned into blood; and in the second, when the frogs boil forth; and in the third, when the gnats assail; in the fourth, when [0204A] the dog-fly is brought forth; in the fifth, when the hand of the Lord is upon the cattle of the Egyptians, they are said to be like things.

VERS. 12.-- *Dragons.* They are so called Apo tou δέρκειν, that is, *to see,* because they see more sharply than other animals.

*But it devoured,* etc. [n: (AUG. Quaest. 21.)] If it had been said, The dragon of Aaron swallowed up their rods, it would be understood that the true dragon of Aaron had not swallowed up the phantom feignings, but the rods, etc., *as far as* but God alone is the true creator, who has implanted in things their very causes and seminal reasons.

VERS. 13, 14.-- *And it was hardened,* etc. God hardened the heart of Pharao, because he so hardened the devil after his sin that he is never softened by the compunction of repentance, as is said in Job XLI: *His heart shall be hardened as a stone.*

[0204B]VERS. 15, 16.-- *Go to him in the morning, behold he will go forth,* etc. [n: (ORIG. hom. 4.)] First indeed Moses does not enter in to Pharao, but meets him as he goes out to the waters; but afterwards he enters in; after these things he neither enters in, but being summoned he approaches: because whether our contest with Pharao is in the word of God and in the assertion of religion, or whether we strive to snatch from him the souls he besets, and there is in us a wrestling in disputation, we ought not at the first onset to go in to the uttermost places of the questions, but we must meet the adversary, and at his own waters, that is, the authorities of the philosophers of the Gentiles, that we may first convict them and show that they have erred; then we must go in to the inner parts of the contest. For the Lord says: *Unless a man first bind the strong one, he cannot [0204C] enter into his house and plunder his vessels [n: (Marc. XII)]*. First therefore the strong one must be bound and constrained with the bonds of questions, and so we must enter in to plunder the vessels, and to free the souls which he had possessed by deception and fraud. And when we shall have done this often, and shall have stood against him, girt namely as to our loins with truth, and standing in the Lord, and acting manfully, the ancient and crafty artificer will feign himself conquered and to give way, that he may make us more negligent for the contest. He will feign repentance also, he will entreat us to depart, but not far off: he wishes us to be near to him in some part. But unless we withdraw farther from him, and cross the sea, and say in thought, in speech, and in deed: As far as the east is distant from the west, so far hath he removed our iniquities from us, [0204D] we cannot be saved. Let us therefore pray for the mercy of God, that he may snatch us also out of the land of Egypt, out of the power of darkness, and may sink Pharao with his army like lead in the most violent water. But let us, being set free, sing a hymn to the Lord with joy and exultation. For he is gloriously honoured, etc.

[n: (ISID.)] The plagues were wrought bodily in Egypt, but in us they are wrought spiritually. For Egypt is the figure of the world.

VERS. 17-19.-- *The water of the river,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] The water turned into blood signifies worldly wisdom, in which everyone who has remained is slain, as a fish in blood. For worldly wisdom thinks it foolish [0205A] that God should die for men; whosoever therefore shall have clung to it will not be able to live.

[n: (ORIG.)] The water of the river is turned into blood, to which they had delivered the children of the Hebrews to be slain, that it might render to the authors of the crime a cup of blood: and that by drinking they might feel the gore of the polluted flood which they had stained with parricidal slaughter. Allegorically also the waters of Egypt are the erring and slippery teachings of the philosophers, who deceived those little in sense and understanding: but when the cross of Christ showed the light of truth, they are exacted for the punishments of the slaying of their own and for the guilt of blood. Whence: *All the blood which hath been shed upon the earth shall be required of this generation [n: (Matth. XXIII)]*.

VERS. 20, 21.-- *Which was turned into blood,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] The first precept is concerning the worship of one God: [0205B] Thou shalt not have other gods before me [var: Vulg. Ex 20:3 reads *Non habebis deos alienos coram me*; Migne prints *Non erunt tibi dii alii praeter me*, the form Origen expounds, and the gloss's enumeration turns on it] : the first plague, the water turned into blood. Compare the first precept with the first plague: water, from which all things are generated, signifies the one God, from whom are all things; blood, mortal flesh. The water therefore was turned into blood, because *they changed the glory of the incorruptible God into the likeness of the image of corruptible man,* and this change indeed takes place in the heart of the impious.

*Into blood,* etc. Because they who feel carnally about the causes of things are reproved by corrections of this sort, that from the quality of the punishments they may recognize their own error.

VERS. 22, 23.-- *And the heart of Pharao was hardened. [n: (AUG. Quaest. 22.)]* It seems that the heart of Pharao was hardened for this cause, that the enchanters of the Egyptians [0205C] did like things. But what follows teaches how great that hardening was, even when the enchanters failed.

VERS. 24, 25.-- *And they dug,* etc. Because the Gentile world, confounded by the meditation of secular philosophy, when it sees that there is nothing living nor wholesome in it, is zealous by searching to seek on every side a draught of wisdom, and finds it not, until it comes to him who says: He that thirsteth, let him come to me and drink [n: (Joan. VII)].



## CHAPTER VIII.

VERS. 2-6.-- *Behold, I will strike all thy borders with frogs,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] There are three kinds of frogs: one of the river and vocal; another of that which is called the calamite, of which it is reported that if it be thrown into the mouth of a barking [0205D] dog, the dog at once falls dumb; a third of that which is called the bramble-frog, because it lives among briars.

[n: (ORIG.)] Frogs signify the songs of the poets, which by an empty and inflated modulation, like the sounds and singing of frogs, have brought the fables of deception into the world; for that creature is good for nothing, save that it gives back the sound of its voice in shameless and importunate clamours.

[n: (RAB.)] The second precept: *Thou shalt not take the name of thy God in vain.* [var: Vulg.
