Ex 20:7 *Non assumes nomen Domini Dei tui in vanum*, "Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy God in vain"] The name of God is truth, for he himself says: *I am the truth.* He who speaks truth, speaks of God; for *he who* *speaks a lie, speaks of his own.* For to speak truth is to speak rationally; to speak vanity is to make a din. The second [0206A] precept, therefore, is concerning the love of truth, to which the love of iniquity is contrary. Truth speaks, vanity clamours. Contrary to this is the second plague, the abundance of frogs, whose loquacity signifies vanity. The lovers of truth do not take the name of God in vain, because they do not withdraw from truth into vanity. But those who contradict the truth, and, deceived by vanity, deceive others, are frogs, bringing weariness to the ears, not food to minds. Hear men speaking rationally, psalm XVIII: *There are no speeches nor languages whose voices are not heard.* Hear the frogs: *They have spoken vain things, every one to his neighbour,* etc.

VERS. 7-9.-- *And the magicians did so,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 23.)] It is asked whence they did it, if it had already [0206B] been done everywhere. But there is a like question: whence did they turn water into blood, if all the water of Egypt had already been turned into blood? But it is to be understood that the region where the children of Israel dwelt had not been struck with such plagues, and thence the enchanters were able to draw water which they might turn into blood, or to bring forth frogs for the display of magical power; although they were also able to do it after those things had been checked. But Scripture in narrating joined together what could have been done afterwards.

VERS. 10-14.-- *He* answered: *To-morrow.* [n: (THEOD.)] Pharaoh, wishing to know whether the prayers of Moses and Aaron had any power with God (for he supposed that they rested through the night), said: *To-morrow,* that he himself, having gone out to the river, might see whether the frogs had ceased. [0206C] Wherefore God, that Moses might be believed, not only made the frogs cease at that very time which Moses had appointed, but also commanded those which were upon the land to perish, lest they should think that the same had returned.

VERS. 15.-- *But Pharaoh seeing,* etc. [n: (AUG. Quaest. 24.)] Here it appears that Pharaoh was not hardened only because the enchanters did the like, etc., *as far as* he did not say gifts, or rewards, but thy judgments.

VERS. 16, 17.-- *Stretch forth thy rod,* etc. *Mystically.* Moses chastising Egypt with the rod signifies the law, which rebukes the world with the ten plagues of the commandments. The rod also, by which Egypt is corrected and Pharaoh overcome, is the cross of Christ, by which the world is conquered and the devil overcome, or triumphed over. The rod cast down becomes a serpent, which signifies wisdom, according to [0206D] that of Matthew X: *Be ye wise as serpents,* etc. The rod devoured the serpents of the magicians, and the cross of Christ, whose preaching seems foolishness, overcomes all the wisdom of the world. Whence: *For he wrote of me [n: (Joan. V)]*. The rod cast upon the earth was turned into a serpent, because when the mystery of the cross came to the faith of men, it made the wisdom of the world foolish.

*And let there be sciniphs,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] This creature is upheld by wings, flitting through the air, but so fine and minute that it escapes the sight of the eye, unless of one who sees keenly. Yet when it has settled upon the body, it bores with a most bitter sting, so that he who is not able to see it flying feels it stinging. This creature therefore is compared to the art of dialectic, [0207A] which bores souls with minute and subtle stings of words, and encompasses them with such craft that the man deceived neither sees nor understands whence he is deceived.

[n: (RAB.)] *Morally.* The third precept: *Remember to sanctify the day of the sabbath.* [var: Vulg. Ex 20:8 *Memento ut diem sabbati sanctifices*, "Remember that thou keep holy the sabbath day"] By which is intimated the leisure and tranquillity of mind which a good conscience makes. There is sanctification, because there is the Spirit of God. Whence: *Upon whom shall my Spirit rest, save upon the humble and the quiet?* etc. [n: (Isa. LXVI)]. [var: Vulg. Is 66:2 *ad quem autem respiciam, nisi ad pauperculum, et contritum spiritu, et trementem sermones meos*, "but to whom shall I have respect, but to him that is poor and little, and of a contrite spirit, and that trembleth at my words"] The restless, therefore, spring back from the Holy Spirit, provokers of quarrels, lovers of calumnies, nor do they admit the rest of the spiritual sabbath. Be thou therefore meek; let there be no tumult in thy heart, with phantasms flitting through corruption and stinging thee. Hear God saying in psalm XLV: *Be still and [0207B] see that I am God.* Thou through restlessness wilt not be still, and, blinded, thou demandest to see what thou canst not. For mark the contrary plague: the sciniphs were born in the land of Egypt out of the slime, flies namely most minute and most restless, flying without order, rushing into the eyes, and denying rest to men; while they are driven off, they return: so are the phantasms of the restless. Hold the precept, beware the plague.

VERS. 18.-- *And there were sciniphs.* Blood is the doctrine of the philosophers; frogs, the fictions of the poets; sciniphs, the sophisms of the dialecticians: all which have deceived the world.

VERS. 19.-- *This is the finger of God,* etc. [n: (ISID. ex Aug. ep. 119.)] At the third sign the magicians fail, saying: *It is the finger of God:* who had the type of heretics and their animosity. [0207C] Whence: *As Jamnes and Mambres resisted Moses, so these also resist the truth, men corrupted in mind, reprobate concerning the faith,* etc. [n: (II Tim. III)]. But these, who through corruption of mind were restless, failed at the third sign, confessing that the Holy Spirit, who was in Moses, was against them. For the Spirit is set in the third place, who is the finger of God: and he, as when appeased he affords rest to the meek and humble of heart, so when angered he harries the unmeek and the proud with restlessness, which the most grievous flies signified, under which the magicians failed, saying: *This is the finger of God,* that they might not understand whence they were deceived.

[n: (STRAB.)] It is to be noted that the magicians were overcome at the third sign, because all perfidy and worldly wisdom, or philosophy, is overcome by faith in the Trinity. For although [0207D] they discovered philosophy and disputed concerning the fabric of the whole world, they were not able to attain to the knowledge of the Trinity. By the finger the Holy Spirit is understood, not that he is less than the Father and the Son, but because in a finger there are very many divisions, and through the Holy Spirit the division of graces is given.

[n: (AUG., quaest. 25 in Exod., tom. III.)] What the magicians said to Pharaoh, etc., *as far as,* although they were not silent concerning the Son.

VERS. 20.-- *Thus saith the Lord,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] The fourth precept: *Honour thy father and thy mother.* Contrary to this is the fourth plague, namely the *cynomyia*, that is, the dog-fly: it is doglike not to acknowledge one's parents; whence their whelps are born blind.

[0208A]VERS. 21.-- *Every kind of flies.* [n: (ISID.)] *Mystically.* In the fourth place Egypt is struck with flies. Now the fly is an insolent and restless creature, in which are figured the insolent cares of carnal desires. But Egypt is struck with flies, because the hearts of those who love the world are smitten with the restlessnesses of desires. The Seventy set down *cynomyia*, that is, the dog-fly: by which doglike manners are signified, in which the pleasure of the human mind and the lust of the flesh are reproved. The forensic eloquence of men also can be signified by the dog-fly, men who like dogs tear one another.

[n: (PROCOP.)] God does not use great beasts in the storming of Egypt, but summons the ministry of the most contemptible little creatures and insects: namely, lest [0208B] the effect should be ascribed to the power of those beasts. To those abject and feeble little creatures he granted unconquerable strength, so far that Egypt despaired of being able to resist those little creatures, and cried out: *This is the finger of God:* For the whole world massed together will not bear the hand of God.

[n: (ORIG.)] The dog-fly is the sect of the heretics [ *Al.,* Cynicorum], who, upon the remaining shamelessnesses of their deception, preach pleasure and lust as the highest good. Since therefore the world was first deceived by each of these, deservedly the word and law of God, at its coming, reproves with rebukes of this kind, that from the quality of the punishments they may know the qualities of their own error.

VERS. 22, 23.-- *And I will make wonderful,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 26.)] This is to be understood to have been [0208C] done in the earlier signs also, that the land in which the people of God dwelt was vexed by no such plagues. But it was opportune that this should be set down openly there; whence now begin the signs which the magicians did not attempt to do the like of. For it is certain that the sciniphs had been everywhere in the kingdom of Pharaoh, but had not been in the land of Gessen or Gesen: there the magicians attempted to do the like, and were not able: until therefore they should fail, nothing was said concerning the separation of that land, but from the time when those things began to be done which they would not even dare to attempt.

VERS. 24, 25.-- *And there came a most grievous fly,* etc. [n: (AUG., ibid.)] The fly is put for the multitude of flies. For I know not how it is that, to the senses of men steeped in the custom of speaking, often more seems to be said by what is said in the singular than by what is said in the plural; [0208D] for example: There is a soldier there, than There are soldiers there; There is a fish there signifies more than There are fishes there.

VERS. 26-31.-- *Abominations.* The perverse Egyptians abominate good things, that is, the citizens of this world abominate sheep, that is, the simplicity of the good. For as the good abominate evils, so the evil abominate goods. Whence the good abominate the ungodly man, and the ungodly those who are in the right way.

[n: (AUG. Quaest. 28.)] Moses said that he wished to go into the desert where the Egyptians would not see their abominations. But this is to be understood as mystically signifying what we have said of the shepherds, who were abominable to the Egyptians; and therefore the Israelites [0209A] received a separate land in Egypt. For thus the sacrifices of the Israelites are abominations to the Egyptians, as the life of the just is to the wicked.

*[n: (STRAB.)] Historically.* The Egyptians abominate sheep and adore them because of Jupiter Ammon, who is painted with a ram's head. Likewise: the Egyptians worship the ox, which is consecrated to Apis, as the cow to Isis. For Apis was the greatest deity of the Egyptians, who once in the year at a fixed time appeared to their priests. And they, attending him with hymns and praises, prophesied like men frenzied.

[n: (GREG., lib. XVIII Moral., c. 28, tom. II.)] Egypt is struck with flies, etc., *as far as Be still and see, that I am God.*

VERS. 32.-- *And it was made heavy,* etc. *(AUG., quaest. [0209B] 29.)* When the locust had been taken away, it was said of Pharaoh: *Pharaoh made his own heart heavy at this time also, and would not let the people go.* Certainly it is not said now, The heart of Pharaoh was made heavy, but *Pharaoh made his own heart heavy.* So assuredly in all the plagues. From the will of man is the origin of vices. But the hearts of men are moved by causes, some in this way, others in that, even by causes not diverse, often in a diverse manner, according to their own qualities, which come from their wills.



## CHAPTER IX.

VERS. 1-6.-- *And the Lord said to Moses,* etc. [n: (PROCOP.)] Among the beasts horses are reckoned first, which are deceitful or false unto salvation. Then the unclean [0209C] beasts, which neither divide the hoof nor chew the cud, yet are yoked to the chariot. Next camels, which although on account of their chewing the cud they are reckoned as it were clean, yet, because their hoof is not cloven, are judged unclean. Nevertheless camels are declared to be partly of clean, partly of unclean condition. But since this plague was believed to be but little harmful, because of the vileness of the unclean animals, it passes on to oxen, and thence to the gentlest of animals, the sheep.

[n: (ORIG., hom. 4.)] In the fifth place Egypt is scourged with the slaughter of animals, or of cattle. In which the senselessness or folly of mortals is reproved, who like irrational cattle laid the worship and the name of God upon figures, not only of men but of beasts, stamped upon wood and stones: worshipping Ammon Jupiter in a ram, [0209D] Anubis in a dog, Apis in a bull, and the other monstrous gods which Egypt admires; so that in those to which they believed divine worship to belong, they might behold a pitiable punishment.

[n: (RAB. in Exod., tom. II.)] The fifth precept is: *Thou shalt not commit adultery,* etc., *as far as* mark the plague: if thou art a beast, at least fear to die.

VERS. 7.-- *And it was made heavy,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 30.)] If the cattle of the Israelites had died, there would seem a fitting cause why his heart should be weighed down to the contempt of God, as though his magicians had slain the cattle of the Israelites; but from that whence he ought to have been moved to fear or to believe, it was made heavy, and that heaviness has gone forward even to this point.

[0210A]VERS. 8.-- *Take handfuls,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 31.)] Another reading: *Take to you handfuls of ashes out of the furnace, and let Moses sprinkle it,* etc. The earlier signs were done by the rod, which not Moses but Aaron stretched over the water, or struck upon the ground. But now, two signs having been interposed, namely of the dog-fly and the deaths of the cattle, where neither Aaron nor Moses work anything with the hand, it is said that Moses should sprinkle ashes toward heaven; both are bidden to take, but he to sprinkle, not upon the earth but toward heaven: as though Aaron (who had been given to the people) ought to strike the earth, or to stretch out his hand upon the water or upon the earth; but Moses, who was in those things which pertain to the Lord, is bidden to sprinkle the ashes. What of those two earlier signs, where neither Moses nor Aaron work anything [0210B] with the hand? What does the diversity itself mean? For neither is it nothing.

[n: (RAB.)] The sixth precept: *Thou shalt not kill;* the sixth plague, pustules upon the body, blisters boiling and swarming, the burnings of wounds and the ashes of the furnace. For murderers burn with anger, with madness. If thou couldst see the souls of murderers, thou wouldst bewail them more than the putrefying bodies of the ulcerated.

VERS. 9-12.-- *[n: (ORIG., ibid.)] Wounds and blisters* with burning are brought forth at the sixth stroke. In the ulcers a crafty and purulent malice is reproved; in the blisters a swelling and inflated pride; in the burning, anger and the madness of fury.

VERS. 13-15.-- *And the Lord hardened the heart,* etc. [n: (ORIG., ibid.)] Note that in the sixth plague it is not said, [0210C] The heart of Pharaoh was hardened, but something more terrible is added; for it is written *that the Lord hardened the heart of Pharaoh,* etc. Again in the seventh, when hail and fire lay Egypt waste, the heart of Pharaoh is indeed hardened, but not by the Lord. But in the eighth, where the locust is brought forth, the Lord is said to harden the heart of Pharaoh. And in the ninth, when the darkness becomes palpable; and at last, when, the firstborn being destroyed, the Israelites had departed, it is thus said: *And the Lord hardened the heart of Pharaoh king of Egypt and of his servants, and he pursued them.* And also, when Moses is sent from the land of Madian into Egypt and is commanded to do wonders, it is added: *Thou shalt do them in the sight of Pharaoh: but I will harden the heart of Pharaoh,* etc. Hence first [0210D] it was said by the Lord: *I will harden the heart of Pharaoh,* etc. A second time also, the princes having been enumerated, it is said to Israel: *I will harden the heart of Pharaoh,* etc. Not in vain was that variety made, that now the Lord is said to have hardened the heart of Pharaoh, now that it was hardened of its own accord. I see Paul, as one understanding what difference there is between the heart of Pharaoh's having been hardened and the Lord's having hardened the heart of Pharaoh, saying in Romans II: *Or despisest thou the riches of his goodness, and of patience, and of longsuffering? Knowest thou not that the benignity of God leadeth thee to penance,* etc. Here without doubt he blames him who was hardened of his own accord. But elsewhere, as it were proposing a question upon this, he says: *Therefore on whom he will he hath mercy, and whom he will he hardeneth,* etc. He adds also to these: *O man, thou who art, who repliest against God [n: (Rom. IX)]*. [var: Vulg. Rom 9:20 *O homo, tu quis es, qui respondeas Deo*, "O man, who art thou that repliest against God", which the Vulgate sets as a question; Migne's *tu qui es* puts a relative where the interrogative stood, and he prints the same verse with *quis* eleven lines below at 0211A] By which, concerning [0211A] him who is said to have been hardened by the Lord, he answers not so much by the solution of the question as by apostolic authority, because of the incapacity of his hearers, as he says elsewhere [n: (II Cor. XII)]: *And he heard secret words, which it is not granted to man to utter.* Whence in what follows he deters the man who, not so much by the merit of his studies as by the lust of knowing, plunges himself too curiously [curiosus] into the more secret questions, saying in Romans IX: *O man, who art thou that repliest against God.*

[n: (RAB.)] The seventh precept: *Thou shalt not steal;* [var: Vulg. Ex 20:15 *Non furtum facies*, "Thou shalt not commit theft"; Migne's *Non furaberis* is the wording of Rom 13:9, not that of the Decalogue in Exodus] the seventh plague, hail upon the fruits. For no one has unjust gain without just loss. While thou stealest, thou gainest a garment, thou losest faith: gain visible, loss invisible: gain out of thy blindness, loss out of the Lord's cloud. For those who steal outwardly by evil desire [0211B] are hailed upon inwardly out of judgment, and the field of their heart is laid waste.

VERS. 16, 17.-- *And therefore have I set thee up,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 32.)] The Apostle set down these words, etc., *as far as* Pharaoh therefore was preserved for their profit.

VERS. 18.-- *Behold I will cause it to rain to-morrow,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] Thus far the punishments are tempered to the world through the figures of its own errors: after these come stripes from on high.

VERS. 19-21.-- *Send therefore now,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 35 in Exod., tom. III.)] God seems to forewarn of this not so much in indignation as in mercy, etc., *as far as* since God had threatened the death of the cattle, although Scripture has been silent of it.

[n: (PROCOP.)] Moreover it was brought about by divine providence [0211C] that the beasts should not all perish. For there was need of beasts to draw the chariots with which Pharaoh was afterwards to pursue the fugitives, that he might slaughter them in the wilderness. But divine providence governed the whole business otherwise.

VERS. 22.-- *Stretch forth thy hand,* etc. [n: (PROCOP.)] The reader is to be advised in this place that Moses is not bidden to do all the wonders with the rod, but only certain of them; certain also to foretell, as the gnats and the destruction of the beasts; certain to accomplish by the ministry of the hands, as when he takes soot from the furnace and stirs up the hail. But what is the cause of these things? Lest the common people should suppose that the rod had been made by magical superstition, and should assign to it those wondrous effects.

VERS. 23-29.-- *And Moses stretched forth the rod,* etc. [0211D] [n: (ORIG., ubi supra. )] This also is to be noted, that Aaron is said to inflict certain stripes upon Pharaoh or upon Egypt, Moses certain, and certain the Lord himself. For in the first plague, where he turns the waters into blood, Aaron lifts up the rod and strikes the water. In the second he strikes the waters and brings forth frogs. In the third he stretches out his hand and strikes the dust of the earth with the rod, and there are made sciniphs. In the fourth the Lord is said to have done it, that the dog-fly should come. In the fifth also, when the cattle are destroyed, he is said to have done this thing. But in the sixth Moses sprinkles the ashes, and there are made ulcers and blisters. In the seventh he lifts his hand toward heaven and there are made thunders and hail, namely, etc. In the eighth likewise he stretches his hands toward heaven, and the Lord brings on a wind, and brings in [0212A] the locusts. And in the ninth also he stretches his hand toward heaven, and there are made darkness and gloom. But in the tenth the end and completion of the whole work is administered by the Lord. For thus it is said: *And the Lord struck every firstborn in the land of Egypt.*

[n: (IDEM.)] There is another difference to be observed: for in the first plague, when the water is turned into blood, it is not yet said to Moses that he should go in to Pharaoh, but, *go forth to meet him,* etc. But in the second, the first having been ministered constantly and faithfully, it is said to him: *Go in to Pharaoh,* etc. But in the third the sciniphs are brought in, the magicians yield, confessing that *this is the finger of God.* But in the fourth Moses is bidden to watch in the morning, and to stand before Pharaoh as he goes forth to the waters, when the houses of the Egyptians are filled with the dog-fly. [0212B] In the fifth, when the cattle are destroyed, he is bidden to go in to Pharaoh. In the sixth Pharaoh is despised, nor is it said that Moses and Aaron went in to him, because ulcers and blisters with burning were made even upon the magicians, and they could not resist Moses. In the seventh he is bidden to watch very early, and to stand before Pharaoh, when he brings forth hail and fire and thunders. In the eighth he is commanded to go in to him when the locust is brought forth. In the ninth again Pharaoh is despised, and Moses [Moses] is commanded to stretch his hands toward heaven, that palpable darkness may be made: nor does he go in, but he is called to Pharaoh. Likewise in the tenth, when the firstborn are destroyed, and he is compelled to go out of Egypt with haste.

*And the Lord gave thunders,* etc. [n: (ID., homil., 4.)] [0212C] See the moderation of the divine correction: he does not scourge in silence, but gives voices, and sends doctrine from heaven, by which the chastised world may be able to acknowledge its own fault. He gives hail, by which the tender things of the vices, still being born, are laid waste. He gives fire also, knowing that there are thorns and thistles which had to be grazed down; of which the Lord says in Luke XII: *I came to send fire in the earth [n: (Luc. XII)]*. By this fire the incitements of pleasure and of lust are consumed.

[n: (IDEM.)] There are many other things to be observed, in which the divine wisdom is shown. For at the first Pharaoh is not bent, when the water is turned into blood. At the second he seems a little softened: *For he called Moses and Aaron, saying: Pray to the Lord for me,* etc. At the third [0212D] the magicians yield, saying: *This is the finger of God,* etc. At the fourth, scourged by the dog-fly, he says: *Go and sacrifice to the Lord your God,* etc. At the fifth, where the slaughters of the cattle are said to be, he does not yield, but hardened himself the more. Likewise at the sixth, over the plague of ulcers. But at the seventh, when he is laid waste with hail and with fires, *he called Moses and Aaron, saying: I have sinned this time also; the Lord is just, but I and my peoples are wicked,* etc. At the eighth, when he was pressed with locusts, he hastily called Moses and Aaron, saying: *I have sinned before the Lord your God, and against you. Take up my sin, and even now pray to the Lord your God.* At the ninth, when he is overspread with darkness, he calls Moses and Aaron, saying: *Go and serve the Lord your God.* At the tenth, when the firstborn are destroyed, he calls them by night and says: [0213A] *Arise, go out from my people, you and the children of Israel,* etc. And he adds: *And bless me also.* These things cannot be explained save by the same Spirit by which they were done, because, according to the Apostle [n: (I Cor. XIV)], the spirits of the prophets are subject to the prophets; therefore the sayings of the prophets are said to be subject, not to any whomsoever for the explaining of them, but to prophets.



## CHAPTER IX. (cont.)

VERS. 30-35.-- *But I know that,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 35 in Exod.)] Pharaoh had been terrified by the crash of heaven and by the hail. Whence he begged Moses to pray for him, confessing his own iniquity and that of his people. But Moses was seeking another fear. For it is easy to fear punishment; but this is not to fear God, with that fear of piety, namely, of which Jacob says: *Unless the God of my father Abraham, and the fear of Isaac had been present with [0213B] me, thou wouldst now have sent me away empty [n: (Gen. XXXI)]*.

## CHAPTER X.

VERS. 1-3-- . . . *For I have hardened his heart,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 36 in Exod.)] He did not say this as though God had need of anyone's malice, but it is thus to be understood, as if he should say: I have been patient over him and his servants, that I might not take them away, so that my signs might come upon them in order; for because the evil mind was becoming the more obstinate through the patience of God, for this, that I was patient over him, it is said, *I have made his heart heavy.* The plagues of the Egyptians are the instruction of the children of Israel, lest they be hardened in like manner, and suffer like things. When the pestilent man is punished, the little one shall be the wiser.

[n: (ORIG.)] The ten plagues with which Egypt is scourged through [0213C] the Israelites can be compared to the times of the Roman empire, because these things were done in a figure of us. The Christian and the Israelite people are of one God; the cause of both was one and made subject, the Synagogue to the Egyptians, the Church to the Romans. The Egyptians persecuted, and the Romans also persecuted. There Moses is contradicted ten times, here ten edicts against Christ. There diverse plagues of the Egyptians, here diverse calamities of the Romans. There first blood flows from the wells and rivers; here the first plague under Nero, that everywhere there was the blood of the dying, either corrupted by diseases in the City, or poured out by wars throughout the world. There the second plague, frogs in the inner chambers; famine almost destroyed the inhabitants: here under Domitian unbridled henchmen, at the command of a bloody [0213D] prince, and want, dispersed almost all the Roman citizens. There the third plague, sciniphs, most savage little flies, which in midsummer fly in swarms through squalid places, and are borne with a burning bite into the hair of men and the bristles of cattle: here the third plague stirred up the Jews under Trajan, who, whereas before, being scattered, they were quiet as though they were not, being moved by a sudden heat, went about ravaging. Besides, many cities in those same times fell down by an earthquake. There the fourth plague, dog-flies, nurslings of rottenness, mothers of worms; here under Marcus Antoninus a pestilence poured into many provinces gave over to rottenness and to worms Italy and the city of Rome, and the army of the Romans scattered through diverse winter quarters and dissolved by death. There the fifth plague, of cattle and of beasts of burden: here under Severus the persecutor, by civil [0214A] wars, the very bowels and supports of the commonwealth, that is, the commons of the provinces and the legions of soldiers, were broken in pieces. There the sixth plague, swelling blisters and running ulcers: here the plague which followed the persecution of Maximinus, who had commanded that especially the bishops and clerics, the popular crowd being passed over, the chief men of the Churches, should be tortured; and, swelling frequently with anger and perfidy, it was exalted not through the slaughter of the common people, but through the wounds of dying princes and men of power. There the seventh plague, hail gathered in the air, which was destruction to men and to beasts: here under Gallus and Volusianus, who succeeded Decius the persecutor, a pestilence was poured through all the spaces of the Roman empire, and gave nearly the whole human race to death. There the eighth correction, locusts stirred up on every side: here nations stirred up on every side [0214B] laid waste the Roman world with slaughters and burnings. There the ninth plague, darkness that might be handled, threatening more of peril than working it: here, while Aurelian the persecutor was decreeing, amid dreadful whirlwinds a terrible thunderbolt fell at his very feet, showing what so great an avenger would do, were he not patient; although within six months three emperors were slain, Aurelian, Tacitus and Florianus. There the tenth and last plague, the slaying of the firstborn: here the destruction of all the idols, which, being made first, they loved among the first. There the king proved the power of God, and felt it, and feared, and let the people of God go free: here also he did all these things, and even believed.

[n: (IDEM.)] There the people of God was not drawn back to servitude, nor here afterwards compelled to idolatry. There [0214C] the precious vessels of the Egyptians were handed over to the Hebrews: here the precious temples of the pagans passed over to the Church of the Christians.

[n: (IDEM.)] Upon the Egyptians, pursuing the Hebrews who had been let go after the ten plagues, the destruction of the sea rushed in: upon us also, as we journey, the persecution of the Gentiles remains, until we pass through the Red Sea, that is, the fire of judgment, with Christ as leader and as judge. But those who persecute Christians shall be drowned, with their king Antichrist, in the pool of eternal fire.

VERS. 4-12.-- *Behold I will bring in the locust to-morrow,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] The eighth precept: *Thou shalt not bear false witness:* [var: Vulg. Ex 20:16 *Non loqueris contra proximum tuum falsum testimonium*, "Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbour"] the eighth plague, the locust, a creature namely harmful with the tooth. For a false witness harms by biting and consumes by lying. Whence: *If you bite [0214D] and consume one another, take heed lest you be consumed one of another [n: (Gal. V)]*. [var: Vulg. Gal 5:15 *comeditis*, "devour"; the gloss's own verb two lines above is *consumit*, which follows Migne's reading and not the Vulgate's]

*[n: (ORIG.)] hom. 4 in Exod.)* I think that by this kind of plague the inconstancy of the human race, ever at variance and discordant with itself, is refuted. For the locust, although it has no king, according to the Scripture, leads an ordered army: but men, although they were made rational, were neither able to govern themselves in order, nor did they patiently bear the guidance of God who governs.

[n: (STRAB.)] By the locust, which has no king, is signified the rashness of those who, although punished with diverse penalties, always remain in their own hardness, as though they had no ruler.

[0215A] *The locust.* [n: (ISID.)] Locusts are taken for the mobility of levity, as souls wandering and leaping after the pleasure of the world.

VERS. 13-18.-- *And Moses stretched forth the rod.* [n: (RAB.)] Certain wonders are done through Moses, certain through Aaron, and certain through the Lord himself; that we may know that in some things we are to be cleansed through the sacrifices of the priests and the supplications of the pontiffs, whom the person of Aaron designates; in some to be amended through the knowledge of the law, which the office of Moses designates. But in the more difficult things we have need of the power of the Lord himself.

[n: (GREG., lib. XXXI, Moral., cap. 20)]. The plagues of Egypt shown forth from heaven, etc., *as far as* when with empty praises they unnerve the works of certain men who are now as it were strong.

[0215B]VERS. 19, 20.-- *There remained not so much as one,* etc. [n: (AUG.)] Scripture certainly recalled a benefit of God, in that he took away the locusts, and going on it said that the Lord had hardened the heart of Pharaoh, namely by his own benefit and patience, by which that man was becoming the more obstinate while he was being spared: as all evil hearts of men grow hard by ill using the patience of God.

VERS. 21.-- *Stretch forth thy hand,* etc. [n: (AUG.)] A third time it is said to Moses: *Stretch forth thy hand toward heaven,* that the plague of darkness also might be made. But never was it said to Aaron that he should stretch his hands toward heaven. What therefore was said to Moses: *Stretch forth thy hand over the land of Egypt, and let the locust come up upon the land,* I believe this to be signified, that he who can do the greater can do the less also; but not at once that he to whom the lesser things are granted [0215C] can do the greater.

[n: (ORIG.)] The ninth plague is darkness, that the blindness of their mind may be reproved, or that they may understand that the reasons of the divine dispensation and providence are most obscure. For God *hath put* *darkness his covert [n: (Psal. XVII)]*: and they, boldly and rashly searching it out, and asserting one thing from another, have been rolled down into the thick and palpable darkness of errors.

[n: (STRAB.)] By the darkness their folly [Judaeorum] is shown, who were afflicted and did not know to what end their punishments would come. Or also by the darkness is signified the secret of the divine judgment, by which he mercifully saves the just and justly condemns the ungodly.

[0215D]VERS. 24-29.-- *Only the sheep,* etc. [n: (AUG. in Locutionibus in Exod., l. 52.)] The Greek has, *besides sheep and oxen leave ye,* and it is an unusual manner of speech, unless after a pause there be inserted, *leave ye;* and there be understood, these things, so that the sense may be: Go, besides sheep and oxen, and leave these things. For such an ellipsis is wont to be made often in the manners of speech of the Scriptures.

[n: (RAB.)] The ninth precept: *Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's wife:* [var: Vulg. Ex 20:17 *Non concupisces domum proximi tui, nec desiderabis uxorem ejus*, "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's house, neither shalt thou desire his wife"; Migne's wording here is that of Dt 5:21, not that of the Decalogue in Exodus] the ninth plague, thick darkness. For there is a certain adultery even in desiring another man's wife, when his own does not suffice a man, and he attempts another's also. Truly the darkness is thick; for nothing so grieves the sufferer, nothing does the doer so refuse to suffer: he who does this is truly blinded with a horrible fury.



## CHAPTER XI.

[0216A]

VERS. 2.-- *Thou shalt tell therefore all the people,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 94 in Exod., tom. III.)] No one ought to take an example from this, etc., *as far as* that not even by this loss should those be smitten who had not been struck by the other plagues.

*That every man ask,* etc. [n: (GREG., lib. XXVIII Moral., cap. 17.)] Minds cleaving to the use of the carnal life, etc., *as far as* a greater fortitude of spirit is announced.

VERS. 3, 4.-- *And the Lord will give favour to his people in the sight of the Egyptians,* etc. Those, namely, who dwelt with the Hebrews in the land of Gessen, and for their sake had not been hurt by the nine plagues, and therefore loved them: by the tenth plague they were afflicted, when the Hebrews had departed from them.

[0216B]VERS. 5, 6.-- *And every firstborn shall die,* etc. [n: (ORIG. hom. 4)] At the last the destruction of the firstborn is brought in, in which there is perhaps something above our understanding, which seems to have been committed by the Egyptians against the Church of the firstborn, which is enrolled in the heavens. Whence also the destroying angel is sent to this ministry, who spares those only who had both door-posts marked with the blood of the lamb. Again the firstborn of the Egyptians are destroyed: whether we call them principalities and powers, and the rulers of the darkness of this world, whom Christ by his coming led forth, that is, led captive and made captive, when he triumphed upon the wood of the cross; or the authors and inventors of false religions, which the truth of Christ has extinguished together with their authors. Principalities, and powers, [0216C] and the rulers of these darknesses, are the firstborn of the Egyptians, which [ *Al.,* qui] in the last plague, namely when the end of the world shall have come, when he shall separate the sheep from the goats, the grain from the chaff, shall be condemned, the licence of wandering and of tempting being lost.

[n: (ORIG.)] *Morally.* The soul, while it lives in this world in errors, in ignorance of the truth, is placed in Egypt: if the law of God has begun to draw near, it turns the waters into blood, that is, it turns the fluid and slippery life of youth to the blood of the Old or of the New Testament. Then the law draws out of it the vain and empty loquacity against the providence of God, like to the croaking of frogs. It purges also its malignant thoughts, and shakes off the stings of craftiness that are like to the biting of the sciniphs. The bites of lusts also, [0216D] like to the darts of the dog-fly, it drives away; and the folly and the understanding like to that of cattle, through which *man, when he was in honour, did not understand, but was compared to senseless beasts,* etc. [n: (Psal. XLVIII.)] It reproves the ulcers of sins, the swelling of arrogance, and extinguishes the burning of fury. After these it applies the voices of the sons of thunder, that is, the evangelical and apostolic doctrines, and the chastisement of hail, that it may restrain luxury and pleasures; and at the same time the fire of penitence, that it may say: *Was not our heart burning within us [n: (Luc. XXIV)]*? Nor does it fail to draw off from it the examples of the locusts, by which all its restless and turbid motions may be grazed down, that it may learn to do all things according to order. But when it shall have been sufficiently chastised for its manners, when it shall have perceived the author [0217A] of the stripes, confessing that *it is the finger of God,* and shall have received a little of acknowledgment: then chiefly it sees the darkness of its own deeds and the gloom of its errors, and then it shall deserve that the firstborn of Egypt be destroyed in it. For every soul, when it shall have come to the fulness of age, and as it were the natural law shall have begun to defend its own rights within it, brings forth the first motions according to the desire of the flesh, which the inciting force has stirred up out of the tinder of concupiscence or of anger. Whence as a singular privilege the prophet says of Christ: *He shall eat butter and honey; before he shall do or bring forth evil things, he shall choose the good [n: (Isa. VII)]*; [var: Vulg. Is 7:15 *Butyrum et mel comedet, ut sciat reprobare malum, et eligere bonum*, "He shall eat butter and honey, that he may know to refuse the evil and to choose the good"] since before the child knows good or evil, he shall resist malice, that he may choose that which is good. But David says of himself: *The sins of my youth and my ignorances [0217B] remember not [n: (Psal. XXIV)]*. Because therefore these first motions of the soul, brought forth according to the flesh, rush into sin, deservedly in the moral sense the firstborn of the Egyptians are set down as the first things, because they are extinguished if the conversation of the remaining life directs a more amended course. So therefore in the soul, which the divine law, having taken it up out of its errors, chastises, the firstborn of the Egyptians are destroyed, unless, enduring after all these things in unbelief, it refuse to be joined to the people of Israel, so as to come out of the deep and escape unharmed, but remain in iniquity, and go down as lead into the mightiest water. For iniquity, according to Zacharias [n: (ZAC. V)], sits upon a talent of lead: and therefore he who remains in iniquity is sunk like lead into the deep [sic: *profondum*].

[0217C]VERS. 7.-- *Among all,* etc. [n: (AUG. in libro Locutionum. )] It is a most elegant manner of speech: for by the dog he signified the last, whether of men or of cattle: thus he set forth how great a quiet there should be among the Hebrews, while the Egyptians cried out in bitter bereavement.

VERS. 9, 10.-- *Pharaoh shall not hear you,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 40 in Exod.)] As though there had been need of the disobedience of Pharaoh that those signs might be multiplied: which were profitably done for the terrifying of the people of God, and for forming it to piety by that very discrimination. But this was of God, who used well the malice of that heart, not of Pharaoh, who ill abused the patience of God.



## CHAPTER XII.

[0217D]VERS. 2-4.-- *This month,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] This is April, which among the Hebrews is called Nisam, among the Macedonians Ξανθικός; and it is called *Aprilis* as it were *aperilis*, because then the earth is opened for the bringing forth of shoots, herbs and flowers: or *Aprilis* from Venus, as it were *Aphroditis*; for Venus in Greek is called *Aphrodite*. The Hebrews hold this month as the first, because the fifteenth of its Kalends was the first day of the world: the fourteenth, the second day; the thirteenth, the third; the twelfth, the fourth, on which the sun and the moon were made, and then was the first equinox; for the sun in the east and the moon in the west divided the sphere of the world equally.

[n: (STRAB.)] This is Nisan, according to the Hebrews, in which the world was made, whose full moon is always to be looked for after the vernal [0218A] equinox: in which the people of Israel is freed from the servitude of Egypt, and the human race is snatched by the blood of Christ from the power of the devil. On the tenth day of the month the lamb is taken, on the fourteenth it is sacrificed: because in the law the passion of Christ is figured, and, the grace of the Gospel flashing forth, it is fulfilled; for the number ten, because of the decalogue, signifies the law, but the number four the four gospels.

VERS. 5, 6.-- *And it shall be,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 42 in Exod., tom. III.)] That manner of speech may disturb a man, etc., *as far as* what was figured by that precept.

*According to which rite,* etc. *Allegorically.* Because Christ took flesh of the just and of sinners, that no one might despair of pardon. Or because Christ was a kid to the Jews, who said: *This man is a sinner (Joan. [0218B] IX)*, but to us a lamb without spot. Or because he appeared *in the likeness of sinful flesh* and *died for sin, whereas he had no sin [n: (I Petr. II)]*.

[n: (STRAB.)] On the tenth day of April he came to Jerusalem, and on the following day he was received by the crowds with branches of palms and with divine praises. Through the three remaining days he was watched by the Jews until the fourteenth day, that is, until the fifth day of the week, on which he supped with the apostles, and afterwards was betrayed.

VERS. 7.-- *And they shall take of the blood thereof,* etc. [n: (GREG., homil. 22 in Evang., tom. II.)] The blood is set on when it is drunk not only with the mouth of the body, but also with the mouth of the heart, etc., *as far as* because we bear the cross of his passion upon the forehead.

VERS. 8.-- *And they shall eat the flesh,* etc. [n: (GREG., ibid.)] [0218C] By night we eat the lamb, etc., *as far as* for he offers praise out of leaven who prepares for God a sacrifice out of rapine.

*With wild lettuces,* etc. [n: (GREG., ibid.)] Wild lettuces are exceeding bitter, etc., *as far as* that he may know that all things are dispensed through the power of the Holy Spirit.

VERS. 9-11.-- *The head with the feet,* etc. *[n: (GREG., ibid.)] The head of Christ is God [n: (I Cor. XI)]*. To devour therefore the head of the lamb, etc., *as far as* let no one be sluggish on the journey of this life, lest he lose his place in the fatherland.

[n: (GREG. NYSS., de Vita Mosis. )] By those wrappings of things he admonishes us that through these riddles we should be willing to consider and to understand that this our life is a certain passing over. For after we have entered upon it, we are always driven by a certain necessity toward the going out, [0218D] for which he wills us to be prepared with hands and feet and the remaining equipment for the journey. Lest therefore our feet be hurt by thorns on this steep journey of living, we ought to travel not with bare feet, but fortified with harder shoes. But the thorns, which, fixed in the feet, not only hold back and retard but also kill, are sins, from which the hardness of the shoes defends: namely a continent, spare and hard life, which weakens and breaks the points of the thorns, by which, from a small and slender beginning, sins so make their way onward to the things before them, that they slay utterly. To the leading of this life an ankle-length and flowing garment, that is, a voluptuous and vicious manner of living, which lies open most widely, is a hindrance: wherefore it is to be drawn in [0219A] with a certain girdle and constrained, lest it flow about hither and thither. But the girdle is modesty and a careful manner of living, which admonishes us to use things for necessity, not for pleasure. The staff with which we drive off wild beasts, and by which we are sometimes supported, is hope. But the food set before us is only from the fire, since faith, which is signified by that food, ought to be most burning, possessing nothing moist or slippery.

*Phase.* In Hebrew it is *Pesach* פסח , in Greek Πάσχα, that is, *a passing over;* because it was first celebrated by the children of Israel as they went out of Egypt: we also must pass over from things transitory to things eternal, from things earthly to things heavenly.

VERS. 12, 13.-- *In all the gods,* etc. The Hebrews report that all the idols of Egypt were broken in pieces on that night, [0219B] and the temples also, either by an earthquake or by an inundation of the river Nile. But allegorically, as we go out of Egypt, the idols of errors fall down, and all the cultivation of perverse doctrines is shaken.

VERS. 14-17.-- *And you shall have this day,* etc. *[n: (AUG., in Exod. quaest. 43, tom. III.)] And you shall keep this day in your generations as lawful, everlasting* or *eternal;* which in Greek is called αἰὼνιον, is not to be taken thus, etc., *as far as* at every time of the present life simplicity is to be kept, malice to be shunned.

VERS. 18-20.-- *In the first month, the fourteenth day,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] *Mystically.* The evening of the fourteenth day of the first month, on which the Pasch is celebrated and the immolation of the lamb is accomplished, signifies the putting off of the old man and the beginning of the new, which is begun in baptism, [0219C] that figures the death of Christ. Whence: *As many of us as are baptized in Christ Jesus, are baptized into his death,* etc. [n: (Gal. III)]. From the evening therefore of the fourteenth day of the first month until the evening of the twenty-first, a whole week namely, we are commanded [jubentur] to eat unleavened bread, because from the receiving of baptism until the end of life we ought to live simply and without guile. Whence Peter: *Laying aside all malice and all guile and dissimulation, as newborn babes,* etc. [n: (I Petr. II)].

VERS. 21.-- *The ancients.* Note that he communicates the commandments of God first not to boys, not to young men, but to the elders, because in the aged is wisdom, and in length of time prudence.

VERS. 22.-- *And a bunch of hyssop,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] [0219D] Hyssop is a lowly herb, growing upon rocks, purging the breast and the lung: and it signifies true humility, of which Christ afforded us the example in himself. We dip therefore the bunch of hyssop in the blood, when, mindful of the humility of Christ, we study to imitate him.

*Dip it in the blood,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 46 in Exod.)] It is asked what blood he calls that which is beside the door, since he would have understood the blood of that lamb by whose immolation the Pasch is made. Or does he command that it be done in that manner consequently, although he has been silent of this, that the same lamb be slain beside the door? Or, which is the more credible, he therefore said *of the blood which is beside the door,* because he who is to anoint is about to set [0220A] beside the door the very vessel in which he received the blood, that he may have it at hand while he dips.

VERS. 23-29.-- *And he will not suffer the destroyer,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] Whether the smiter was a good angel or an evil one, it is not necessary to inquire: for whoever he was, he obeyed the command of the Lord. Yet it is to be known that evil angels are wont to work such plagues, by the permission of God.

The tenth precept is: *Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's goods,* etc. [var: Vulg. Ex 20:17 *Non concupisces domum proximi tui … nec omnia quae illius sunt*, "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's house … nor any thing that is his"] Contrary to this is the tenth plague, the death of the firstborn. By another precept theft is forbidden, by which rapine also is condemned: for it is worse to snatch away by violence than by stealth. He therefore who wishes to take away openly or secretly has his own precept: but to covet a neighbour's goods, even if thou seek it by a just succession, [0220B] is not lawful. For those who wish to possess other men's goods as it were justly, seek also to be heirs to them when they die. When thou praisest a thing left behind, as though the man possessed it by right, God condemns him who desires it unjustly. For thou who seekest to be made heir to another dost not wish that he should have heirs, among whom nothing is dearer than the firstborn. By right therefore art thou punished in thine own firstborn, thou who, by coveting other men's goods, seekest out as it were a shadow of right, and losest the firstborn of the heart, which are the better things. The firstborn indeed is faith. For no one does good things in whom faith has not gone before. All good works are spiritual sons, but among these faith is the firstborn. Whosoever therefore thou art that secretly covetest another's goods, thou losest faith within. For thou servest not by charity but by fraud, as one loving him whom thou desirest to die, that thou mayest succeed him.

[0220C]VERS. 30-33.-- *For there was not a house,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 44 in Exod.)] Since only the firstborn were dying, how was there no house in which there was not one dead? Or had this also been divinely provided, that in all the houses there should be firstborn, in whom the Egyptians should be struck, even those who dwelt in the land of Gessen? For here they were of men, or of animals, not of the land; that is, men and animals that were firstborn were dying, by a hidden and angelic stroke: not anything had been done in the earth or in the heaven, as the frog, or the locust, or the darkness. For since the land of Gessen had been free from such plagues, the benefit reached the Egyptians who dwelt in it with the Hebrews: but by this plague all were struck.

VERS. 34.-- *The meal sprinkled,* etc. [n: (AUG.)] [0220D] *Mystically.* Without leaven the children of Israel set out from Egypt; that we, going out of this world, may cast away the leaven of malice and of wickedness, and feast in the unleavened bread of sincerity and of truth.

VERS. 35, 36.-- *And they asked,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 45.)] This had already been done before the deaths of the firstborn. But now it is repeated by way of recapitulation, for it was narrated when it was done. For how could it now come about that these things should be lent amid so great a mourning: unless perhaps one should say that by this plague also those Egyptians were not struck who dwelt with the Hebrews.

*Vessels of gold,* etc. These signify the doctrines which are learned out of the very custom of the nations by no unprofitable study; or precious souls out of the nations, as [0221A] vessels of gold and of silver; with garments, that is, with bodies: which join themselves to the people of God, that once for all they may be freed out of this world as out of Egypt.

VERS. 37-45.-- *And they set forward,* etc. [n: (ORIG. hom. 5 in Exod.)]. He who prepares to set out from Egypt, who desires to leave behind the dark deeds of the world and the darkness of errors, must first set out from Ramesse, which is interpreted *the commotion of the moth.* If thou wilt come to this, that God may be thy leader, and that the rock may follow which bestows spiritual meat and drink, go out from Ramesse, lest thou lay up treasure where the moth destroys and thieves dig through, etc.

*If thou wilt be perfect, go and sell all that thou hast, and give to the poor, and thou shalt have treasure in heaven, and come, follow me [n: (Matth. VI)]*: this is to set out from Ramesse, and to follow Christ.



## CHAPTER XII. (cont.)

[0221B] *From Ramesse to Socoth,* etc. [n: (ORIG. ibid.)] Let us see what is the place of encampment to which they come. From Ramesse they came into Socoth, which is interpreted *tabernacles.* When therefore, leaving Egypt, thou hast shaken off the moths of every corruption and hast cast away the incitements of the vices, thou shalt dwell in tabernacles, by which, namely, we would not be unclothed but clothed upon. And the dwelling in tabernacles shows that he who hastens to God is unencumbered. But not even in this must one stand still: the camp must be moved from Socoth also, and one must hasten into Othon, which is interpreted *signs to them,* because here, namely, thou shalt hear that God goes before them by day in a pillar of cloud, and by night in a pillar of fire; not this in Ramesse, nor in Socoth, which are the second camp. The third are those in which the divine signs are wrought: this [0221C] was the three days to which Moses and Aaron were hastening, and Pharao contradicted them, saying: *Go no further.* He was unwilling that the children of Israel should reach the place of the signs, that they might be able to enjoy the mysteries of the third day. Whence Osee VI: *God will raise us up after two days, and on the third day we shall rise again, and we shall live in his sight.* [var: Vulg. Os 6:3 *Vivificabit nos post duos dies; in die tertia suscitabit nos, et vivemus in conspectu ejus*, "He will quicken us after two days: on the third day he will raise us up"] The first day is for us the passion of Christ; the second, that on which he descended into hell; the third, that on which he rose again: therefore on the third day he went before them by day in a pillar of cloud; but by night, in a pillar of fire. *It is necessary therefore that they who are baptized in Christ should be baptized in his death, and be buried together with him, and on the third day rise again, whom, namely, he hath raised up with himself and hath made to sit in the heavenly places [n: (Rom. VI)]*.

*About six hundred thousand,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 47.)] It is asked [0221D] whether the Hebrews came to so great a number through those years in Egypt, etc., *as far as* that is, after the death of Joseph they were oppressed with hard servitude.

VERS. 46-51.-- *Nor of him,* etc. The bone of Christ was not broken on the cross, because, seeing him already dead, they did not break his legs: or the strength of the Godhead in the flesh is not corrupted by blasphemy, because he is not mere man, but true God.

## CHAPTER XIII.

VERS. 2-8.-- *Sanctify unto me every firstborn,* etc. Because I slew all the firstborn of the Egyptians in your deliverance.

VERS. 9-12.-- *And it shall be as a sign,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 48 in Exod.)]. What is it that he says when he gives commandment concerning the pasch, [0222A] etc., *as far as* otherwise grace would now be no longer grace.

VERS. 13-15.-- *But every firstborn of man,* etc. [n: (CYRILL.)] He commands the firstborn of men to be redeemed, that he may forbid manslaying: he who created all things that they might be incorruptible.

[n: (GREG., lib. XXVII Moral., cap. 1.)] The ass signifies uncleanness, the sheep innocence. Therefore to exchange the firstling of an ass for a sheep is to turn the beginnings of an unclean life into the simplicity of innocence, so that after the sinner has done those things which the Lord rejected as unclean, he may now by his doing bring forth those things which he may lay upon the sacrifice of God. For we are all born in sin, and conceived of the delight of the flesh, and have contracted original guilt to ourselves. Whence also by our own will we are entangled [0222B] in sins. But he too who after the malice of the flesh is turned to the innocence of the heart exchanges the firstling of an ass for a sheep, that is, the malice of his former life. For he who ceases to be such as he was before, and, having laid hold of cleanness of the flesh, keeps innocence, has exchanged the firstling of the ass for a sheep in the offering of God.

VERS. 16.-- *And as a thing hung up.* The Jews, understanding this ill, used to bind little patches or phylacteries about their heads, in which they had the ten words written before their eyes.

VERS. 17.-- *When therefore he had sent them out,* etc. [n: (GREG. lib. XXIV Moral. cap. 13)]. Often the converted, in the very entrance of their conversion, receive tranquillity of the flesh, gifts of prophecy, the teachings of doctrine, miracles of signs or the grace of healings. Afterward they are wearied by the hard [0222C] provings of temptations, from which they believed themselves to be free: because if in the beginning the bitterness of temptations were to beat upon them, they would easily return to the vices from which they have not far withdrawn. From those therefore who go out of Egypt wars near at hand are taken away: because to those who leave the world a certain tranquillity is first shown, lest, troubled in that very tenderness and beginning, they return to what they had left: first therefore they feel the sweetness of security, first they are nourished by the quiet of peace; but after the sweetness has been known they bear the contests of temptations the more tolerably, the more deeply they have known in God the things they love.

VERS. 18-20.-- *God led them not,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 49)]. Here it is shown that all things ought to be done which can rightly be done by counsel, for the avoiding of those things which [0222D] are adverse, even when God is most openly a helper [n: (Epist. ad Dam., tom. I)].

*And the children went up armed,* etc. [n: (HIERON.)] Aquila, who translates word for word, not, as they suppose, from contentiousness, but from diligence, where the Seventy set, *but in the fifth generation the children of Israel went up out of the land of Egypt,* rendered, *and the children of Israel went up armed out of the land of Egypt,* although, in place of what we have called *armed,* it may, according to the ambiguity of the Greek speech, be understood *equipped* or *furnished,* on account of the household stuff wherewith they despoiled the Egyptians. But where the Seventy set *in the fifth generation,* they who have diligently inspected the books of the Hebrews report that not "fifth" but "five" is written, so that the sense may be: but five went up, [0223A] the children of Israel, out of the land of Egypt; and because this seemed to be less understood, "generation" was added. But Aquila is said to have translated properly both in the rest and chiefly in this place, as all the benches of the synagogues agree.

*[n: (AUG., quaest. 53.)] But in the fifth progeny,* etc. Whether he means to reckon a progeny within a hundred years, etc., *as far as* Naason begot Salmon.

VERS. 21-22.-- *And the Lord went before them,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] When the mystery of the third day has been received by thee, God will begin to lead thee forth and to show thee the way of salvation.

[n: (GREG.)] In the fire is terror, in the cloud the gentle soothing of a vision. The day is the life of the just man; the night, of the sinner. Whence Ephes. V: *You were once darkness, but now [0223B] light in the Lord,* etc. [var: Vulg. Eph 5:8 *Eratis enim aliquando tenebrae: nunc autem lux in Domino*, "For you were heretofore darkness"] By day the pillar was shown by the cloud, and by night by the fire; because almighty God will appear both gentle to the just and terrible to the wicked. Coming to judgment, he soothes these by the mildness of his lenity, and terrifies those by the strictness of his justice. Otherwise: the pillar of cloud went before the people in the desert, and shone with the splendour of fire not by day, but by night: because Christ, affording leadership to his followers by the example of his own conversation, shone with no light to those who trusted in their own justice; but upon those who acknowledged the darkness of their sins he shed the fire of his love. Christ is the cloud and the pillar, because he is upright, and firm, and upholds our infirmity; because he shines by night and does not shine by day, *that they who see not may see; and they who see may become blind [n: (Joan. IX)]*. The sacrament of Christ also [0223C] is manifest as it were by day in the flesh as it were in a cloud: but in the judgment as it were in the terror of night; because then there will be great tribulation, and it will shine as fire to the just, and burn the unjust.



## CHAPTER XIV.

VERS. 2.-- *Phihahiroth, which is between Magdalum,* etc. [n: (ORIG., hom. 5 in Exod.)] Let us see what way is enjoined to be chosen. Turning back from Othon, he says, make your journey between Epauleum and Magdalum, which is over against Beelsephon, etc. Epauleum is interpreted *a winding ascent,* Magdalum *a tower,* Beelsephon *the ascent of a watchtower,* or *having a watchtower.* He believed perhaps that God would show a level and easy way, but it is an ascent, and a winding one. For the way by which one goes toward the virtues is not downhill, but the climb is narrow and difficult. [0223D] Whence Matthew VII: *For the way which leadeth to life is strait and narrow.* [var: Vulg. Mt 7:14 *Quam angusta porta, et arcta via est, quae ducit ad vitam*, "How narrow is the gate, and strait is the way that leadeth to life"] The journey therefore by which one goes forward is *a winding ascent,* and *the ascent of a watchtower* or *having a watchtower.* The ascent belongs to deeds, the watchtower to faith; it is shown therefore that both in deeds and in faith there is much difficulty, much labour: for many temptations meet us, many stumbling-blocks, when we would do the things that are God's. Then in faith thou wilt find many winding things, very many questions, the objections of heretics, the contradictions of unbelievers: this journey therefore must be made by those who follow the Lord. But there is also a tower on this road, of which it is said in Luke XIV: *Which of you, wishing to build a tower, doth not first sit down and reckon the charges, whether he have wherewith to finish it?* [var: Vulg. Lc 14:28 *Quis enim ex vobis volens turrim aedificare, non prius sedens computat sumptus, qui necessarii sunt, si habeat ad perficiendum*, "which are necessary, whether he have wherewith to finish it"] This then is [0224A] the tower, high and lofty by these virtues; and Pharao, seeing it, says: These men are astray. With Pharao, he who follows God is said to go astray, because the way of wisdom is winding, having many difficulties and turnings. If thou confess one Father invisible, and his one only-begotten Jesus Christ, and one Holy Spirit, this seems winding, difficult and incredible among unbelievers. If thou say that the God of majesty was crucified and is the Son of man who came down from heaven, these things seem winding and difficult; and he who hears (unless he hear it with faith) says that these men are astray. But be thou fixed in this faith, knowing that God has shown thee this way of faith, who says: Taking up the camp from Othon, place it between Epauleum and Magdalum.

[0224B] *He had been.* [n: (ORIG.)] Fleeing Egypt thou comest to the ascent of works and of faith, and to the building of the tower: thou comest to the sea, and the waves of temptations meet thee. Whence II Tim. III: *For they who will to live godly in Christ suffer persecution,* [var: Vulg. 2Tim 3:12 *Et omnes, qui pie volunt vivere in Christo Jesu, persecutionem patientur*, "And all who will to live godly in Christ Jesus shall suffer persecution"] because our life upon earth is a temptation. But if thou follow Moses, that is, the law of God, Egypt pursues thee. But see what shall come to pass: *There lifted himself up,* he says, *the angel of the Lord, who went before the camp of Israel, and he went away behind them. He lifted up also the pillar of cloud from before their face, and it stood behind them, and entered in between the camp of the Egyptians and Israel.* [var: Vulg. Ex 14:19-20 *Tollensque se angelus Dei, qui praecedebat castra Israel, abiit post eos: et cum eo pariter columna nubis, priora dimittens, post tergum stetit, inter castra Aegyptiorum et castra Israel*, "the angel of God … and with him together the pillar of cloud, leaving the front, stood behind"] This pillar of cloud is made a wall to the people of God: on the Egyptians it lays darkness; the pillar of fire is not carried over to the Egyptians, that they may see the light: but the pillar of cloud, that they may remain in darkness, because they loved darkness, not [0224C] the light. And thou therefore, if thou withdraw from the Egyptians, if thou flee from the power of the demons, see how great helps are prepared for thee, provided thou remain strong in faith: let not the horsemen and chariots of the Egyptians terrify thee, nor do thou cry out against the law of God, that is, against Moses, saying: *As though there were no sepulchres in Egypt, so hast thou led us forth.* [var: Vulg. Ex 14:11 *Forsitan non erant sepulchra in Aegypto, ideo tulisti nos ut moreremur in solitudine*, "Perhaps there were no graves in Egypt, therefore thou hast taken us away"]

VERS. 11-12.-- *Perhaps there were no sepulchres,* etc? [n: (ORIG., homil. 5 in Exod.)] These are the words of a mind failing under temptation: blessed is he who so receives temptations that no thought of doubt creeps upon him. To Peter also it is said in Matthew XIII: *O thou of little faith, why didst thou doubt?* These are the words of temptation and of frailty. Otherwise it is false, for it is better to die in the desert than to serve the Egyptians. For he who dies in [0224D] the desert, by this very thing, that he is separated from the Egyptians, that is, from the rulers of darkness and from the power of Satan, has had some profit, even if he could not attain to the whole. For it is better for one seeking the perfect life to die on the road than not even to set out. Whence the opinion of those seems to be false who, while they set forth the way of virtue as arduous and perilous, judge that it is not even to be begun. For it is better to die on the road than to be slain among the Egyptians and overwhelmed by false and bitter waves.

VERS. 13-14.-- *For the Egyptians whom you now,* etc. *[n: (AUG. quaest. 5 in Exod.)] For as you have seen the Egyptians today, you shall not again see them any more unto eternal time.* [var: Vulg. Ex 14:13 *Aegyptios enim, quos nunc videtis, nequaquam ultra videbitis usque in sempiternum*, "the Egyptians whom you now see, you shall see no more for ever"] But how are these words [sic: *ververba*] to be taken, [0225A] since the Israelites afterwards saw Egyptians? Or is it because those who then saw them did not see them further, since both they died who were pursuing, and these all, each on the day of his own death? For their descendants saw the descendants of those. Or is it to be understood, you shall not see them as today, that is, not as today pursuing and enemies, so that there may be no question even about the eternal time which he has here set down; because although both shall see one another at the time of the resurrection, yet not so as today.

VERS. 15-17.-- *Why criest thou to me?* etc. [n: (GREG.)] It is not our words that make voices before the ears of God, but our desires. For if we ask eternal life with the mouth and do not desire it with the heart, in crying out we are silent: but if we desire with the heart, though we are silent with the mouth, in keeping silence we cry. [0225B] Whence in the desert, when the people made a din with their voices, and Moses was silent from the noise of words, being silent he is heard, and it is said to him: *Why criest thou to me?* For in desire the secret cry does not reach human ears, but fills the divine.

[n: (ORIG., ut supra. )] No voice of Moses is heard, and the Lord says: *Why criest thou to me?* I would gladly know how the saints cry to God without a voice. The Apostle to the Galatians IV says: *For God will give the Spirit of his Son into our hearts, crying: Abba, Father.* [var: Vulg. Gal 4:6 *Quoniam autem estis filii, misit Deus Spiritum Filii sui in corda vestra, clamantem: Abba, Pater*, "And because you are sons, God hath sent the Spirit of his Son into your hearts"] And he adds: *the Spirit himself intercedeth for us with unspeakable groanings.* [var: Vulg. Rom 8:26 *sed ipse Spiritus postulat pro nobis gemitibus inenarrabilibus*, "the Spirit himself asketh for us"] And again in Romans VIII: *And he that searcheth the hearts knoweth what the spirit desireth.* So then, at the asking of the Holy Spirit, the cry of the saints is heard through silence. Moses is commanded to strike the sea with his rod, [0225C] that it may give way to the people of God as they enter, and that the waters which were feared, being made a wall on the right hand and on the left, may not only know no ruin, but may even afford a defence. The liquid takes on solidity, and the floor of the sea dries into dust: understand the goodness of the Creator; if thou obey his will, if thou follow his law, he compels the elements to serve thee against their nature. I have heard it handed down by our elders that for the several tribes there were made several divisions of the waters, and a way of its own for each. And this is shown from that which is said in Psalm CXXXV: *Who divided the Red Sea into divisions,* etc. And elsewhere, in Psalm LXVII: *There is Benjamin the lesser in a stupor. The princes of Juda, their leaders; the princes of Zabulon and the princes of Nephthali:* [var: Vulg. Ps 67:28 *Ibi Benjamin adolescentulus, in mentis excessu; principes Juda, duces eorum; principes Zabulon, principes Nephthali*, "There is Benjamin a youth, in ecstasy of mind"] an entrance seems to be enumerated proper to each tribe. The Apostle [0225D] says that a baptism was accomplished in Moses in the cloud and in the sea, so that thou who art baptized in Christ, in water and in the Spirit, mayest know that the Egyptians pursue thee and would call thee back to servitude, the rulers, namely, of the world, and the spiritual wickednesses which thou didst serve before. But thou goest down into the water, and comest out unharmed and risest up new, washed from the filth of thy sins, made ready to sing a new song. The Egyptians as they pursue are drowned in the deep, and if they seem to beseech Jesus not to drown them again in the deep.

[n: (ID.)] Otherwise, if thou flee Egypt, that is, the darkness of ignorance; if thou follow Moses, that is, the law of God, and the sea meet thee, that is, the waves of gainsayers, do thou also strike the struggling waves with the rod, that is, with the word [0226A] of the law and with the vigilance of the Scriptures; open thyself a way by disputation through thy adversaries, and the waves will give place to the victor; and while they who before gainsaid stand amazed and fixed with astonishment, thou wilt cut a straight path of faith by the lawful lines of disputation, and thou wilt so far advance in the word of doctrine that thy hearers, whom thou hast instructed in the words of the law, will now rise up against the Egyptians like waves of the sea, and will not only assail but even overcome and extinguish them. For he extinguishes Egypt who does not the works of darkness, who lives not carnally, but spiritually; who either drives out filthy thoughts from his heart, or does not receive them at all, according to that of Ephesians VI: Taking the shield of faith, that you may be able to extinguish all the fiery darts of the wicked one. [var: Vulg. Eph 6:16 *in omnibus sumentes scutum fidei, in quo possitis omnia tela nequissimi ignea extinguere*, "in all things taking the shield of faith, wherewith you may be able to extinguish all the fiery darts of the most wicked one"] In this way therefore we can even today see the Egyptians dead, and Pharao himself, [0226B] if we live with so great faith that God crush Satan under our feet speedily through Jesus Christ.

VERS. 19-20.-- *And the angel of God lifting himself up,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] The angel, who is interpreted *messenger,* signifies the teachers, who announce to us the precepts of life, and with the cloud, that is, the knowledge of the Scriptures, go before the camp of Israel, that is, the Church, because with the knowledge of the Scriptures they preside. When the Egyptians come upon them he goes back behind, because in the time of persecution they set themselves in front on behalf of their subjects. But the cloud was dark to the Egyptians, bright to the Hebrews.

VERS. 21-31.-- *All the night,* etc. Because through the whole time of this life, which the night signifies, the grace of the Holy Spirit makes the waters of baptism passable to the faithful, [0226C] that they may pass over from spiritual Egypt into the land of promise: but it chokes the army of the enemy.

[n: (ISID.)] The Red Sea signifies the baptism consecrated by the blood of Christ; the enemies following at the back die together with their king, because past sins are blotted out in baptism, and the devil is choked: the Egyptians press on as far as the sea, and sins as far as baptism. After the passage of the Red Sea the people sing, the enemies being drowned; and the faithful, coming up from the laver, their sins extinguished, chant a hymn, saying: *Let us sing to the Lord, for gloriously,* etc.



## CHAPTER XV.

VERS. 1.-- *Then sang Moses,* etc. [n: (ORIG., homil. 6 in Exod.)] We read many canticles in the Scriptures, [0226D] but this is the first of them all, which, when the Egyptians and Pharao were drowned, is sung after the victory. For it is the custom of the saints, when the adversary is overcome (since they know that the victory was wrought not by their own strength but by the grace of God), to offer to God a hymn of glorifying. Yet as they sing they take timbrels in their hands, as is related of Mary and Aaron. And thou therefore, if thou hast crossed the Red Sea, if thou hast seen the Egyptians drowned and Pharao cast headlong into the deep, canst thankfully sing a hymn to God, and say: *Let us sing to the Lord gloriously,* etc. And thou wilt say this more worthily if thou have a timbrel in thy hand, that is, if thou have crucified thy flesh with its vices and concupiscences.

*For gloriously,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] It is not enough, *he is glorified;* [0227A] for it is one thing to be glorified, another to be gloriously glorified. For when Christ took flesh for us, he was glorified, who came to seek what had perished, yet not gloriously, because he had no comeliness nor beauty; he was glorified when he came to the cross and endured death; whence John XVII: *Father, the hour is come, glorify thy Son,* etc. [var: Vulg. Jo 17:1 *Pater, venit hora: clarifica Filium tuum*, "Father, the hour is come, clarify thy Son"] The passion of the cross therefore was also a glory, wherein he was glorified, yet not gloriously, but humbly; whence Philip. II: *He humbled himself, becoming obedient unto death, even the death of the cross.* And Wisdom II: *Let us condemn him to a most shameful death.* In these things therefore he was glorified concerning himself humbly, not gloriously; whence Luke XXIV: *Because it behoved Christ to suffer, and so to enter into his glory.* [var: Vulg. Lc 24:26 *Nonne haec oportuit pati Christum, et ita intrare in gloriam suam?*, "Ought not Christ to have suffered these things, and so to enter into his glory?"] When he shall come in the glory of the Father, and of the angels, [0227B] to judge the earth, and shall slay the true Pharao, that is, the devil, with the spirit of his mouth, when he shall shine again in the majesty of the Father: then shall he be gloriously glorified, when all shall honour the Son as they honour the Father.

*He is magnified, the horse and the rider he hath cast into the sea,* etc. In the signs of Egypt. But he was gloriously magnified, because he drowned Pharao and his army. Or he was magnified in working miracles before the passion, in preaching, in dying on the cross: but he was gloriously magnified when he rose again a conqueror, the devil conquered, hell despoiled.

*The horse,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] The men who pursue us are horses; and all who are born in the flesh are figuratively horses, and have their riders. There are those whom [0227C] the Lord mounts, and they go about the whole earth; of whom it is said in Habacuc III: *Thy cavalry is salvation.* [var: Vulg. Hab 3:8 *Qui ascendes super equos tuos, et quadrigae tuae salvatio*, "and thy chariots are salvation"] Others the devil and his angels mount. Judas was a horse, and while he had the Lord for his rider he was of the cavalry of salvation: for, sent with the other apostles, he gave health to the sick and soundness to the languishing; but when he laid himself down under the devil, who entered into him after the morsel, being made the horse of Satan and led by his reins he rode against the Lord. All persecutors therefore are neighing horses, but they have evil angels as riders by whom they are driven, and therefore they are fierce. If thou see thy persecutor raging beyond measure, know that he is being pricked by his rider, and therefore he is savage and truculent.

VERS. 2.-- *This is my God,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] He therefore [0227D] shall be both my God and the God of my father. Our Father, who made us and begat us, is Christ, who said in John XVI: *I go to my Father,* etc. [var: Vulg.
