Jo 16:28 *vado ad Patrem*, without *meum*] If therefore I acknowledge that he is my God, I will glorify him; if also that he is my father's, I will exalt him: for it is a higher understanding, how Christ, that he may bind together and unite the truth of the one God, calls him his God whom by nature he knows as Father.

VERS. 3.-- *The Lord is as a man of war,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] Another reading: *The Lord shall destroy wars.* Amos IX: *The Lord is his name.* [var: Vulg. Am 9:6 *Dominus nomen ejus*, "The Lord is his name"; the wording printed is that of Jdt 16:3] He destroys not only visible battles, but those also which are ours against *principalities, and powers, and the rulers of these darknesses.--The Lord* indeed *is his name,* and there is no [0228A] creature of which he is not Lord. The chariots of Pharao and his army he cast into the sea: the chosen riders, the *ternistratores,* he drowned in the Red Sea. [var: Vulg. Ex 15:4 *currus Pharaonis et exercitum ejus projecit in mare: electi principes ejus submersi sunt in mari Rubro*, "his chosen princes are drowned in the Red Sea"] Pharao, as being more powerful in malice and holding the kingdom of wickedness, drives chariots: it is not enough for him to mount one horse, but he drives many at once, and compels them with a twisted lash. Those whom thou seest fouler in luxury, more savage in cruelty, more loathsome in avarice, know that they are of the chariots of Pharao, which he yokes to his car: on these he is borne and flies over the open fields of crimes, with loosened reins. There are other chosen riders, chosen, that is, unto malice.

[n: (ORIG.)] They are called *ternistratores* for this reason, that there is a threefold way of sinning for men. For they sin in deed, in word and in thought. Therefore they are called *ternistratores* [0228B] one by one, because they possess these three ways of sinning in us, and are ever on the watch and work by ambushes, that either the one may draw an evil word out of the wretched man, or the other wring out an iniquitous work, or the third snatch away a most wicked thought. Finally, where the seed of the word of God falls and perishes, a threefold place is described: by the wayside, among thorns, in stony ground. Contrariwise the good earth brings forth a threefold fruit, sixtyfold, hundredfold, thirtyfold [n: (Matth. XIII)]. For there is also a threefold way of doing well, since something good is done either by work, or by thought, or by word; whence the Apostle says in I Cor. III: *He who buildeth upon the foundation silver, gold, precious stones,* [var: Vulg. 1Cor 3:12 *Si quis autem superaedificat super fundamentum hoc, aurum, argentum, lapides pretiosos*, "gold, silver, precious stones"] indicating a threefold way of good things; and calling the way of evil things wood, hay, stubble. These *ternistratores* therefore are wicked angels, [0228C] who keep watch in these ways, that they may drive anyone into sin; whom the Lord will plunge into the Red Sea, and will deliver over to fiery waves in the day of judgment, and cover with a sea of punishments.

VERS. 5, 6.-- *They went down into the deep like a stone.* [n: (ORIG.)] Because, namely, such men were not stones out of which the children of Abraham might be raised up, but such as loved the deep and liquid element, that is, the bitter and flowing pleasure of things present. Whence it is said of them: *They were sunk like lead in most mighty water.* [var: Vulg. Ex 15:10 *submersi sunt quasi plumbum in aquis vehementibus*, "they were drowned like lead in the mighty waters"] Sinners are heavy, and iniquity is read to sit upon a talent of lead: hence the wicked are drowned in the deep like lead, but the saints walk upon the waters, because they are light, [0228D] and are not weighed down by the burden of sin. Christ walked upon the waters, who truly did no sin: Peter also walked, but doubted a little; for he had somewhat of lead, on account of which it is said to him in Matthew XIV: *O thou of little faith, why didst thou doubt?* All therefore are saved by fire, that if perchance anything of the kind of lead has been admitted, the fire may boil it away and dissolve it, so that all may be made the best gold, since the gold of that land is the best which the saints are to possess; and as the furnace tries gold, so tribulation tries just men. All therefore must come to the melting-pot. For God sits and melts, and purges the sons of Juda. If any man bring many good works and little of iniquity, that little is dissolved and purged as by fire, and the whole remains pure gold. [0229A] If any man bring more lead, more is burned away, that it may be the more boiled down; so that even if little, yet something of purified gold may remain; but he who comes wholly leaden is plunged *like lead in most mighty water.*

VERS. 7-10.-- *Thou hast sent forth thy wrath,* etc. [n: (AUG. quaest. 55 in Exod.)] Another reading: *Thou hast sent forth thy spirit and the sea covered them,* etc. Behold, now for the fifth time the Spirit of God is mentioned, etc., *as far as* Scripture only commanding, without the help of grace.

VERS. 11.-- *Who is like to thee,* etc. [n: (ORIG., ut supra. )] He does not compare God to the images of the nations, nor to the demons who falsely claim to themselves the name of gods, but he calls those gods who are so named by grace and by participation of God, according to that of Psalm LXXXI: [0229B] *I have said: You are gods.* And in the same place: *God hath stood in the assembly of gods.* But these, although they are capable of God, and seem to be endowed with this name by grace, yet none is like to him in power or in nature; and as to what is said, *we shall be like to him,* this likeness is referred not to nature but to grace, as a picture is like to him whose image is expressed in it as to grace, but far unlike as to substance. For that is the appearance of flesh, and the comeliness of a living body; this is a dye of colours, and wax laid upon boards that lack sense. None therefore among gods is like to the Lord, invisible, namely, incorporeal, unchangeable, without beginning and without end. None is the creator of all things save the Father, creating all things through the Son, and sanctifying all things in the Holy Spirit.

[0229C]VERS. 12, 13.-- *Thou hast stretched forth,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] That moves me which he says: *Thou hast stretched forth thy right hand and the earth swallowed them,* [var: Vulg. Ex 15:12 *Extendisti manum tuam, et devoravit eos terra*, "Thou hast stretched forth thy hand"] as though the cause of their being swallowed by the earth was that he stretched forth his right hand. If thou consider how the Lord, lifted up on the cross, all the day stretched out his hand to a people not believing but contradicting, and how death for iniquity crushed the unbelieving people that cried out: *Crucify, crucify him [n: (Joan. XIX)]*, thou wilt find how *he stretched forth his right hand, and the earth swallowed them.* Yet one must not altogether despair. For if he who has been swallowed come to himself, he can be vomited forth like Jonas. All of us the earth once swallowed and held in the inmost parts of hell: therefore Christ came down not only to the lands, but also to the lower parts of the earth: and there he found us swallowed up, and [0229D] sitting in the shadow of death; and leading us out thence, not only to the earth, lest we be swallowed again, but he prepares also the kingdom of heaven.

[n: (ORIG.)] The earth swallows up the wicked even today, who always think of earth, do earthly things, speak of earth, wrangle, desire earth, place their hope in it, look not up to heaven, think not of things to come, fear not the judgment of God, nor desire his promises; when thou seest such a one, say ye that the earth has swallowed him: and if thou see any man given over to lust and to the pleasures of the body, in whom the mind avails nothing but desire possesses the whole, say thou that the earth has swallowed him.



## CHAPTER XV. (cont.)

VERS. 14, 15.-- *They went up,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] The nations heard [0230A] and were angry, sorrows took hold of the inhabitants of Philisthiim, etc. [var: Vulg. Ex 15:14 *Ascenderunt populi, et irati sunt: dolores obtinuerunt habitatores Philisthiim*, "The peoples went up, and were angry: sorrows laid hold of the inhabitants of Philisthiim"] As to the history, it is certain that none of these nations was present at the wonders: how then were the Philisthiim, and the Moabites, and Edom, and the remaining nations which he enumerates, terrified with terror or angered? But, spiritually, thou wilt find that the Philisthiim, that is, *falling by the cup,* and Edom, namely, *the earthly one,* tremble: and their princes run to and fro and quake, bound fast with sorrows, when they see their kingdoms, which are in hell, penetrated by him who descended into the lower parts of the earth, that he might snatch away those who were possessed by death. Hence they fear and tremble, because they have felt the greatness of his arm; hence also the inhabitants of Chanaan pined away, that is, *the changeable* and *the moveable,* when they saw their kingdoms shaken, and [0230B] the strong one bound. Let fear then come upon them, and trembling: the demons tremble and fear the cross of Christ, in which they were triumphed over, and their principalities and power were stripped from them. Fear therefore and trembling shall fall upon them, when they shall see in us the sign of the cross faithfully fixed, and the greatness of the arm which the Lord spread out upon the cross, as Isaias LXV says: *All the day I have spread forth my hands to a people that believeth not,* etc. [var: Vulg. Is 65:2 *Expandi manus meas tota die ad populum incredulum*, "I have spread forth my hands all the day to an unbelieving people"; the wording printed is that of Rom 10:21] They will not fear thee otherwise, nor will the trembling of thee rush upon them, unless they see in thee the cross of Christ, so that thou mayest say: *But God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ [n: (Gal. VI)]*.

VERS. 15, 16.-- *Let them become unmoveable as a stone,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] To be made as a stone is not to be a stone by nature. For nothing would be made save what it was not. This [0230C] we have said on account of those who say that Pharao or the Egyptians were of an evil nature, and were not brought into this from the freedom of choice; and who accuse God the Creator as cruel, as one who turns men into stones: he did not say, Let them become as stones, and then keep silence, but he appointed a time and a measure of the domination, namely, *until thy people pass over.* The former people, which was before us, was made as a hard and unbelieving stone, not that the nature of a stone should remain, but until the people pass over which the Lord has purchased. Whence Rom. XI: *For blindness in part hath happened in Israel, until the fulness of the Gentiles should come in, and then Israel should be saved;* [var: Vulg. Rom 11:25-26 *quia caecitas ex parte contigit in Israel, donec plenitudo gentium intraret, et sic omnis Israel salvus fieret*, "and so all Israel should be saved"] which by unbelief was made as a stone, whence Matthew III: *For God is able of these stones to raise up children to Abraham.* But [0230D] since he is the creator of all things, how is he said to have purchased what there is no doubt is his own? For it is said in the canticle of Deuteronomy: *Is not this the same Lord thy God, who made and created thee, and purchased thee? [n: (Deut. XXXII.)]* [var: Vulg. Dt 32:6 *numquid non ipse est pater tuus, qui possedit te, et fecit, et creavit te?*, "Is not he thy father, that hath possessed thee, and made thee, and created thee?"] For each man seems to acquire that which was not his own. Whence the heretics say of the Saviour that those whom he purchased were not his: for at a price given he bought men whom the Creator had made, and they say it is certain that everyone buys what is not his own. For the Apostle says in I Cor. VII: *You are bought with a price.* But hear the Prophet: *For your sins you are sold, and for your iniquities have I put away your mother [n: (Isa. L)]*. [var: Vulg. Is 50:1 *Ecce in iniquitatibus vestris venditi estis, et in sceleribus vestris dimisi matrem vestram*, "Behold, for your iniquities you are sold, and for your wickednesses have I put away your mother"] We are therefore all creatures of God, but each is sold by his own sins, [0231A] and departs from the Creator. We are God's, therefore, according to that we were created by him: but made servants of the devil, according to that we are sold by our own sins. But Christ coming redeemed us who served the devil, to whom we had sold ourselves by sinning; and thus he seems both to have received back his own, whom he created, and to have purchased strangers, who by sinning had acquired a strange master. But he is rightly said to have redeemed us, who gave his own blood for our price. But what did the devil give, that he should buy us? Manslaying is the devil's money, for he is a murderer from the beginning: thou hast done a manslaying, thou hast received the devil's money. The same is to be said of adultery, sacrilege, theft, false witness and the like: for with this money he buys and makes his own servants all who have received anything, however much, of this [0231B] revenue of his.

VERS. 17.-- *Thou shalt bring them in and plant them,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] The Lord will not have us planted in Egypt, nor in low and lowly places, but in the mountain of his inheritance. Of this planting the Prophet says in Psalm LXXIX: *Thou hast brought a vineyard out of Egypt, thou hast cast out the nations and planted it: thou wast the guide of the way in its sight, and thou didst plant its roots, and it filled the earth. Its shadow covered the mountains, and its branches the cedars of God.* [var: Vulg. Ps 79:10 *plantasti radices ejus, et implevit terram*, "thou didst plant its roots, and it filled the earth"; Migne prints *replevit*] Dost thou mark how God plants, and where? He plants not in valleys, but on mountains, in high and lofty places. For those whom he leads out of Egypt, that is, out of the world unto faith, he will not have set again in lowly places, but that their conversation be lofty, and that they dwell on mountains and on the heights, not creep along [0231C] the ground. He will not have his vineyard bear its fruits any longer cast down in a low place, but that the shoots be led upward, be set on high, become layered vines: and layered not upon lowly trees, but upon lofty and most high cedars of God, namely the prophets and apostles: to whom if we be joined, and the vine which God brought out of Egypt, and our shoots be spread through their branches, and being entwined with them we be made layers, bound one to another by the bonds of charity, we shall bring forth very much fruit. *For every tree that maketh not good fruit shall be cut down and cast into the fire [n: (Matth. III)]*.

*Thy most firm dwelling place,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] In the prepared habitation which thou hast prepared, O Lord. See the goodness of the merciful Lord: he will not bring thee to labour, he will not that thou thyself make thee a dwelling place; [0231D] he will now bring thee into a habitation already prepared. Whence John IV: *Others have laboured, and you have entered into their labours.*

*Thy sanctuary,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] *Thy holy place, O Lord,* etc. [var: Vulg. Ex 15:17 *sanctuarium tuum, Domine*, "thy sanctuary, O Lord"] The tabernacle of God, or the temple, is called a holy place, because it sanctifies those who approach. In Hebrews IX this is said to be made not by the hand of men, but by the hand of God: for thy sake therefore God both plants and builds; he is made a husbandman and a builder, that nothing be wanting to thee. Whence the Apostle in I Cor. III: *You are God's husbandry, God's building.* What then is this holy place prepared not by the hand of men, but of God. Prov. IX: *Wisdom hath built herself a house,* that is, the flesh of Christ, not by the hand of man, that is, not [0232A] by human work: the temple of the flesh is built in the Virgin, but the stone cut without hands grew, and became a great mountain [n: (Dan. II)]: this is the holy place of the flesh assumed, and cut both without hands and without the work of men out of the mountain of human nature and the substance of flesh.

VERS. 18.-- *The Lord shall reign,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] *O Lord, who reignest unto the age and unto the age and yet further.* [var: Vulg. Ex 15:18 *Dominus regnabit in aeternum et ultra*, "The Lord shall reign for ever and beyond"] As often as it is said *unto the age,* a length of time indeed is signified, but that there is some end; and if it is said unto another age, something longer is signified, but still an end is set: and as often as ages of ages are named, perhaps some term is signified which, though unknown to us, is yet appointed by God. But that he has here added *and yet further,* leaves no sense of any term [0232B] or of an end: for whatsoever thou hast thought, in which thou supposest the end to stand, the prophetic word always says to thee, *and yet further;* as if it should say to thee, Thou thinkest the Lord will reign unto the age of the age, and yet further thou thinkest unto the ages of ages: and yet further, and whatsoever thou shalt have said of the spaces of his kingdom, the prophet always says to thee, *and yet further.*

VERS. 19.-- *But the children of Israel,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] And thou, if *thou art a son of Israel, thou canst walk on dry ground in the midst of the sea.* If thou shalt be in the midst of a depraved and perverse nation, going as the light of the sun in the midst of sinners, the moisture of sin shall not soak thee, and as thou passest through this world no wave of lust shall bespatter thee, no heat of covetousness shall lash thee. He who is Egypt, and follows Pharao, is drowned in the waves of vices. [0232C] But he who follows Christ, and walks as he walked, to him the water becomes a wall on the right hand and on the left. He himself goes forward by the middle way, over the dry ground he turns aside neither to the right hand nor to the left, until he come out to liberty, and sing a hymn of victory to the Lord, saying: *Let us sing to the Lord,* etc.

VERS. 20, 21.-- *A timbrel.* [n: (STRAB.)] According to Jerome it is a kind of trumpet having at the top a reed or a pipe; through which it renders a sounding voice. The choir, according to the same, is a simple skin with two hemlock pipes; through the one it is blown into, through the other it renders a sound: by both therefore is signified the narrow understanding of the old law. For how brief and how small it is, is shown from this, that it is carried by the hand of a woman. According to Cassiodorus a timbrel is a skin stretched [0232D] between two pieces of wood: by which the maceration of the flesh is signified. But a choir is a multitude singing together, and it is called *chorus* as it were the song of those of one age. Because therefore in a choir all the voices sound equally, by it charity is signified, whereby we are all one in Christ. Whosoever therefore renders praises to God in jubilation, let him do this in the choir, that is, in charity, and in the timbrel, that is, in the mortification of the flesh. Whence Psalm CL: *Praise ye him with timbrel and choir,* etc.

VERS. 22.-- *And Moses took Israel,* etc. The baptized are led through the world as through a desert after the passage of the Red Sea, not yet having the promised country, but hoping and awaiting it through patience, [0233A] while they are wearied by laborious and perilous temptation. But lest they turn back in heart into Egypt, neither there does Christ forsake them: for that pillar too does not withdraw.



## CHAPTER XV. (cont.)

VERS. 23, 24.-- *And they came,* etc. [n: (ORIG., hom. 6 in Exod.)] After the passage of the Red Sea, after the choirs and the timbrels, after the triumphal hymns, one comes to Mara, that is, to *water* that is *bitter,* which the people could not drink; one comes to the peril of thirst. But Moses cried to the Lord, and he showed him a wood, by which the water was made sweet, and there God set for him justices and judgment; where, namely, there was bitterness, where there was thirst, and, what is heavier, in an abundance of waters, there was no other place more worthy and more fitting. Besides, as to what he says, *God showed him a wood,* and he cast it into the water, and it was made sweet, as though God could not [0233B] make the water sweet without a wood, or as though Moses did not know the wood. But if the law be taken according to the letter, it is bitter, and is itself Mara. For what is so bitter as that a child should receive the wound of circumcision on the eighth day, and that tender infancy should suffer the hardness of the iron? For the cup of the law is bitter to the people of God, not the people that was baptized in Moses in the cloud and in the sea, but that which in spirit and in water cannot drink and taste it, nor bear the bitterness of the victims, nor the observance of the sabbath. But if God show a wood to be cast into this bitterness, the water of the law becomes sweet and drinkable. Prov. III: *Wisdom is a tree of life to all that embrace her.* [var: Vulg. Pr 3:18 *Lignum vitae est his qui apprehenderint eam*, "She is a tree of life to them that lay hold on her"] If therefore the wood of the wisdom of Christ shall have been cast into the law, showing how circumcision is to be understood, [0233C] and the sabbath, and the law of leprosy and the like, the bitterness of the law is turned into the sweetness of spiritual understanding, so that the people can drink which has left the idols and taken refuge with God. If it hear the law teaching about sacrifices, it at once shrinks back and cannot drink. Into this bitterness therefore, that is, into the letter of the law, God set justices and testimonies, as it were, namely, into a certain little vessel he shuts up [condidit] the treasures of his wisdom and knowledge. Whence: *We have this treasure in earthen vessels [n: (II Cor. III)]*. That this water therefore may be drunk, God shows a wood to be cast into it, that he who drinks may not die, may not feel the bitterness. If therefore any man drink without the wood of life, that is, without the mystery of the cross, without the faith of Christ, without spiritual understanding, he shall die of the bitterness. Whence: *The letter killeth,* [0233D] that is, the water of Mara, if it be drunk unchanged and not turned into sweetness.

VERS. 25-27.-- *Who showed him,* etc. [n: (AUG., Quaest. 57 in Exod.)] There was a kind of wood that had this power: or could God have done it with any wood whatever, who was doing such great wonders? Yet what is said seems to signify, *he showed him,* as though it were already such; unless perhaps it was a place where no wood at all was to be found, so that this very thing was of the divine help, that the Lord showed him a wood where there was none, and by the wood made the waters sweet, prefiguring the glory and the grace of the cross. But even in such a wood who is to be praised save the Creator and the Shower of it.

*There he appointed for him precepts. [n: (ORIG., hom. 7 in Exod.)]* [0234A] Why did he add: After *God set for him justices and judgments, there he tempted him, saying: If thou wilt hear the voice of the Lord thy God.* [var: Vulg. Ex 15:25 *ibi constituit ei praecepta, atque judicia, et ibi tentavit eum*, "there he appointed for him precepts and judgments, and there he tempted him"] He seems to expound why he gave justifications and judgments, namely that he might tempt them whether they would hear the voice of the Lord God, and keep his commandments. For as to the former people, what good or perfect thing could be commanded to them who murmured and gainsaid, who a little after, forgetting the wonders of God, turn to idols? Precepts therefore are given in which they may be tempted; whence: *I gave you precepts and justifications that are not good, in which they shall not live [n: (Ezech. XX)]*. [var: Vulg. Ez 20:25 *Ergo et ego dedi eis praecepta non bona, et judicia in quibus non vivent*, "Therefore I also gave them precepts that were not good, and judgments in which they shall not live"] For being tempted in the precepts they are not found faithful, and therefore it was found that what was unto life was unto death for them. For the same thing, if it be kept, begets life; if it be not kept, death. To those therefore who keep them not [0234B] the commandments are called not good, and such as they shall not live in. But with the wood of the cross mingled in, being turned into sweetness and spiritually understood, the commandments of life have also been kept. *All the languor which I laid upon Egypt I will not bring upon thee.* He seems to say that if a man keep the commandments he shall suffer no sickness, that is, neither fevers, nor other bodily pains. But Job, altogether most just and full of all piety, is filled from the head to the feet with the worst ulcer. But the just do not have those infirmities which the Egyptians have. Egypt is the world: to love the world therefore and the things that are in the world is the Egyptian languor; to observe months, seasons, to cling to the courses of the stars, to serve the flesh, to give labour to luxury and to be at leisure for delights, is the Egyptian languor.

[0234C] *And they came,* etc. [n: (ORIG., ibid.)] The people is not led into Elim before, where there were twelve fountains of waters, in which there was nothing of bitterness, but very great pleasantness from the thickness of the palm trees: but first it was led to the salt and bitter waters, which being sweetened by the wood shown by the Lord, one comes to the fountains. If we follow the history alone, it does not much edify us to know to what place they came first, to what second. But first the people is led to the letter of the law, from which, while it remains in its bitterness, it cannot withdraw. When therefore it has been sweetened by the wood of life, and spiritually understood, it passes from the Old Testament to the New, and to the twelve apostolic fountains, where are found seventy trees of palms. For not the twelve apostles alone [0234D] preached the faith of Christ, but seventy others also, through whom the world knew the palms of Christ's victory. It is not enough therefore for the people of God to drink the water of Mara, although sweetened by the wood of life; the Old Testament alone does not suffice for drinking, for one must come to the New, of which one drinks without scruple and without difficulty. The Jews also are even now at Mara, that is, at the bitter waters: for God has not yet shown them the wood by which they grow sweet, because *unless they shall have believed, they shall not understand [n: (Isa. VII)]*. [var: Vulg. Is 7:9 *Si non credideritis, non permanebitis*, "If you will not believe, you shall not continue"]



## CHAPTER XVI.

VERS. 2.-- *And all the congregation of Israel murmured,* etc. [n: (ORIG., homil. 7 in Exod.)] O ungrateful [0235A] people! it desires Egypt, it that saw the Egyptians destroyed; and the flesh of Egypt, it that sees the flesh of the Egyptians given both to the fishes and to the birds! It murmurs against Moses, nay rather against God; but the first time pardon is granted, and the second, and perhaps the third: but if they do not cease, but persist, hear what follows, it is related in the book of Numbers [n: (Num. XXI)], and the same Apostle rehearses it, saying [n: (I Cor. X)]: *Neither do you murmur, as some of them murmured, and perished by the serpents. These things were written on account of us, upon whom the ends of the world are come.* [var: Vulg. 1Cor 10:10 has *et perierunt ab exterminatore*, "and perished by the destroyer"; the serpents belong to v. 9, and v. 11 opens *scripta sunt autem ad correptionem nostram*] But if we do not cease to murmur and complain against God, we shall incur a like peril of offence, or a like example of it. For when we lay the blame upon the inclemency of the heavens, the unfruitfulness of the crops, the rarity of showers, and upon the prosperity of some and the unprosperity of others, [0235B] we murmur against God. But these things are pardoned at first, and are severely avenged upon those who persist, because serpents shall be sent upon them, that is, they shall be handed over to unclean and poisonous spirits, which by hidden bites feed upon the inner chambers of the heart.

[n: (ORIG.)] For the correction of those who read, the sin of the people is set down: that it murmured and was ungrateful for the divine benefits, when it had received the heavenly manna. In the second month, on the fifteenth day of the month, which is not written without reason. Recall the laws of the pasch which were spoken [n: (Num. IX)], and thou wilt find there that this is the time which is appointed for the keeping of a second pasch by those who have been unclean in soul, or taken up with business abroad. Those who had not been unclean in soul, or were not journeying far off, [0235C] kept the pasch on the fourteenth day of the first month; but those far off on pilgrimage, and the unclean, keep the second pasch at this time, in which the manna came down from heaven. On the day on which the first pasch is kept, the manna does not come down, but on that on which the second is kept: the first pasch is that of the Jewish people; the second is ours. For we were unclean in soul, we who *worshipped idols; and we were pilgrims, and far off, strangers and pilgrims from the testaments of God,* etc. [n: (Eph. II.)] But manna is given from heaven, not on the day on which the first pasch is kept, but on that on which the second is: *For the bread which came down from heaven [n: (Joan. VI)]* did not come to those who were celebrating the first solemnity, but to us, who have received the second. *For Christ our pasch is sacrificed, who as true man came down as bread from heaven [n: (I Cor. V.)]* Let us see [0235D] meanwhile what is shown to have been done on this day: On the fourteenth day of the second month the people murmured, etc.

VERS. 3.-- *We sat over the fleshpots,* etc. They had flocks of sheep and herds of oxen, of which they could eat; but they burned with desire for the fish and the fowl in which Egypt abounds.

VERS. 4.-- *Behold I will rain,* etc. [n: (ORIG., homil. 8 in Exod.)] I should wish first to have a discourse upon this scripture with the Jews, to whom the oracles of God were entrusted, as to what seems to them of that which it says: *For six days together you shall gather, but on the sixth day double.* It appears therefore that that day is named the sixth which is set before the sabbath; but the sabbath is the seventh day. [0236A] I ask therefore on what day the manna began to be given? and I wish to compare our Lord's day with the sabbath of the Jews. For from Scripture it appears that on the Lord's day the manna is first given, gathered for six days together; on the seventh, which is the sabbath, there is a ceasing. Since therefore on the Lord's day God rained manna, and not on the sabbath, let the Jews understand that even then our Lord's day was preferred to their sabbath; and it was signified that on that day the grace of God did not come down from heaven to them, and that the heavenly bread, which is the word of God, did not reach them; whence Osee III: *For many days the children of Israel shall sit without king, without prince, without victim, without sacrifice, without priest.* [var: Vulg. Os 3:4 names instead *sine altari, et sine ephod, et sine theraphim*, "without altar, and without ephod, and without theraphim," and has no victim and no priest] But on our Lord's day the Lord always rains manna, that is, the heavenly words which are joyful to us. Those unhappy men sigh even [0236B] after manna as their fathers did, and do not deserve to receive it; for they cannot eat what is small like coriander seed, and white like hoar frost. For they perceive nothing in the word of God that is small, subtle and spiritual, but all of it fat and gross. *For the heart of this people is grown gross.* The interpretation also of this name sounds thus. For manna is interpreted *What is this?* The very force of the name invites to learning, so that when thou hearest the law of God recited, thou mayest always inquire and say to the teachers, *What is this?* Thou therefore, if thou wilt eat manna, and receive the word of God, know that it is small and very subtle, like coriander seed: for it has somewhat of the herb whereby it can nourish and refresh the weak. *For he that is weak eateth herbs [n: (Rom. XIV)]*. For it has also somewhat of sharpness, and therefore is as hoar frost; [0236C] but of whiteness and sweetness very much. What is whiter, more splendid, than divine learning? what sweeter, more delightful, than the words of God, which are above honey and the honeycomb?

*Loaves.* The manna, namely: it is a manner of speech by which bread is put for food; and Scripture loves rather to say *loaves* in the plural than *bread* in the singular.

*That I may prove.* This proving is a testing, not a seducing to sin; nor are they proved for this reason, that God did not know: but he shows them to themselves, that they may become the more humble in asking help and in acknowledging the grace of God.

VERS. 5.-- *But on the sixth day,* etc. [n: (ORIG., hom. 7.)] On the sixth day let double be gathered into store, as much as may suffice also for the sabbath. The sixth day is this [0236D] life in which we now are, whence in six days God made this world; on this day therefore we ought to lay up as much as will suffice in the world to come. For if thou acquire here anything of good work, if thou lay up to thy credit anything of justice, of mercy and of piety, this will be food for thee in the world to come. For he who acquires here ten talents [n: (Luc. XIX)], there shall receive ten cities, and *what a man shall sow, that also shall he reap [n: (Gal. VI)]*. The rich of this world lay up those things which are corrupted in this world, nay together with this world: but goods laid up remain until the morrow. And it is written. *They who were unfaithful kept of the manna, and worms boiled forth out of it and it putrefied;* but what was laid up for the day of the sabbath remained whole. And thou [0237A] therefore, if for the sake of the present life and the love of the world thou lay up treasure, straightway worms boil forth, of which it is said in Isaias LXVI: *Their worm dieth not;* [var: Vulg. Is 66:24 has the future *non morietur*, "shall not die"; the present is the form of Mc 9:43-47] these are begotten by avarice and the blind greed of riches in those who have money, and who, seeing their brethren in need, shut up their bowels from them. Whence the Apostle [n: (I Tim. VI)] charges *the rich not to be highminded, nor to trust in the uncertainty of riches,* etc.; nor do worms come to be in us from any other source than from the manna, that is, the word of God. Whence John XV: *If I had not come and spoken to them,* etc. After the word of God has been received, therefore, if anyone sins, that very word is made a worm, which ever digs into his conscience and gnaws the secret places of his breast.

VERS. 6.--[n: (ORIG.)] *In the evening you shall know,* etc. *Toward evening [0237B] you shall know that I am the Lord; and in the morning you shall see the majesty of the Lord.* Let the Jews answer me how the Lord is known toward evening, and his majesty seen in the morning? Where was he acknowledged toward evening, and his majesty seen in the morning? But this is not understood save in the Gospel [n: (Matth. XXVIII)], in which on the evening of the sabbath, which *dawneth on the first day of the week, Mary Magdalen came and the other Mary to the sepulchre, and they found the stone rolled back from the monument, and an earthquake made,* and the monuments opened, and the centurion and the guards of the sepulchre saying: *Indeed this was the Son of God.* The Lord was acknowledged in this evening, because he himself was the Lord: he was acknowledged in the power of the resurrection, and in the morning his glory was seen. When the other women had come on the first day of the week very early in the morning, they found angels [0237C] sitting at the sepulchre with great brightness, and saying: *He is not here, but is risen from the dead. Come and see the place,* etc. [n: (Luc. XXIV.)] His majesty therefore was seen in the morning, because the resurrection also was announced by angels.

VERS. 7-11.-- *But what are we,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 59 in Exod., tom. III.)] They wished that they availed not so much as God, etc., *as far as* he showed the Holy Spirit to be God by adding, Acts V: *Thou hast not lied to men, but to God.*

VERS. 12-13.-- *In the evening you shall eat flesh* [n: (ORIG., ut supra. )] Morally we can understand that for each one of us there is a morning and a beginning of the day, the time in which we are first enlightened and kindled to the light of faith. In this time we cannot eat [0237D] the flesh of the word, that is, we are not yet capable of perfect doctrine; but after long exercises, after very much progress, when we are already near to evening, that is, to the faith of perfection, then we are made capable of more solid food. Let us hasten therefore to receive the manna, which yields in the mouth what savour each one wills. Whence Matthew IX: *Be it done to thee according to thy faith.* [var: Vulg. Mt 9:29 has the plural *secundum fidem vestram fiat vobis*, "according to your faith be it done unto you"] And thou therefore, if thou receive with all faith and devotion the word of God which is preached in the Church, be it done to thee whatsoever thou desirest. If thou art in tribulation, it consoles thee, saying: *A contrite and humbled heart God spurneth not [n: (Psal. L)]* [var: Vulg. Ps 50:19 has *non despicies*, "thou wilt not despise," in the second person addressed to God]; if thou rejoicest for the hope to come, it heaps up joys for thee, saying: *Be glad in the Lord, and rejoice, ye just [n: (Psal. XXXI)]*; if thou art wrathful, it says: *Cease from anger, and leave [0238A] indignation [n: (Psal. XXXVI)]* [var: Vulg. Ps 36:8 has *derelinque furorem*, "leave wrath"]; if thou art in griefs, it heals thee, saying: *The Lord healeth all thy diseases [n: (Matth. IV)]*; if thou art consumed by poverty, it says in consolation: *The Lord lifteth the needy from the earth, and raiseth up the poor out of the dunghill [n: (Psal. CXII)]* [var: Vulg. Ps 112:7 has the participles *suscitans a terra inopem, et de stercore erigens pauperem*, "raising up the needy from the earth, and lifting the poor out of the dunghill"]. Yet if anyone receives it unfaithfully, and does not eat it, but hides it, worms boil forth out of it. Nor be thou troubled if the word of God is brought to this, that it is thought to be made a worm; for he himself says: *I am a worm, and no man [n: (Psal. XXI)]*. For as he himself is the one who becomes to some a ruin, but to others a resurrection [n: (Luc. II)]; so also is he the one who in the manna is made to the faithful the sweetness of honey, to the unfaithful a worm. For he is the word of God which convicts the minds of the wicked and pierces the conscience with the darts of correction. For he is the one who is made fire to those to whom he opens the Scriptures [0238B], who say: *Was not our heart burning within us?* etc. [n: (Luc. XXIV.)] To others he is fire, in whom he burns up the thorns of the evil earth, that is, consumes evil thoughts in the heart; and therefore for sinners neither does the convicting worm ever die, nor is the burning fire quenched: but for the just and the faithful he remains sweet and pleasant. Whence it is said in psalm XXXIII: *Taste and see,* etc.



## CHAPTER XVI. (cont.)

[n: (ORIG., ubi supra. )] Another reading: *In the evening you shall eat flesh, and in the morning you shall be filled with loaves.* Let the Jews say what consequence there is, that in the evening they should eat flesh without loaves, or in the morning loaves without meat? what of the divine gift, what of the tempering of heavenly grace, is shown in this? Is this that wherein they can acknowledge God, who eat flesh in the evening without loaves, [0238C] and do they say that the majesty of God appears, if again loaves are eaten without flesh? let them keep such things to themselves, for those who, acquiescing in you, think that God is acknowledged in quails; but we, for whom at the end, toward the evening of the world, the Word was made flesh, say that God is acknowledged in that flesh only which he took from the Virgin: for this flesh of the Word of God no one ate either in the morning or at noon, but toward evening: for the coming of the Lord in the flesh was made toward evening, as John also says: *Children, it is the last hour [n: (I Joan. II)]* [var: Vulg. 1Jo 2:18 has *Filioli*, "Little children"] .-- *In the morning,* he says, *you shall be filled with loaves.* To us therefore the bread is the Word of God: *He is the living bread which came down from heaven, and giveth life to the world [n: (Joan. VI)]*. But as to its saying that this bread is given in the morning, whereas he was incarnate toward evening, it is so to be understood [0238D] that the Lord came toward the evening of the world and near its end; but by his coming, since he is the sun of justice, he restored a new morning to believers; a new light, namely, of knowledge, in which they are filled with loaves who have received his precepts. Nor wonder that the word is called both the flesh of God, and bread, and is called milk and herbs, and is named diversely according to the measure of believers and the capacity of those who take it. Or, after the morning made by the resurrection, he filled believers with loaves, because he granted the books of the law and the prophets, before unknown, for the instruction of the Church, and he himself is made bread in the Gospel, while the other books of the law and the prophets or of the histories may be called many loaves, with which believers from among the Gentiles are filled. Whence: *They shall go up into the mountain, and [0239A] shall drink wine, they shall be anointed with ointment.* Fittingly therefore do we receive flesh toward evening and are filled with loaves in the morning: because neither could we eat flesh in the morning, for the time was not yet; nor at noon, for scarcely do the angels eat flesh at noon, and to that order perhaps the noonday time is granted.

*And in the morning.* On the ninth day, when Christ, the sun of justice, by his coming restored to believers the things that were lost.

[n: (ISID.)] By the food of birds is figured the preaching divinely sent, which runs through sounding words, as winged things through the air, on which they are fed by faith who set out for the heavenly fatherland. Or the utterances of the old law, which fed the carnal people as with flesh through words divinely sent, as through flying things. Whence also it is given in the evening, because [0239B] all things granted to them carnally were to have an end. But in the manifest light of faith the manna is given, which is Christ, namely the *living bread which came down from heaven [n: (Joan. VI)]*, who is rained through the evangelical clouds upon the whole world: not now upon the murmuring and tempted Synagogue, but upon the believing and hoping Church.

VERS. 14.-- *In the likeness of hoar frost upon the earth.* The hoar frost signifies Christ: for the manna was first given on the Lord's day, and Christ rose again on the Lord's day.

*Pounded.* That is, most clean. For as wheat, or millet, or some other such thing is bruised in a little mortar with a pestle and stripped of its husks, so the body of Christ was stripped of mortality through the tribulation of the passion and the pestle of the cross. Or the small manna is the subtlety of heavenly [0239C] doctrine, which is pounded with the pestle that the literal sense may be stripped away.

VERS. 15.-- *Man hu. [n: (GREG., lib. XXVII Moral., cap. 7.)]* What is designated by the manna given from above to the children of Israel, save the wonder of the heavenly sweetness? For sweet is the food which is received from on high; it is called *man hu*, which is interpreted *What is this?* which we say when we behold what we wonder at not knowing. The soul therefore perceives the heavenly manna, when, lifted up by the voice of compunction, it is astonished at the new appearance of the eternal refreshment, so that, filled with the divine sweetness, it may rightly answer *What is this?* because while it is held suspended away from the lowest thought, it beholds those things which are from on high and wonders at them as at things unwonted. And when by this voice the deafness of our torpor is broken, the practice of the old life is [0239D] changed, so that the soul, breathed upon by the heavenly Spirit, both desires in the highest things what it had despised, and despises in the lowest things what it had desired.

VERS. 16, 17.-- *According to the number of your souls,* etc. [n: (AUG., locutio 78.)] Often Scripture calls men souls, the whole from a part: but not readily fleshes for men, but flesh; as in psalm LV: *I will not fear what flesh can do;* which it says more openly in the same psalm, *what man can do to me.* [var: Vulg. Ps 55:5 has *non timebo quid faciat mihi caro*, "what flesh can do to me," keeping the *mihi* which the second quotation retains] But it would not say, according to the number of your fleshes, as it said, *according to the number of your souls.*

VERS. 18, 19.-- *According to what they were able to eat,* etc. Because in the present life everyone ought to learn as much as [0240A] he can, lest he leave anything unexamined, lest he begin to learn when he cannot.

VERS. 20.-- *But some of them left of it until the morning,* etc. The beginning of the other life, in which it will not be permitted to give leisure to doctrine, but the manna will putrefy, and swarm with worms, that is, it will beget for them eternal punishment.

VERS. 21.-- *And when the sun grew hot, it melted.* Because then all understanding of Scripture will lie open, since *now we see through a glass in a dark manner, but then we shall see face to face [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*.

VERS. 22.-- *Double food.* The things necessary, namely, for soul and for body. For in the seventh, which is understood as the age of the dead, there will, namely, be no place for working.

VERS. 23-32.-- *The rest of the sabbath,* etc. By hearing the word of God and doing it, or by living well and showing mercy [0240B] to others. What was laid up for the sabbath was not corrupted, because good things done for the rest to come remain whole in the life to come. Otherwise worms boiled forth and what was kept putrefied: for those who lay up treasure for the sake of the present life and the love of the world, for them boils forth the worm that shall not die.

VERS. 33, 34.-- *And lay it up before,* etc. [n: (AUG. quaest. 61 in Exod.)] It can be asked where Aaron was to lay it up before the Lord, when there was neither any tabernacle, nor had the ark of the testament yet been set up. Or was it perhaps of the future, and did he therefore say *lay up,* that it might be understood that it could then be laid up before the Lord, when the ark was to be? Or rather was it said 'before the Lord' of that which is done by the very devotion of him who offers, in whatsoever place [0240C] it were placed? For where is God not? But what he adds: *And Aaron laid it up before the tabernacle to be kept,* rather asserts the former sense. For in this manner Scripture spoke by prolepsis of what was done afterwards, when the tabernacle of the testimony began to be.

VERS. 35, 36.-- *And the children of Israel ate manna forty years,* etc. *(AUG., ibid., quaest. 62.* Another reading: *And the children of Israel did eat manna forty years,* etc., *as far as* for God knew what they desired and from what kind of flesh he might satisfy them.

*They ate manna forty years.* The body of Christ, namely the Word of God, which we now perceive in a dark manner, but then face to face [n: (I Cor. 13)].

*Until they came into a habitable land,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 63 in Exod., tom. III.)] Another reading: *Until [0240D] they came into the region of Phoenice.* By repetition he sets forth what land he said was inhabited, etc., *as far as* for antiquity changed the names of many lands, places, rivers and cities for certain causes.



## CHAPTER XVII.

VERS. 1, 2.-- *All the multitude of the children of Israel therefore setting forward,* etc. [n: (ORIG., hom. 11 in Exod.)] All who wish to live piously in Christ suffer persecution, and are assailed by enemies: and he who walks the road of this life ought always to be ready and to stand in the camp. Whence all the synagogue of the children of Israel set forward from the desert of Sin according to their camps by the word of God. There is indeed one Synagogue of the Lord. but it is divided into four camps, which are described in the book of Numbers as placed [0241A] round about the tabernacle of the Lord. Do thou therefore, armed and serving in the camp of the Lord, not entangle thyself in secular business, and keep watch always in the camp of the Lord, that it may be said of thee that thou hast set forward by the word of the Lord from the desert of Sin, and comest into Raphidim. Now Sin is interpreted *temptation*; Raphidim, *soundness of judgment:* he who comes well out of temptation comes to soundness of judgment. For in the day of judgment he will be sound. Whence Apocalypse II: *To him that overcometh, I will give of the tree of life, which is in the paradise of my God.* He therefore comes to soundness of judgment who orders his words well in judgment.

*Sin.* Here Sin is taken specially for one station out of those five which were from the Red Sea as far as mount Sina, which Josephus describes by name. For in general that whole desert is called Sin, [0241B] as also Raphidim is.

*They encamped in Raphidim. [n: (HIERON. de Loc. Hebr., tom. III.)]* It is a place in the desert beside mount Horeb. And it is to be known that the whole wilderness as far as mount Sinai is called Sin, and that from the whole province the place of a single station also took its name, as Moab is the name both of a city and of a province. In this solitude there are five stations, Zuph, and the desert of Sin, Alus, Depheca, Raphidim. Whence it is well said that *all the multitude of the children of Israel set forward from the desert of Sin by their stations according to the word of the Lord, and they came into Raphidim.* Whence it is plain that many stations are denoted by the name of one region.

VERS. 3, 4.-- *The people therefore thirsted there,* etc. [n: (ORIG., ubi supra )]. Another reading: *The people,* he says, *thirsted for water, [0241C] and murmured against Moses.* It was enough to say that *he thirsted,* what need was there to add *water?* The addition is not superfluous. There are therefore diverse thirsts, and each man has his own. They who are good hunger and thirst after justice [n: (Matth. V)]. Others too say: *My soul hath thirsted after thee, O God [n: (Psal. XLI)]* [var: Vulg. Ps 41:3 has *sitivit anima mea ad Deum fortem vivum*, "my soul hath thirsted after the strong living God"]. But they who are sinners suffer not the thirst of water, not the hunger of bread, but of hearing the word of God. The people therefore thirsted for water, which ought to have thirsted for justice. But God, the instructor of the simple, the master of the unwise, corrects faults and amends errors, saying to Moses that he take the rod, and strike the rock, and bring forth waters. For he wills them now to drink of the rock and to make progress, and to come to the inner mysteries. For they murmured against Moses, and therefore the Lord commands that he show them the rock [0241D] whereof they may drink. For if there is anyone who, reading Moses, murmurs against him, and the law according to the letter displeases him, because in many things it seems not to hold together, Moses shows him the rock, which is Christ, that from it he may drink and repair his thirst. But this rock does not give waters unless it is struck, and when struck it brings forth fountains: for Christ, being struck and slain upon the cross, brought forth the fountains of the New Testament. Whence Zacharias VIII: *I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep of the flock shall be scattered.* [var: Vulg. Zach 13:7 has the imperative *Percute pastorem, et dispergentur oves*, "Strike the shepherd, and the sheep shall be scattered"; the words printed here are those of Mt 26:31] For unless he had been struck, and blood and water had gone forth from his side, we should all suffer thirst for the word of God. Whence: *And they drank of the spiritual rock that followed them: and the rock was Christ [n: (I Cor. X)]*.

[n: (ISID.)] The people, thirsting for water, murmured against [0242A] Moses: therefore God commands that he show them the rock whereof they may drink. But if anyone, reading Moses, murmurs against him, and the letter of the law displeases him, Moses shows him the rock, which is Christ, and leads him to it, that from it he may drink and repair his thirst.

VERS. 5.-- *Go before the people,* etc. Not Moses alone leads the people to the waters, but also the elders of the people. For not the law alone announces Christ, but also the prophets and the patriarchs and all the elders by birth.

*And the rod,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 64 in Exod.)] It is read that Aaron, not Moses, struck the river with the rod. For Moses divided the sea with that same rod, not the river. What then does this mean: *Take the rod wherewith thou didst strike the river?* Or did he perhaps call the sea a river? and is an example of this manner of speech to be sought? Or was what Aaron [0242B] did rather attributed to Moses, because through Moses God commanded the things which Aaron did, and in the one was the authority, in the other the ministry? Whence: *He shall be to thee for the people, but thou to him in those things which are unto God.*

VERS. 6, 7.-- *And thou shalt strike the rock.* Christ, who was struck upon the cross by the Jews: to whom the rod came near, that is, the wood of the passion, that water might remain for the faithful.

VERS. 8.-- *But Amalec came,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] Amalec was the son of Ismael, from whom are the Amalecites, who are also Ismaelites: these are the Saracens. Now it is interpreted *drinking* or *licking up blood,* and it signifies the devil, who ever assails the saints going out of Egypt and making toward the land of promise, and desires to drink the blood of souls.

[0242C] [n: (ORIG., hom. II in Exod.)] The war made with the Amalecites is described. Before the people ate bread from heaven, and drank water from the rock, it is not said to have fought; but it is said to it: *The Lord will fight for you, and you shall hold your peace.* There is therefore a time when the Lord fights for us, and does not permit us to be tempted above what we can bear, nor suffer those unequal in strength to come to a stout encounter. Job also, being already perfect, fulfilled the contest of his temptation; and thou therefore, when thou hast begun to eat manna, the heavenly bread of the word of God, and to drink water from the rock, and when thou hast drawn near to the inner things of spiritual doctrine, look for the fight, prepare thyself for war.

VERS. 9, 10.-- *And Moses said to Josue,* etc. [n: (ORIG., ibid.)] Another reading: *Moses said to Jesus: [0242D] Choose out men for thee,* etc. Up to this point mention was never made of the blessed name of Jesus. Here first Moses called Jesus, and said to him: *Choose men for thine own self.* Moses calls Jesus, the law invokes Christ, that he may choose for himself mighty men from the people: Moses could not choose, but Jesus alone, who says in John XV: *You have not chosen me, but I have chosen you [n: (Matth. XII)]*. He is the leader of the elect and the prince of the mighty. He contends with Amalec. For he himself enters into the house of the strong man, and binds the strong man, and plunders his vessels.

*I will stand on the top of the hill.* [n: (ORIG., ubi supra. )] Moses, he says, went up not to the top of the mountain, but to the top of the hill. For it was kept for him to go up then to the top of the mountain when Jesus was to go up, [0243A] and with him Moses and Elias, and there to be transfigured in glory. Now therefore, as not yet glorified by the transfiguration of Jesus, he does not go up to the top of the mountain, but to the top of the hill. And when Moses lifted up his hands, Israel prevailed. Moses lifts up his hands, he does not stretch them out; Jesus, who embraced the whole world, being lifted up upon the cross, says: *I have stretched out my hands to a people that believeth not [n: (Isa. LXV)]*. [var: Vulg. Is 65:2 has *expandi manus meas tota die ad populum incredulum*, "I have spread forth my hands all the day to an unbelieving people"; Rom 10:21, whose wording is nearest, has *expandi manus meas*] To lift up the hands, this is to raise one's acts to God, that a man may not have his acts cast down and lying on the ground, but pleasing to God and raised toward heaven. He therefore lifts up his hands who lays up treasure in heaven. For where the treasure is, there is the eye, there the hand. He lifts up his hands who says: *The lifting up of my hands an evening sacrifice [n: (Psal. CXL)]*. If therefore our acts be lifted up, Amalec is overcome: whence Isaias [0243B] XXXV: *Lift up the hands which hang down and the feeble knees.* [var: Vulg. Is 35:3 has *confortate manus dissolutas, et genua debilia roborate*, "strengthen ye the feeble hands, and confirm the weak knees"; the wording printed here is nearest Hbr 12:12] If the people keeps the law, Moses lifts up his hands, and the adversary is overcome. If it does not keep the law, Amalec grows strong. Since therefore for us the fight is against the princes and powers, the rulers of the darkness of this world, if thou wilt conquer, lift up thy hands, that is, thy acts; let thy conversation be not on earth, but in heaven. Do thou too lift up thy hands to heaven unto God, fulfil the commandment of the Apostle: *Pray without ceasing [n: (I Thess. V)]*; and then shall come to pass what is written, that as the calf licks up the green grass in the fields, so shall this people lick up the people that is upon the earth; wherein it is brought in that the people of God fought not so much with hand and with arms as with voice and tongue, and laid its enemies low by prayer. Lift up thy acts therefore, be instant in prayer: this is the Christian's [0243C] fight, which overcomes the enemy. And I think that by this figure two peoples are signified, of whom one, from the Gentiles, lifts up the hands of Moses, and raises on high the things which he wrote and the understanding of them, that so it may conquer; but the other, that is, the Jewish people, does not lift up the hands of Moses from the earth, nor understands anything high or lofty in him, and so is overcome by its adversaries.

*Having the rod of God,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 65.)] Now it is called the rod of God, which first was called Aaron's, afterwards is called Moses'. As the spirit of Elias is spoken of, which is the Spirit of God, of which Elias is said to be partaker; and the justice of God, which is ours. Whence: not knowing the justice of God, and wishing to establish their own [n: (Rom. X)], that is, [0243D] as though prepared by themselves for themselves.

VERS. 11.-- *And when Moses lifted up his hands,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] Because when the law is understood mystically, and the passion of Christ is spiritually weighed, the devil is overcome and put to flight. But when the hands are let down, that is, when the precepts of the law are less zealously practised, the enemy follows after as victor.

VERS. 12, 13.-- *Taking therefore a stone,* etc. [n: (GREG., homil. 33 in Evang.)] Moses sat upon a stone, etc., *as far as* those whom she justly condemns, he himself mercifully sets free.

VERS. 14.-- *For I will blot out,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] In this he signifies that almighty God, through the law (which Moses signifies, and which pronounces concerning Christ), blots out [0244A] the memory of the spiritual enemies. Whence psalm XIX: *Their memory hath perished with a noise.*

VERS. 15, 16.-- *And Moses built an altar,* etc. All who conquer the devil ought to build in their hearts altars of faith, on which they may offer spiritual victims.



## CHAPTER XVIII.

VERS. 1-4.-- *And when Jethro had heard,* etc. That Moses, when he was about to deliver the children of Israel, sent away his wife and sons, whom afterwards he received back in the desert, signifies that preachers, when they are sent to the work of preaching, ought to unburden themselves of temporal solicitude: but when afterwards they are taken up to the episcopate or to some other dignity, then they ought to bear solicitude for those subject to them [0244B] both in spiritual and in temporal things.

VERS. 5-11.-- *Jethro therefore came,* etc. [n: (ORIG., hom. 11 in Exod.)] Another reading: *After these things Moses came to the mount of God, and Jethro went out of the camp to meet him as he came toward him, nor did he lead him to the mount of God, but to his own tent.* For the priest of Madian could not go up into the mount of God, nor yet go down into Egypt, nor could the wife of Moses; but now she comes to him with her sons. For no one could go down into Egypt, and undergo the contests of the Egyptians, save an approved athlete, and one who, striving in the contest, refrains himself from all things. Moses, because a great and mighty athlete, went down into Egypt to the contests and the exercises of the virtues; Abraham too and Jacob, who [0244C] by his very name is an athlete, when he had gone down into Egypt, was made as the stars of heaven in multitude. To others it turns out contrariwise to have gone down into Egypt. I know that Jeroboam, fleeing from Solomon, went down into Egypt, and rent and corrupted the people of God; because going down into Egypt he received from king Susachim as wife Tethimene, the sister of his wife.

VERS. 12-15.-- *Jethro therefore offered,* etc. *[n: (AUG. in Locutionibus, locut. 85.)] And Jethro the kinsman of Moses took holocausts,* etc. He put *took* for *offered*: or is it perhaps a matter of the sense, not of the manner of speech, so that he is understood to have offered what he is understood to have taken; although Moses is not read to have sacrificed, nor Aaron, nor any one of the Hebrews who were led out of the land of Egypt: yet nevertheless it was said above that Moses set up an altar. But Jethro [0244D] we read to have been a priest of Madian, that is, of the nation of Madian. But it is a wonder if at his coming Moses began to sacrifice, and not rather he himself who was a priest.

*And they came,* etc. [n: (ORIG., ubi supra. )] Another reading: *There came,* he says, *the elders, Aaron, and all Israel, to eat bread with the kinsman of Moses in the sight of God.* Not all eat bread in the sight of God, but they who are elders, who are the older men, that is, who are perfect, proved in their merits, who whether they eat or drink, do it to the glory of God. All therefore that the saints do, they do in the sight of God: the sinner flees from the sight of God. Adam, after he had sinned, fled from the face of the Lord, and being questioned answered: [0245A] *I heard thy voice, O Lord, and I hid myself, because I was naked [n: (Gen. III)]*. [var: Vulg. Gn 3:10 has *vocem tuam audivi in paradiso: et timui, eo quod nudus essem, et abscondi me*, "I heard thy voice in paradise; and I was afraid, because I was naked, and I hid myself"] Cain went out from the face of the Lord, because he was unworthy of the sight of the Lord. Therefore they who receive a fuller knowledge of God, if they do evil, do it before God. Whence psalm I: *To thee only have I sinned,* etc. He who does evil before God straightway repents, and says, *I have sinned.* He who departs from the sight of God knows not how to be converted and to purge his sin by repenting. This therefore is the difference between doing evil before God, and departing from the sight of God.

[n: (ORIG.)] Not in vain did Jethro come to Moses, nor in vain did he eat bread with the elders of the people in the sight of God; he gives Moses a counsel approved and useful, that he should choose men, and appoint as princes of the people such as worship God, are able, and hate pride. For such [0245B] they ought to be as are not proud, and hate pride; that is, that not only should they themselves be without vice, but should hate the vices in evil men: I do not say the men, but the vices.

*Before God,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 66.)] It is asked: where, seeing that there was neither a tabernacle, nor the ark of the testament, which were instituted afterwards? nor can we take this as said of the future, as it was said of the manna, which was placed in a vessel, a golden one, namely. Therefore we ought to take that as done before God which was done in honour of the Lord.

VERS. 16-18.-- *I will show the precepts,* etc. [n: (ID., quaest. 67.)] It can be asked how Moses here said, etc., *as far as* he could not judge justly.

VERS. 19, 20.-- *But hear my words,* etc. *(ID., [0245C] quaest. 68.)* The first question is why God should have his own servant, etc., *as far as* whereby it becomes the fuller, the more freely it is extended into things heavenly and eternal.

*Be thou to the people,* etc. [n: (ID., quaest. 69.)] But as to what he adds, etc., *as far as* but whence this can be proved I know not.

VERS. 21-23.-- *But provide,* etc. Unto the keeping of humility he is instructed by a stranger, lest he who was learning so many secrets of God from God himself should be lifted up, and should think that he had no need of human counsel. It was also prefigured that the teaching of the Gentiles would surpass the knowledge and the pride of the Jews; and that the things which were lacking in the law, believers from among the Gentiles would suggest for their fulfilling, and would bring a spiritual understanding to the law of God. Moses also knew that the law would hear this people, and [0245D] would do whatsoever it should say, and that a lessening of the law would also come about at the suggestion of the Gospel.

*And appoint,* etc. [n: (ORIG., hom. 11.)] Thou shalt ordain them, he says, tribunes and centurions, and they shall point out [cj: *indicabunt*; read *judicabunt*, "shall judge"] the people at every hour, but nevertheless what shall be of greater weight they shall refer to thee. Let the princes of the people and the elders of the commons hear, who ought at every hour to judge the people, always and without ceasing to sit in judgment, to settle suits, and to reconcile those at variance, to call back the discordant into favour. Let each one learn from the holy Scriptures his own office. Moses, he says, that he may be in those things which are unto God, and may set forth the word of God to the peoples. But the other princes, whom he called tribunes, because they are set over a tribe, or centurions, or captains of fifty, [0246A] who are set over the lesser judgments, deciding those matters which pertain to individuals. And this figure I think is to be kept also in the world to come. Whence Matthew XIX: *When the Son of man shall sit upon the throne of his majesty, you also shall sit upon twelve seats,* etc. Thou seest therefore that not only does the Lord judge, to whom the Father has given all judgment [n: (Joan. V)], but he has appointed princes to judge of lesser causes; but the matter that shall be of greater weight, let them refer to him. Therefore the Lord says of one man, Matthew V: *That he shall be in danger of the council;* but of another, Matthew XII: *He shall be in danger of hell fire.* But also of every idle word they are said to render an account. And therefore he did not say that we shall render an account to God, as concerning perjury. *But thou shalt render thy oaths to the Lord.* Matthew XII: *The queen* also *of the south [0246B] shall rise in judgment with the men of this generation, and shall condemn it.* [var: Vulg. Mt 12:42 has *cum generatione ista*, "with this generation"; *cum viris generationis hujus*, with its men, is the wording of Lc 11:31, which closes *et condemnabit illos*, "shall condemn them"] Behold another kind of judgment, Matthew XIII: *He that hath ears to hear, let him hear:* for all these things are a shadow of things heavenly and an image of things to come.

[n: (IDEM.)] As it is written in Ecclesiastes I: *The eye shall not be satisfied with seeing, nor the ear with hearing,* [var: Vulg. Ecl 1:8 has *non saturatur oculus visu, nec auris auditu impletur*, "the eye is not filled with seeing, neither is the ear filled with hearing"; the gloss takes up *satiari* from the form printed here] neither can we be satisfied in considering the things which are written, wherewith it may build us up, in how many ways it may instruct us. For when I behold that Moses, a prophet full of God, to whom God spoke face to face [n: (Exod. XXXIII)], received counsel from Jethro his kinsman, a priest of Madian, I am struck dumb with exceeding wonder. For Moses heard the voice of his kinsman, and did whatsoever he said to him. He did not say, Since God speaks to me what I ought to do, how shall I receive counsel from a man, and from a Gentile man, [0246C] a stranger to the people of God? and he listens not to who it is that speaks, but to what is said. We therefore, if we find anything wisely said by the Gentiles, let us not, together with the author's name, despise the sayings also; nor, because we hold the law of God, does it become us to swell with pride and to despise the words of the prudent; but proving all things, let us hold fast that which is good. But they who today are set over peoples, I do not say if certain things have already been revealed to them by God, but if they have any merit in the knowledge of the law, disdain the counsel of an inferior, even of a priest, much less of a layman, much less of a Gentile: but Moses, who was meek above all men [n: (Num. XII)], received [sic: *accepti*] the counsel of an inferior, that he might give to princes a pattern of humility, and might mark out the image of a mystery to come. He knew that one day it would come to pass that Gentiles would bring forth good [0246D] counsel, bringing to Moses a good and spiritual understanding for the law, and that the law would hear them, doing all things that they should say. For the law cannot do as the Jews say, because the law is weakened in the flesh, that is, in the letter, and can do nothing according to the letter. For the law brought nothing to perfection [n: (Hebr. VII)]. But according to this counsel which we bring to the law, all things can be done spiritually, and sacrifices offered which now cannot be offered carnally; the law of leprosy can be kept spiritually. As therefore we understand, perceive and give counsel, the law does all things: but according to the letter not all things, but few.

VERS. 24.-- *Moses did all things which he had suggested,* [0247A] etc. A man familiar with God, most wise, not blushing to be taught by a lesser man and a Gentile, gave a pattern to teachers, that they should not blush to be instructed by any whomsoever.

VERS. 26, 27.-- *But whatsoever was of greater weight,* etc. [n: (AUG., loc. 88.)] Others: *But the weighty matter they referred upon Moses:* for so the Greek speech has it, which the Latins have translated, *to Moses.* By that manner of speech indeed the very care itself is figured, whereby it was said *upon Moses,* as though a burden laid upon him is signified. And *matter* moreover *weighty* was said for a weighty question.



## CHAPTER XIX.

VERS. 1, 2.-- *In the third month,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] Another reading: *And the going out of the children of Israel from the land [0247B] of Egypt was of the third month: on this day they came into the desert of Sina, and they set forward from Raphidim, and came into the desert of Sina, and Israel pitched there over against the mountain. And Moses went up into the mount of God, and the Lord called him from the mountain, saying: These things shalt thou say to the house of Jacob, and thou shalt announce to the children of Israel,* etc. And a little after: *Go down and charge the people, and thou shalt purify them today and tomorrow, and let them wash their garments, and let them be ready against the third day. For on the third day the Lord will come down upon mount Sina in the sight of all the people.* On this day the law is found to be given, which was written on tables of stone by the finger of God, as the things that follow teach. And this third day appears to be of the third month from the going out of the children of Israel from Egypt; from the day on which they kept the pasch, that is, immolated and ate the lamb, which was the fourteenth of the first month, as far as [0247C] that on which the law is given, fifty days are numbered, seventeen namely of the first month, the rest from the fourteenth itself. Then all thirty of the second month, which make forty-seven, and the third of the third month, which from the solemnity of the slain lamb is the fiftieth; and by this, in that shadow of things to come, according to the feast day of the immolated lamb; because on the fiftieth day the law was given, written by the finger of God. So in the very truth of the New Testament, from the festival of the immolated Lamb Jesus Christ fifty days are numbered, wherein the Holy Spirit was given from the highest places, who is the finger of God.

*On this day,* etc. On the forty-seventh day, namely, after they went out of Egypt, on the first day, namely, of June, they came to mount Sina, and there, when the two days were passed in which they were sanctified, on the third, [0247D] that is, the fiftieth, they received the law: and this is Pentecost.

[n: (RAB.)] In Sina there was one station, but the greatest of all. For there the law was received, the tabernacle set up. There Exodus and the greatest part of Leviticus and all the books of the law were almost wholly written.

VERS. 3.-- *But Moses,* etc. [n: (GREG., lib. VI Moral., cap. 27.)] Another reading: *But Moses went up into the mountain, and the Lord came down to him.* The mountain is the height of contemplation, into which we go up, that by gazing we may be lifted to those things which are beyond our weakness. But the Lord comes down, because, to saints who make much progress, he opens some small thing of himself.

VERS. 4, 5.-- *The wings of eagles.* He calls the teachings [0248A] of the two Testaments, which bear us from the lowest things to the heavenly.

VERS. 6.-- *You shall be to me a priestly kingdom,* etc. Members, namely, of the high priest who is to come into the kingdom of heaven.

VERS. 7-9.-- *He set forth all the words,* etc. [n: (GREG.)] It is to be noted that the law is not thrust upon the unwary or the ignorant: but when the rewards which are due to those who keep it have been set forth, and the past benefits which they have already experienced, and the wonders, when of their own accord they devote themselves to the keeping of the law, the law is given to those who ask for it: so that according to their own promise, as men bound by a vow, those who violate it may be judged, and may not complain of violence done to them.

VERS. 10.-- *Go to the people,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] *Go down [0248B] and testify to the people, and purify it today and tomorrow, and let them wash their garments, and let them be ready against the third day,* etc. He who wishes to come to the word of God, let him hear what the Lord commands: he ought to come sanctified and with washed garments. For if thou bring soiled garments, it will be said to thee, Matthew XXII: *Friend, how camest thou in hither not having on a wedding garment?* No one therefore can hear the word of God, unless first sanctified, that is, holy in body and in spirit and with garments washed: for he is about a little after to enter to the marriage supper, to eat of the flesh of the lamb, and to drink the cup of salvation. No one enters to this supper in soiled garments; whence Ecclesiastes IX: *At all times let thy garments be clean.* [var: Vulg. Ecl 9:8 has *Omni tempore sint vestimenta tua candida*, "at all times let thy garments be white"] For thy garments were washed once by the grace of baptism. II Corinthians VII: *Thou art purified in body, cleansed [0248C] from all defilement of the flesh and of the spirit.* [var: Vulg. 2Cor 7:1 has the first-person exhortation *mundemus nos ab omni inquinamento carnis et spiritus*, "let us cleanse ourselves from all defilement of the flesh and of the spirit"] Acts XI: *What therefore God hath cleansed, do not thou make unclean.* [var: Vulg. Act 11:9 has *tu ne commune dixeris*, "do not thou call common"; Act 10:15 *Quod Deus purificavit*] Hear the manner of sanctification: go not near, he says, to a woman today and tomorrow, that on the third day thou mayest hear the word of God; this is what the Apostle says, I Corinthians VII: *It is good for a man not to touch a woman,* saving nevertheless to the weak the remedy of marriage. I Corinthians VII: *But because the time is short, it remaineth that they who have wives be as if they had none.*

*Sanctify them,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] He who desires to come to the sight of God, let him sanctify himself by faith and by conversation, and wash his garments, that is, his works, today and tomorrow, that is, in the present and in the world to come, that he may come to the glory of the resurrection, which the third day intimates.

VERS. 11.-- *For on the third day,* etc. On this day was given [0248D] the law written on tables of stone by the finger of God, this is the third day of the third month from the going out of the children of Israel from Egypt. From the day therefore on which they kept the pasch, that is, the fourteenth of the first month, until the law was given, fifty days are numbered, seventeen namely of the first month, the rest from the fourteenth, and all thirty of the second, which are forty-seven, and the third of the third month, which from the slaying of the lamb is the fiftieth: as from the passion of Christ, until the Holy Spirit is given, fifty days are numbered. for the finger of God signifies the Holy Spirit.

[n: (ISID., Quaest. in Exod., cap. 28, tom. V.)] On the fiftieth day after the pasch the law of Moses was given etc., *as far as* or because he spoke the truth of the law to the unbelieving through a mist, [0249A] *that seeing they might not see, and hearing they might understand us [sic: *nos*] [n: (Isa. VI)]*. [var: Vulg. Lc 8:10 has *et audientes non intelligant*, "and hearing they may not understand"; the Clementine Is 6:9 reads *Audite audientes, et nolite intelligere*]
