VERS. 12.-- *Take heed,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] First he forbids the people to go up into the mountain, and threatens death: now he commands that at the sounding of the trumpet they go up into the mountain, restraining, namely, human boldness, and inviting to obedience: because at the voice of preaching we ought to raise ourselves to things above, and not of our own accord to presume upon the searching out of the mysteries, but to follow the calling of God.

VERS. 13.-- *Trumpet.* As it were *vocina*: from *vox*, the voice, or from *vocare*, to call, or from *bucca*, the cheek

VERS. 14-17.-- *And when they had washed their garments,* etc. It does not suffice, for the virtue of contemplation, to cleanse one's works with the tears of penance, unless there follow chastity [0249B] of mind and of body. Whence Matthew V: *Blessed are the clean of heart,* etc. And Wisdom I: *Into a malevolent soul wisdom shall not enter.*

VERS. 18, 19.-- *And all mount Sinai was smoking,* etc. [n: (GREG. lib. VI Moral., cap. 27.)] The Lord, being about to give the law, came down in fire and smoke, etc., *as far as* they overcome what they are.

[n: (ID., lib. II Moral., cap. 2.)] The sacred words in the opening of their narratives express the qualities and the bounds of their subjects, sometimes from the position of a place, sometimes from the position of the body, sometimes from the quality of the air, etc., *as far as* but they are recorded to have come in the evening when they were to punish Sodom.

VERS. 20-25.-- *And the Lord came down,* etc. [n: (ID., lib. V Moral., cap. 26.)] Moses went up, etc., *as far [0249C] as* we in some measure echo him by stammering.



## CHAPTER XX.

VERS. 2.-- *I am the Lord thy God. [n: (ORIG., hom. 8 in Exod.)]* Of him who despises the present world, that is to say Egypt, and, being translated by the word of God, is not found, because he hastens to the world to come, God says: *I am the Lord thy God,* etc. Not to those only who set out from Egypt, but to those who serve the Egyptians no longer, brought out of Egypt, out of the house of bondage [n: (Psal. LXXX)]. The businesses of the world, the acts of the flesh, are the house of bondage: just as to leave the things of the world, and to live according to God, is the house of liberty. Whence John VIII: *If you shall continue in my word, you shall know the truth, and [0249D] the truth shall make you free.* Egypt, therefore, is the house of bondage; Judea and Jerusalem, the house of liberality. As, therefore, earthly Egypt is called for the children of Israel a house of bondage in comparison with Judea and Jerusalem, which for them is a house of liberty: so, in comparison with the heavenly Jerusalem, which is the mother of liberty, the whole world and the things that are in it are called a house of bondage: and because from the paradise of liberty men came, through sin, to the bondage of the world, the first word of the decalogue is concerning liberty: *I am the Lord thy God who brought thee out of the land of Egypt,* etc.; this voice thou wilt not be able to hear in Egypt. Even if it be laid upon thee to keep the pasch, to gird thy loins, to take sandals [0250A] upon thy feet, to hold a rod in thy hand, and to eat unleavened bread with bitterness. But neither in the first station, nor in the second, nor in the third, nor when thou shalt have passed the Red Sea, even if thou come to the sea and the bitterness be turned for thee into sweetness, nor in Elim, where were twelve fountains of waters and seventy palm trees, even if thou pass Raphidim by and climb the remaining stages, art thou yet judged fit, but only when thou shalt have come to Mount Sinai. Scarcely, therefore, wilt thou at some time deserve to receive the precepts of liberty, and to hear: *I am the Lord thy God, who brought thee out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage.* This, however, is not the word of a commandment, but it shows who he is that commands. The first commandment, therefore, is: *There shall not be to thee other gods beside me.* [var: Vulg. Ex 20:3 *Non habebis deos alienos coram me*, "Thou shalt not have strange gods before me"; Migne's own lemma at VERS. 3 below prints the Vulgate form, and the wording used throughout this gloss is the Septuagint's]

[0250B] [n: (AUG. quaest. in Exod.)] It is asked: how are the ten precepts of the law to be divided? etc., *as far as* unless to that which was said: *There shall not be to thee other gods beside me?*

[n: (ISID.)] God gives to Moses the law of our innocence and of the knowledge of himself, which consists in ten words, written on tables of stone by the finger of God. There are three which pertain to the love and the knowledge of the Trinity, seven to the love of one's neighbour, by which human society is not injured. The first pertains to God the Father: *The Lord thy God is one God,* that, hearing this, thou mayest worship one God the Father, and mayest not pour out fornication upon many feigned gods. The second to the Son: *Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy God in vain;* that is, do not think that Christ the Son of God is a creature, because every creature is made subject to vanity, but equal to the Father, God of God, [0250C] light of light.

VERS. 3.-- *Thou shalt not have strange gods,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] The first commandment is: *There shall not be to thee other gods beside me;* and there follows: *Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness,* etc. [var: Vulg. Ex 20:4 *Non facies tibi sculptile, neque omnem similitudinem*, "Thou shalt not make to thyself a graven thing, nor the likeness of any thing"; the distinction between idol and likeness drawn below rests on the word Migne prints] Some think all these together to be one commandment: but if it be so reckoned, the number of ten commandments will not be made up. The first commandment, therefore, is: *There shall not be to thee other gods beside me,* etc. The second, *Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness,* etc. Let us begin, therefore, from the first commandment: *There shall not be to thee other gods beside me.* If he had said, They are not, the statement would seem more absolute; but because he says, *There shall not be,* he did not deny that they are, but forbade that they should be to him to whom these precepts are given. Hence I think Paul took it, I Cor. VIII: *If indeed [0250D] there are those who are called gods, whether in heaven or on earth.* And in other places thou wilt find gods named, as there: *A great king above all gods,* and *God hath spoken to God,* etc. [var: Vulg. Ps 49:1 *Deus deorum Dominus locutus est*, "The God of gods, the Lord, hath spoken"; the verse is adduced to show that Scripture names gods in the plural] Of lords the same Apostle says: *Whether thrones, or dominations,* etc. [n: (Col. I.)] But dominations are nothing else than a certain order and multitude of lords. In which the Apostle seems to open the sense of the law; as if he should say: Though there be many lords, who rule over other nations, and many gods who are worshipped by others: yet nevertheless there is one God to us, and one Lord. Whence Deut. XXXII: *When the Most High divided the nations and separated the sons of Adam, he appointed the bounds of the peoples according to the number of the angels of God.* [var: Vulg. Dt 32:8 *juxta numerum filiorum Israel*, "according to the number of the children of Israel"; the reading Migne prints is the Septuagint's, and the argument that follows stands on it] The angels, therefore, to whom the Most High committed the nations, Scripture calls lords [0251A] or gods: gods as being given by God, and lords as having obtained power from the Lord. Whence it is said to them in psalm LXXXI: *I have said: You are gods, and all of you the sons of the Most High. But you like men shall die, and shall fall like one of the princes;* imitating, that is, the devil, who was made the prince of all unto ruin. Whence it is plain that transgression, not nature, made them accursed. To thee, therefore, O Israel, who art the portion of God and his cord, *there shall not be,* he says, *to thee other gods beside me:* because he who is truly God is one God, and he who is truly Lord is one Lord; but upon the rest, created by himself, he conferred this name not by nature, but by grace. Nor are these things said only to Israel according to the flesh, but rather to thee who hast been made Israel by seeing God in mind, and circumcised in heart, not [0251B] in flesh; if only thou so act that thou be worthy to be the portion of God, and to be measured in the cord of his inheritance: otherwise let those be an example to thee who had been called to this, that they should be the portion of God, and, their sins requiring it, were scattered through all nations, and, having been brought out of the house of bondage, now serve not the Egyptians alone, but all nations. *For he who sins is the servant of sin [n: (Joan. V)]*.

VERS. 4.-- *Thou shalt not make,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] *Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness,* etc. Idols are one thing, gods another. For of gods the Apostle says, I Cor. VIII: *If indeed there are many gods, and many lords,* etc. But of idols, *that an idol is nothing in the world;* of likenesses, however, he did not add that they are nothing. But here it is said: *Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness whatsoever.* It is one thing, therefore, to make [0251C] an idol, another to make a likeness. For example, if a man in gold and silver, in wood or in stone, makes the figure of a fourfooted beast, or of a serpent, or of a bird, and sets it up to be adored, he makes not an idol but a likeness. But he who makes what is not, that is, a figure which the eye has not seen but the mind feigns to itself; as if a man should form a dog's head or a ram's upon human limbs, or two faces in the one shape of a man, or the hinder parts of a horse or of a fish upon a human breast; he who does this makes not a likeness, but an idol: for he makes what is not, nor has it anything like itself. Therefore the Apostle says *that an idol is nothing in the world [n: (I Cor. VIII)]*. For no figure is taken from things that subsist, but what an idle and curious mind has invented. But a likeness is when something is formed out of those things which are either in heaven, or on [0251D] earth, or in the waters: but as it is easy to pronounce concerning the likenesses of those things which are on earth or in the sea, it is not so concerning the heavenly ones; unless one should say that this can be understood of the sun, and the moon, and the stars, whose forms the heathen represent. But Moses, learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, desired to forbid also those things which among them were in secret, such as Hecate and the other forms of demons, which the Apostle calls *the spirits of wickedness in the high places*, Ephes. VI; of which perhaps it is said: *My sword is inebriated in heaven [n: (Isa. XXXIV)]*. For by these forms and likenesses they used to invoke demons for the repelling or the avoiding of evils. Yet it must be known that when thou hast resolved to keep this commandment, and to have one God and Lord only, [0252A] this is to declare war upon all the others, whom thou didst renounce in baptism, confessing God alone, Father and Son and Holy Spirit. But confessing this, unless we love the Lord our God with the whole heart, cleaving to him with the whole soul, with the whole strength, we are not made the portion of the Lord, but, set on the border, we suffer those whom we flee to be offended, and him to whom we flee we do not make favourable. To whom the prophet says: *Woe to the double soul [n: (Eccli. II)]*. And again: *How long do you halt upon both feet? [n: (III Reg. XVIII.)]* [var: Vulg. 3 Kg 18:21 *Usquequo claudicatis in duas partes?*, "How long do you halt between two sides?"] From those, then, we are cut down as fugitives, and by our Lord we are not defended as unstable, whence Jer. IV: *Thy lovers are made thine enemies.* [var: Vulg. Jer 4:30 *contempserunt te amatores tui; animam tuam quaerent*, "thy lovers have despised thee, they will seek thy life"; the sentence Migne prints stands nearest Lam 1:2] Many are the lovers of the soul, taken with her beauty, with whom she has played the harlot, saying: *I will go after my lovers who give [0252B] me my wine,* etc. [n: (Ose. II)]: these she offends when she returns to her former husband. Unless, therefore, thou remain in whole faith with thy husband, thou art suspect for the crimes which thou hast committed; he marks all thy motions, thy looks and thy goings; he can see nothing further in thee that is wanton, nothing dissolute or prodigal. If, therefore, thou neglect the grace received, and entangle thyself in worldly businesses, straightway the unclean spirit returns with seven others more wicked [n: (Matth. XII)], that he may not be cast out again, and the last things become worse than the first. There is, then, no fellowship of the temple of God with idols. If we are God's, we ought to be such in whom God may dwell and walk; whence Isa. LII: *You that carry the vessels of the Lord, go out and touch not the unclean thing, and I will receive you,* etc. He says, therefore: *There shall not be to thee other gods beside me.*

[0252C]VERS. 5-11.-- *Thou shalt not adore,* etc. It is one thing to worship, another to adore. A man can adore unwillingly, either flattering idolatrous kings, or overcome by torments, when he knows that an idol is nothing. But to worship is to be made over wholly to these with affection and zeal. He cuts off both, therefore, that thou mayest neither worship with affection, nor adore in outward show.

*I am the Lord,* etc. See the kindness of God: that he may teach us and make us perfect, he does not refuse the frailty of human affections; he does and suffers all things for our sake, and speaks to us by affections known and familiar to us.



## CHAPTER XX. (cont.)

[n: (ORIG., ubi supra.)] Every woman is either under a husband, and is subject to the laws of her husband [n: (Eph. V)], or is a harlot, and uses the liberty of sinning. He who goes in to a harlot knows that he has gone in to one prostituted and open to all, and therefore he cannot [0252D] be indignant about the others. He who uses lawful marriage does not suffer his wife to sin, but is kindled with jealousy to keep the chastity of the marriage, that he may be able to become a lawful father. So every soul is either prostituted with demons, and has many lovers that they may come in to her, now the spirit of fornication, now avarice, and after this will come the spirit of pride and very many others; yet the one does not envy the other, nor is moved with jealous rivalry, but they invite one another in turn; whence, *he brings with him seven other spirits more wicked than himself,* etc. [n: (Luc. XI.)] So the soul that is prostituted to demons suffers no jealous rivalry of her lovers; but if she has been joined to a lawful husband, that is, to Christ, even if at some time she has been a sinner, he does not suffer her to sin further, nor [0253A] can he bear that she should dally with adulterers; his jealousy is stirred up over her, and he defends the chastity of the marriage; but if he sees her violating the rights of the marriage, and seeking an occasion of sinning, he gives her a bill of divorce; whence Isa. L: *For your iniquities has he put away your mother;* and he who speaks this is both jealous and, moved by jealousy, says this. There is also this new kind of his goodness, that he receives her even after adultery when she returns and repents with her whole heart. Whence: *If a woman go out from her husband, and sleep with another man, shall she return again to her own husband?* etc. And elsewhere: *And after thou hast committed fornication in all these things, I said: Return,* etc. [n: (Jer. III.)] Here, therefore, if God being jealous requires and desires thy soul to cleave to him, if he keeps it, rebukes and is indignant, using as it were a certain jealous rivalry [0253B] toward thee, know that there is hope of salvation for thee. But if thou despise the rebuke, his jealousy departs from thee, according to that: *Therefore my jealousy departs from thee,* etc. [n: (Ezech. XVI.)] See the mercy and the loving-kindness of God; when he wishes to have mercy, he says that he is indignant and is angry, as he says by Jeremiah: *Thou shalt be chastised with sorrow and with the scourge, O Jerusalem, that my soul depart not from thee [n: (Jer. VI)]*. *For he scourges every son whom he receives [n: (Hebr. XII)]*. But this is a terrible thing, this is the utmost, if we are not rebuked for our sins: for when we exceed the measure of sinning, even the jealousy of God will be taken from us, and he is angry no more.

*Visiting the iniquity,* etc. [n: (ORIG.)] Rendering the sins of the fathers upon the sons, etc. The heretics say that it is not the word of a good God, who says that one is punished [0253C] for the sins of another; yet they say that the God of the law is just, and they cannot show how he is unjust [cj: *injustus*; read *justus*, "how he is just"], if he punishes one when another sins. But our inward man is said either to have God for father, if he lives according to God; or the devil, if he does his will; whence John VIII: *You are of your father the devil, and you will do the desires of your father.* As, therefore, the seed of God is in us, when, keeping the word of God, we do not sin, according to that of I John III, *He who is of God sins not, because the seed of God abides in him;* so when we are persuaded by the devil to sin, we receive his seed; when we do what he persuades, he has now begotten us, for we are born his sons through sin. But because we scarcely ever sin without a helper, as no one commits adultery alone, it is necessary that a partner in the adultery be made, [0253D] and if not more, it is necessary that some man or woman be joined to the service of the crime: all of whom, as it were one from another, begotten according to the order of the persuasion, draw their offspring from the devil; as in the betraying of the Lord the devil entered into the heart of Judas, and begot the first son of sin, who could not do this alone; he went therefore to the Scribes and the Pharisees and the chief priests; there is born, therefore, from Judas a third and a fourth generation of sin. Let us see, therefore, how God renders the sins of the fathers upon the sons unto the third and the fourth generation, and not upon the man himself. For of the fathers nothing is said. The devil, therefore, because he has already exceeded the measure of sinning, like a garment clotted in blood, will not be clean in this world; he is not scourged [0254A] for sin; for all things are kept for him unto the future. whence Matth. VIII): *Why art thou come to torment us before the time?* But the sins are rendered upon the sons whom he has begotten through sin, who, being set in the flesh, are scourged. *God wills not the death of the sinner, but rather that he be converted and live [n: (Ezech. XXXIII)]*. Mercifully, therefore, he renders the sins of the fathers upon the sons, because the devil and his angels, who are also made the fathers of sin, are unworthy to be rebuked in the present: their sons, and those who through them have been taken into the fellowship of sin, receive in the present what they have done, that, being purged, they may not be made partners with the devil in punishment. Whence in psalm LXXXVIII: *I will visit their iniquities with a rod,* etc., *but my mercy I will not take away from them.* [var: Vulg. Ps 88:34 *misericordiam autem meam non dispergam ab eo*, "but my mercy I will not scatter away from him"; Migne's plural is what carries the argument to the sons] For God is [0254B] jealous, and the soul which he betroths to himself in faith he wills not to remain in the defilement of sin, but to be quickly purged. But if she will not be purged, he will laugh at their destruction, saying to them, Matth. XXV: *Depart from me, you cursed, into everlasting fire,* etc. Let us know, therefore, that we are rebuked in the present, that in the future we may rest. Whence: *When we are chastised, we are rebuked by the Lord, that we be not condemned with this world.*

[n: (ID.)] In this saying the heretics are wont to reproach us, that it is not the word of a good God, who judges one for the sins of another. But those who shall say that the God of the law who commands these things is not good, yet call him just: nor indeed can they themselves show how it should seem agreeable to justice if, when one sins, another is punished.

[0254C] [n: (ISID.)] The first commandment forbids stealthy deceit; the second, error; the third slays the love of the world; the fourth, impiety; the fifth, cruelty; the sixth, fornication; the seventh, rapacity; the eighth destroys falsehood; the ninth, the coveting of the world; the tenth, the thought of adultery. And it is to be noted that as the Egyptians are struck with ten plagues, so the tables are written with ten precepts, by which the people of God may be ruled and the demons slain. For the law was written on two tables that the two Testaments might be signified: or because there are two precepts of love, of God and of one's neighbour, *on which the whole law hangs, and the prophets [n: (Matth. XXII)]*. For on the one are three precepts, which pertain to the love of God; on the other seven, which pertain to the fellowship of one's neighbour. But the tables were [0254D] of stone, because the heart of the Jews was stony and unfeeling, hard and dull; whence Ezech. XXXVI: *I will take away the stony heart, and will give them a heart of flesh.* And the Apostle says [n: (II Cor. III)]: *Not in tables of stone, but in the fleshly tables of the heart,* which, that is, may perceive and understand.

VERS. 12.-- *Honour thy father,* etc. This is the first of the seven commandments which pertain to one's neighbour. And rightly are they seven. For the number seven consists of three and four. And three befits the soul, which is irascible, concupiscible and rational. Four, the body.

VERS. 13.-- *Thou shalt not kill,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 61 in Exod.)] And as to what was said: *thou shalt not kill,* it is not to be thought [0255A] that anything is done against this precept in that the law kills, or that God commands someone to be killed: for he does it who commands what it is not lawful for the minister to refuse.

VERS. 14.-- *Thou shalt not commit adultery.* [n: (ISID.)] It is also asked: how does *thou shalt not commit adultery* differ from that which is said a little after: *Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's wife?* [var: Vulg. Ex 20:17 *Non concupisces domum proximi tui, nec desiderabis uxorem ejus*, "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's house, neither shalt thou desire his wife"; the wording Migne prints is Dt 5:21's] For in that which was said, *thou shalt not commit adultery,* that also could be understood; unless perhaps in those two precepts of not committing adultery and not stealing the deeds themselves are marked, but in these last the very concupiscence: which differ so far, that sometimes a man commits adultery who does not covet his neighbour's wife, when he is joined to her for some other cause. But sometimes he covets, and is not joined to her, fearing punishment: and this perhaps the law wished to show, [0255B] that both are sins.

[n: (ID.)] Again it is wont to be asked whether under the name of adultery fornication also is held, etc., *as far as* and the unlawful use of those members ought to be understood to be forbidden.

VERS. 15.-- *Thou shalt not steal,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 71 in Exod.)] But again it is asked how *thou shalt not steal* differs from that which is said a little after concerning the not coveting of a neighbour's goods? Not that everyone who covets his neighbour's goods steals; but everyone who steals covets his neighbour's goods. In that generality, where it is enjoined not to covet a neighbour's goods, that also which pertains to theft could have been contained.

VERS. 16, 17.-- *Thou shalt not speak against thy neighbour,* etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra. )] In that also which was said, etc., [0255C] *as far as* and things of this sort.

VERS. 18.-- *And all the people saw the voices,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 72 in Exod.)] It is wont to be asked how the people saw a voice, since a voice seems to pertain not to sight but to hearing, etc., *as far as* hear what shines, we are not accustomed to say.

VERS. 19.-- *Speak thou to us,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 73.)] It is signified that fear pertains rather to the Old Testament, as love to the New; although in the Old the New lies hidden, and in the New the Old lies open. But how it can be granted to such a people to see the voice of God, if [0256A] this is to be taken as understanding, since they fear that God should speak to them lest they die, is not sufficiently clear.

VERS. 20.-- *Fear not,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 74.)] Thus they were to be restrained from sins, by fearing, that is, lest they should suffer sensible punishments; because they were not yet able to love justice, and they were tempted by the Lord, that it might appear of what sort they were; not that they might become known to God whatever sort they were, from whom nothing was hidden, but that they might become known among themselves and to their own selves.

VERS. 21, 22.-- *And the people stood,* etc. [n: (GREG., homil. 6.)] In certain places of Scripture the history is to be kept, etc., *as far as* Moses went to the darkness in which God was.

*You shall not make gods,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 76.)] What was said in the first precept is repeated; and by the things of silver [0256B] and of gold, all idols are understood, as in psalm CXIII: *The idols of the nations are silver and gold, the work of the hand of men.*

VERS. 24.-- *An altar of earth,* etc. He shows that therefore, idols being forbidden, an altar of earthly matter is granted, that to the one God victims and sacrifices may be offered upon it.

[n: (GREG., lib. III Moral., cap. 20.)] To make God an altar of earth is to hope for the incarnation of Christ. For then is our gift received by God, when upon this altar our humility, that is, has laid whatever it does upon the faith of the Lord's incarnation. Upon an altar of earth, therefore, we lay the offered gift, if we make our acts firm by faith in the Lord's incarnation.



## CHAPTER XX. (cont.)

VERS. 25, 26.-- *Of hewn stones.* [n: (ISID.)] Hewn [0256C] stones are they who cut the unity asunder, and divide themselves from the fellowship of the brethren: these Christ does not receive into his body, of which body the building of the altar signifies the figure. The stones not hewn, of which the altar is commanded to be built, are they who through faith are made firm in unity; of whom it is said, I Peter II: *You are living stones, built together into spiritual houses.* [var: Vulg. 1 Pt 2:5 *et ipsi tamquam lapides vivi superaedificamini, domus spiritualis*, "be you also as living stones built up, a spiritual house"] Upon these no iron has been laid, because they are uncorrupted, and have not received the fiery darts of the evil one: all make one altar in the unity of faith and the concord of charity. [nt: In numbering the precepts of the decalogue, Origen and Augustine, in the testimonies of the Gloss cited at the head of this chapter, seem to follow contrary opinions. For Origen holds that the ten commandments cannot be reckoned unless it stand thus: Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness, etc. Augustine, however, though he does not reject that enumeration of Origen's, yet judges another, a different one, more fitting, so that he reckons the prohibition of making idols and graven images for the sake of worship to be the explication of the first precept: and then he sets down two precepts for restraining forbidden desires, one whereby the coveting of another's wife, the other whereby the coveting of all other things that are one's neighbour's, is to be restrained. Origen is followed by Athanasius in the Synopsis of Scripture; Gregory Nazianzen in the poem in which he numbers the ten precepts; Chrysostom in his second exposition of Matthew, homily 49; Jerome and Ambrose, chapter six of the Epistle to the Ephesians. Augustine's numbering, in that sense, is followed by the scholastic doctors, who, even as Augustine himself, prefer it to the former, yet without holding the former to be unfitting. And indeed the contrariety which a perverse disposition finds in subduing those two covetings would seem rather to have wanted two distinct precepts, since there are those who covet another's wife, and yet disdain to covet another's wealth or servants, and the reverse: but no true worshipper of God finds it difficult to spurn idols. Yet this diversity of numbering is, in the thing itself, nothing at all: for no precept is laid down by Augustine that is denied by Origen, or the reverse; but the one joins precepts by a certain ordering of subordination, which the other separates by reason of a verbal connection, and conversely. For Augustine joins the precept of not making idols to the precept of worshipping no God but one, because the former is subordinate to the latter and is the fuller explication of it, extending also to the forbidding of the ceremonies of the Gentiles: but Origen divides that same matter into two precepts, because they seem to be distinguished by the very structure of the sentence, with no mark of connection, just as the rest that follow: Thou shalt not have strange gods; Thou shalt not make to thyself a graven thing; Thou shalt not take, etc. And again, those two prohibitions of the twofold coveting Origen joins into one precept, on account of the sequence of the context, which seem to belong to one and the same sentence.]



## CHAPTER XXI.

[0257A]

VERS. 1. 6.-- *These are the judgments,* etc. [n: (AUG., loc. 91 in Exod.)] From now on those things are more distinctly explained which pertain to one's neighbour; the manner of speaking is to be noted, since it was said to Moses: *These are the judgments which thou shalt set before them;* the rest are said to the people.

*If thou buy a Hebrew servant,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 77 in Exod.)] Lest Christians should here demand this of their masters, the apostle commands servants to be subject to their masters [n: (I Petr. II)], lest the name of the Lord and the doctrine be blasphemed: for from this it is plain that it was commanded as a mystery, because God commanded the ear of him who had refused liberty to be bored through with an awl to the door-post.

[n: (GREG., hom. 3 in Ezech., tom. II.)] It must be known that, [0257B] since the active and the contemplative life are from the grace of God, etc., *as far as* because that inmost rest is seen in a dark manner.

*Six years.* [n: (ISID.)] Serving in the six ages of this world, on the seventh day, that is, in the eternal sabbath, we shall be set free, if only we have been willing to be free while in this world we serve sin. But if we have been unwilling, the ear will be bored through in testimony of disobedience, and with our wife and our sons, whom we have preferred to liberty, that is, with the flesh and its works, we shall serve sin for ever.

VERS. 7-12.-- *If a man sell his daughter,* etc. *[n: (AUG., quaest. 78 in Exod.)] She shall not go out as maidservants are wont to depart,* etc., *as far as* it is understood that she shall go out with him freely, not separated from her husband.

VERS. 13-17.-- *But he who lay not in wait for him,* etc. [n: (RAB., in Exod., tom. II.)] For those who are homicides not of their own will, etc., *as far as* he shall have set of Christ, he shall be saved.

*[n: (AUG., quaest. 79 in Exod.)] But if he did it unwillingly, and God delivered him into his hands,* etc. This is so said, etc., *as far as* and therefore Judas justly betrayed him.

VERS. 18.-- *If men quarrel,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] It is the same thing, if a man, contending with his neighbour, has exasperated him by hardness of mind, or by niggardliness, that is, by despising him or by not taking counsel with him; and he, made weak by sadness, has languished away from the fervour of love: let the guilty man, by confession, expend upon the physicians, that is, the teachers, the labour of penance and the cost of the promised amendment, that they may raise up the sick man with the staff of consolation, and by the fomentations of preachings restore him to his former state of love. [0257D] In correction discretion is necessary, lest he who ought to correct should kill, or should cast a man into despair or into hatred.

VERS. 22-28.-- *If men quarrel.* [n: (ID.)] If anyone, after the conceiving of the word, has injured another's soul through carelessness or through fraud, so that it cannot bring forth sound the living germ of a good work: even if that soul itself has persevered still living in the faith, it will be necessary, according to the ministry of the Gospel, that he make restitution [0258A] through penance for the loss inflicted. But if the soul itself, slain by error, after the bringing forth of the abortion, that is, of the death-bearing work, has persevered in unbelief, without doubt the seducer of it, as a true homicide, will be guilty of eternal death.

[n: (AUG., quaest. 80 in Exod.)] That he would not have an unformed birth pertain to homicide, etc., *as far as* and therefore not yet endowed with senses.

*[n: (ORIG., hom. 10 in Exod.)] But if two men shall quarrel, and shall strike a woman with child, and her infant shall come forth not yet formed, he shall suffer detriment, as much as the woman's husband shall lay upon him, and he shall give for his own soul. But if it shall have been formed, he shall give life for life, eye for eye,* etc. [var: Vulg. Ex 21:22-23 *et abortivum quidem fecerit, sed ipsa vixerit: subjacebit damno quantum maritus mulieris expetierit, et arbitri judicaverint. Sin autem mors ejus fuerit subsecuta, reddet animam pro anima*; the Vulgate has no formed/unformed distinction at all, and the whole gloss below expounds it; the reading Migne prints is the Septuagint's] Let us see first concerning him [0258B] who shall come forth not yet formed, how one of the litigants is commanded to be struck with the loss of money, whereas two are spoken of. And also this, that the woman's husband lays it upon him, and not upon them; and he shall give, and not rather they shall give. But if the infant shall come forth already formed, we easily understand the giving of life for life: that is, that the sin be avenged by death. But the things which follow it is worth while to explain. For neither does the infant, even formed, seem to have lost an eye in the womb, when struck by the heel of a litigant, and teeth it had not at all. For if we refer this to the mother, how does a woman miscarrying lose an eye, or suffer in her teeth? But be it that, struck either in the eye or in the tooth, she miscarried, and received a bruise or a wound, what shall we say also of burning for burning? Could a woman standing by men [0258C] who quarrel be burned, so that burning should be paid for burning? In Deuteronomy also these things cannot easily be explained. Yet in the Scriptures the members of the soul are named by the same words and offices by which the members of the body are used or called. Whence Matth. VII: *Thou seest the mote in thy brother's eye, and the beam which is in thine own eye thou seest not.* And Matth. XIII: *He that hath ears to hear, let him hear.* And Rom. X: *How beautiful are the feet of them that preach peace!* Let not, therefore, the likeness of words disturb us. There are, then, two litigants, two disputing also about the doctrines or the questions of the law, and quarrelling about strifes of words; whence the Apostle, II Tim. II: *Contend not in words,* etc. And in the same place: *Avoid the questions of the law, knowing that they beget strifes.* They, therefore, who [0258D] quarrel in these questions to the subverting of the hearers, strike a woman with child, and cast out an infant formed or still unformed. The woman with child is called the soul, which has lately conceived the word of God; whence: *From thy fear, O Lord, we have conceived in the womb and have brought forth [n: (Isa. XXVI)]*. They, therefore, who conceive and straightway bring forth, are not to be reckoned women, but men and perfect; whence elsewhere: *If the earth brought forth in one day, and [0259A] a nation was born in one day together [n: (Isa. LXVI)]*. This, then, is the generation of the perfect, which on the same day is conceived and born. And men bring forth according to that of Gal. IV: *My little children, of whom I am in labour again, until Christ be formed in you.* The perfect, therefore, and men bring forth straightway, that is, they carry through into works the word of faith conceived: but the soul which conceives, and holds in the womb and does not bring forth, is called a woman. Whence Isa. XXXVII: *The pains of birth are come upon her: and there is not strength in her to bring forth.* This soul, therefore, while two are quarrelling among themselves and putting forth scandals, is struck and scandalized: so that it casts away the word of faith which it had thinly conceived, not yet formed. For in some the word is formed, in some not formed; whence: *Until Christ be formed in you [n: (Gal. IV)]*; for Christ is the Word of God. [0259B] He, therefore, shall suffer loss who has struck it out. Concerning losses, therefore, the Apostle speaks of the teacher, I Cor. III: *If any man's work burn, he shall suffer loss,* etc. [var: Vulg. 1 Cor 3:15 *detrimentum patietur*, "he shall suffer detriment"; the exposition that follows is built throughout on the word *damnum* which Migne prints] And the Lord himself in the Gospel, Matth. XVI: *What does it profit a man if he gain the whole world, but lose his own soul?* [var: Vulg. Mt 16:26 *animae vero suae detrimentum patiatur*, "and suffer the loss of his own soul"] Whence it seems to be shown that certain sins pertain to loss, yet not to destruction, because he who shall have suffered loss is said to be saved, though by fire: whence John says that certain sins are unto death, certain not [n: (I Joan. V)]. But which are unto death cannot easily be discerned, *For who understands sins? [n: (Psal. XVIII.)]* But when, upon five minas, five others gained are brought back, and upon two, two [n: (Luc. XIX)], he who loses the reward of this gain is said to be struck with loss, like him who has given cause of scandal to a weaker [0259C] and womanish soul. And he shall give according to what her husband shall lay upon him, and he shall give with honour to the soul that learns. The husband is the master Christ, or he who is for Christ a teacher of souls. He, therefore, shall suffer loss who contends to the subverting of the hearers, for the soul which had not yet a formed infant: which can be taken of the scandal of a catechumen. It can also come to pass that he who has injured should instruct and repair the soul which he has lost, and that with honour, that is, with modesty, with meekness, not with strife, as when he brought in the scandal. But when the infant shall have been formed, he shall give life for life. The formed infant may be seen to be the word of God in the heart of the soul which has received the grace of baptism, or which has received the word of God more evidently and more clearly. He, therefore, who struck shall render life for [0259D] life in the day of judgment, that is, before that judge who can destroy both soul and body in hell. Or, that he who shall be conscious to himself of so great a scandal, should lay down his own soul for the soul of the man scandalized, and even unto death give his labour that he may be restored to the faith. Let him lay down eye for eye: if he has injured the eye of the soul, that is, has troubled its understanding, let his eye be taken away from him by him who presides over the Church, that is, his understanding. If he has injured a tooth, which, receiving the food of the word of God, was wont to break it small or to grind it with the molars, that it might pass on a subtle meaning to the belly of the soul: if he has vexed and plucked out this tooth, so that by the wearing away of it the soul cannot subtly and spiritually receive the word of God, his tooth shall be taken away, who does not well break small and divide the foods [0260A] of the word. For on this account perhaps it is said of the Lord: *Thou hast broken the teeth of sinners [n: (Psl. III)]*. And elsewhere: *The Lord shall break the grinders of the lions [n: (Psal. LVII)]*. So, then, the soul is said to be injured and struck through its members. He demands also hand for hand, and foot for foot. The hand of the soul is the power by which it can hold something and grasp it to the heart, as its act and its strength; and the foot is that by which it goes to good things or to evil. Because, therefore, the soul that is scandalized is cast down not only in faith but also in acts, which are signified by the hands, his hands are taken away by which he does not work well, and his feet, by which he does not walk well. He shall also receive burning, because he burned and delivered a soul to hell. By each of these things it is shown that the striker, all his members being lopped off, is to be cut away from the body of the Church, [0260B] that the rest, seeing it, may have fear, and may not do the like. Therefore the Apostle, when he describes the teacher of the Church, among other things enjoins, *not a striker [n: (I Tim. III)]*: lest, striking women with child, that is, unwise souls, he should lay down life for life, eye for eye, tooth for tooth, etc. Of such it is said, Matth. XXIV: *Woe to them that are with child and that give suck in those days, in which they shall be scandalized, if it be possible, even the elect.* [var: Vulg. Mt 24:19 ends at *in illis diebus*; the clause that follows welds on Mt 24:24 *ita ut in errorem inducantur (si fieri potest) etiam electi*, "so that even the elect (if it be possible) be led into error"] Yet it must be known that it does not belong to the perfect to be scandalized, but to women or to little ones; whence Matth. XVIII: *If any man scandalize one of these little ones,* etc.

VERS. 29-36.-- *But if the ox was wont to push with his horn,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] That is, if the master, that is the spirit, does not restrain the proud motion of the mind, and brings destruction upon souls, or [0260C] harm upon bodies, the guilt redounds upon the keeper. Whence in the sixteenth chapter of Proverbs: *The heart of a man shall instruct his mouth, and shall add grace to his lips.* [var: Vulg. Pr 16:23 *Cor sapientis erudiet os ejus*, "The heart of the wise shall instruct his mouth"]

*Thirty shekels.* That is, according to the commandments of the law, he shall make amends by faith and hope and charity.

*Of silver.* He shall repay the brightness of the divine word, in which they had been instructed in the faith of the Trinity and the knowledge of the decalogue.

*If a man open,* etc. [n: (GREG., lib. XVII Moral., cap. 13.)] To open a cistern is to penetrate the secrets of Holy Scripture with a strong understanding, etc., *as far as* thence the weakness of the hearers should slip down to the depths.

*If another's ox,* etc. [n: (AUG.)] This is to be observed not in the ox alone, but in every beast; for from the part the [0260D] whole is understood. But this cannot be done concerning the flesh of a beast that has been killed, on which they do not feed.



## CHAPTER XXII.

VERS. 1.-- *If any man steal,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] According to the letter, five oxen are rendered for one ox, and four sheep for one sheep: because an ox has five uses, a sheep four. For an ox is sacrificed, ploughs, feeds, gives milk, and furnishes its hide for various uses; but a sheep is sacrificed, and feeds, and yields milk and wool. Allegorically, however, by the ox is understood any Jew, and if any Jew sell him, he will lie under the penalty of the law, which is contained in the five books of Moses. By the sheep, the Christian, who imitates Christ, [0261A] of whom it is said: *He was led as a sheep to the slaughter [n: (Isa. LIII)]*. If any man sell this one, let him render four sheep, that is, let him do penance according to the ordinance of the four Gospels.

VERS. 2-5.-- *If a thief be found breaking open a house, or undermining it,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 84 in Exod.)] If a thief be found breaking in, and being struck shall die, etc., *as far as* for now he is more than a thief.

VERS. 6-13.-- *If a fire break out*, etc. [n: (RAB.)] We kindle a fire when we prepare correction for our subjects, that we may consume the thorns of vices. But it must be seen to, lest, while we pursue the vices, we injure the virtues, and harm before their ripeness those who had begun to bring forth the fruit of good works. He, therefore, who shall have done this, let him bear worthy penance, and what he has injured through slothfulness [0261B] let him diligently restore.

*The ears of corn.* Tender hearers not yet coming to the ripeness of fruit: whose vices he who sharply and indiscreetly rebukes, casts fire as it were into the ears of corn, and does not suffer them to come to ripeness.

VERS. 14-16.-- *He who from his neighbour,* etc. We ask a loan of our neighbour when for an eternal recompense we take up the care of the Lord's flock. But if we always set before the eyes of the heart the fear of God, as of one exacting money, we are as it were always in his presence. Whence II Cor. V: *Knowing the fear of the Lord, we persuade men.* And elsewhere, II Cor. XII: *As before God, in Christ we speak.* The death, therefore, or the maiming of the beast is not imputed to the shepherd, if he has borne its care strenuously, and as though God were present; otherwise he is compelled to render the loss. [0261C] Whence Matth. V: *He shall not go out thence, till he repay the last farthing.*

VERS. 17.-- *If the father of the virgin,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] The sense of the sentence is historical, out of which the discourse is born. If, therefore, the historical sense builds up the reign of charity and destroys that of cupidity, there is no need for it to be expounded allegorically. Yet the reader ought to honour the sentence with the dowry of right faith, and to leave it untouched by his own exposition.

VERS. 18-20.-- *Thou shalt not suffer wizards to live.* Those who work by the sleights of the magic art and by diabolical feignings, understand as heretics, who are to be excommunicated from the fellowship of the faithful who truly live, until the evil-doing of error die in them

VERS. 21.-- *Thou shalt not grieve the stranger.* [n: (RAB.)] He commends the love of one's neighbour through the compassion of mercy, [0261D] lest, moved by the goad of fury or of avarice, we neglect the right of nature. But what we wish men to do to us, the same let us do to them, and what we do not wish to be done, let us not do.

VERS. 22-24.-- *You shall not hurt the widow and the orphan.* [n: (RAB.)] By the widow, holy Church, which in the day of judgment awaits Christ, whom now she has not present; by the orphan, the Christian people, to whom the world their father, and Babylon their mother, is dead, may be taken: whom he forbids to be grieved by persecution or by worldly pride, lest perchance such harrying come to the spiritual ruin of those who vex them in vain with hatreds and reproaches.

VERS. 25.-- *If money on loan,* etc. [var: Vulg. Ex 22:25 *Si pecuniam mutuam dederis*, "If thou lend money"; Migne's nominative *pecunia* does not construe with the accusative *mutuam*] [n: (RAB.)] He warns the preachers [0262A] of the Gospel not to seek superfluity, but having food and clothing, to be content with these, according to that of Matth. X: *Freely you have received, freely give.*

VERS. 26, 27.-- *If a pledge,* etc. [n: (AUG., loc. 99.)] He put the genus for the species: for it was so said as if he had given a pledge of any garment, whereas Scripture testifies from what follows that it speaks specially of him who has no garment but that one wherewith he may be covered by night.

*[n: (GREG., lib. XVI Moral., cap. 2.)] A pledge,* the confession of sin. For our neighbour is made our debtor, etc., *as far as* let him feel that what he has done amiss is presently remitted by us.

VERS. 28.-- *Thou shalt not speak ill of the gods,* etc. *(AUG., quaest, [0262B] 86 in Exod.)* It is asked whom he called gods, etc., *as far as* or to be honoured with the services of latria.

VERS. 29-31.-- *Thy tithes,* etc. [n: (ISID. in Gen., tom. V.)] By the firstfruits of the crops or of the firstborn are shown the first beginnings of good works, etc., *as far as* and the effect of perfection is granted.



## CHAPTER XXIII.

VERS. 3.-- *Neither shalt thou have pity on a poor man in judgment.* [n: (AUG.)] That is, lest against justice thou favour the poor man for mercy's sake. Mercy is good, but it ought not to be against judgment. Now Scripture calls judgment that which is just. But lest anyone, on account of this sentence, should think that God forbids mercy, there fittingly follows:

[0262C]VERS. 4-6.-- *If thou meet thy enemy's ox,* etc. As if to say: show mercy even toward thine enemies, when the power is free from judging; for when thou leadest back the straying ox of thine enemy and restorest it, thou art not sitting as judge between any parties.

VERS. 7-9.-- *Thou shalt fly lying.* [n: (RAB.)] The Lord loves truth and judgment, and to the performing of these he exhorts his people, because he is no respecter of persons.

VERS. 10-12.-- *Six years thou shalt sow thy ground.* [n: (AUG.)] It is asked what the poor gather, if in the seventh year the land is thus spared, etc., *as far as* six years, when those things are gathered which are sown.

[n: (RAB.)] In the number six is the perfection of works, etc., *as far as he that soweth in blessings, in blessings shall also [0262D] reap.* [var: Vulg. 2Cor 9:6 *de benedictionibus et metet*, "shall reap also of blessings"; Migne repeats the preposition of the first member]

VERS. 13.-- *And by the name of strange gods,* etc. He permits swearing by his own name, not because it is good to swear, but because he knew that men are prone to swearing; he does not will that they swear by the names of outward gods, lest by frequent oaths they be led into the worship of them. So too God does not approve victims, yet he had rather that sacrifice be made to himself than to idols.

VERS. 14-18.-- *Three times in every year,* etc. Here, that is, God requires the faith of the holy Trinity: for at the Pasch the Son is immolated, at Pentecost the Holy Spirit is given, at the Scenopegia the power of the Father's majesty is declared.

*Three times in every year,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] This [0263A] is: at every season of the present life, and in the confession of the holy Trinity, through faith and hope and charity, you shall render the services of good works to God your creator. For we celebrate the Pasch in the month of new things, when, delivered by the blood of the unspotted Lamb from Egyptian servitude, we pass through spiritual waters out of the old man into the new, not in the oldness of the letter but in the newness of the spirit, whereby we come into the land of promise through the grace of Christ. But we celebrate the solemnity of the month of firstfruits when we consecrate to the Lord the firstfruits of our works, of our wills and of our words; the solemnity also at the going out of the year, when, the crops being gathered in, that is, the fruits of the virtues, we strive to come to the end of life and to the entrance of the heavenly kingdom; in tabernacles [0263B] commemorating for seven days, because through all the time of the present life we acknowledge that we are pilgrims, and seek rest not here but in the time to come, diligently taking care that we appear not empty in the sight of God.

*The solemnity also,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] When the crops, that is, are ripe, when the fruits of the whole year have been gathered: namely in September, which the Hebrews call by the Chaldee word thisri, תשרי, and the Macedonians ὑπερβεραῖον. At the beginning of it the Jews, keeping holiday, sound the trumpet more than in the other months, and offer more things; in which month is the day of expiations, and the day of the Scenopegia on the fifteenth day, which is the solemnity of Tabernacles.

VERS. 19.-- *Thou shalt not boil a kid,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 90 in Exod.)] How it is to be understood according to the propriety of the words, I know not whether it can be found out, [0263C] etc., *as far as* but afterwards it was not lambs in milk that suffered, but rams in the flock.

[n: (STRAB.)] *Morally.* The kid, that is, the sinner, is not to be boiled in his mother's milk, that is, dissolved by blandishments, but sharply rebuked and refuted.

VERS. 20, 21.-- *Behold I will send an angel,* etc. [n: (RAB.)] Josue signifies the leader of the people, who is therefore called an angel because he is sent to announce the will of the Lord; for ἄγγελος in Greek is called *a messenger*.

VERS. 22.-- *I will be an enemy,* etc. [n: (GREG.)] The root of all evil is pride, of which it is said in Eccli. X: *Pride the beginning of all sin.* Now its offspring are the seven principal vices: vainglory, envy, [0263D] anger, sadness, avarice, gluttony of the belly, lust. And because our Redeemer grieved that we were held captive by these seven vices of pride, he came full of the Spirit of sevenfold grace to the spiritual battle of our deliverance.

VERS. 23, 24.-- *And an angel shall go before thee.* [n: (RAB.)] Josue led the people of Israel into the land of promise, casting out seven nations and destroying their idols and their altars. Mystically the true Jesus exterminates these nations with their images, when he drives out the seven principal vices and every idol of the enemy from our hearts, that we may securely possess the land of our own nature.

*Whom I will destroy.* [n: (CHRYSOST.)] Every vice has its own dwelling in our heart, [0264A] and, claiming that dwelling for itself, it exterminates Israel in the recess of our soul, that is, the contemplation of the highest things, and does not cease to oppose; for virtues cannot abide together with vices. But when the vices have been overcome by the Israelites, that is, by the virtues, the place which the spirit of concupiscence or of fornication possessed, chastity shall obtain; the place which fury had seized, patience shall claim; the place which sadness working death had occupied, saving gladness full of joy shall possess; the place which sloth laid waste, fortitude shall cultivate; the place which pride trod down, humility shall make honourable. And so, the several vices being expelled, their places, that is, the affections, shall be possessed by the contrary virtues; and these are named the sons of Israel, that is, of the soul that sees God: who, when they have driven out all the passions of the heart, are to be believed not so much to have seized [0264B] other men's possessions as to have recovered their own; because, according to the ancient tradition, the sons of Sem obtained by lot, in the division of the world, the land of the Chananaeans, which the Chananaeans possessed by violence: whence by the just, judgment of God they were driven out. The will of the Lord also assigned the possession of our heart not to vices but to virtues, which after the transgression of Adam, when the vices grew insolent like Chananaeans, were driven from their proper region. But when by the grace of God they are restored by our diligence and by our labour, they do not so much occupy another's land as receive back their own.

VERS. 25-27.-- *I will bless thy loaves,* etc. [n: (AUG.)] Another reading: *I will bless thy bread and thy wine,* etc. Although these promises can be understood spiritually also, etc., *as far as* which the ungodly shall not receive.

[0264C]VERS. 28.-- *Sending out hornets. [n: (AUG., quaest. 93.)]* I will send wasps, etc. This God promises, etc., *as far as* if anything is said by Christ.

Another reading. *I will send wasps before thee, and it shall cast out the Amorrhites,* etc. He turns himself from the plural to the singular number. And *it shall cast out the Amorrhites* is understood of the wasp, namely as of the locust, as of the frog: not that it is one, but by the singular the plural is understood.

VERS. 29-33.-- *I will not cast them out from the face,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] These nations signify the vices, which are extinguished in the saints neither all together nor at one time, lest the monsters of the earth be multiplied, that is, lest they be lifted up into pride and vainglory and things of this kind. For when the saints fight against the greater vices and conquer, they leave [0264D] the lesser, and these are permitted to have dominion, lest through elation they fall more grievously. Whence to Paul was given a sting of the flesh for the keeping of humility [n: (I Cor. XXII)].



## CHAPTER XXIV.

VERS. 1.-- *And to Moses he said: Come up to the Lord, thou and Aaron,* etc. [n: (AUG., quaest. 85 in Exod., tom. III.)] Up to this place the justifications of Scripture are understood, etc., *as far as* but the Latins say that those are justifications which the Greeks call δικαιώματα.

*Thou and Aaron,* etc. [n: (STRAB.)] Moses and Aaron and Nadab and Abiu. By these four are signified evangelical men, that is, those who follow the evangelical precepts. [0265A] But by the seventy (which number consists of the number seven taken ten times, or of the number ten multiplied seven times) are signified those who, helped by the grace of the Holy Spirit, fulfilling the ten of the law, stretch toward the contemplation of the Creator. Or, according to the letter, those of whom it is said in Num. XI: *Take unto thee seventy men,* etc., among whom were Heldad and Meldad. [var: Vulg. Nm 11:16 *Congrega mihi septuaginta viros de senibus Israel*, "Gather unto me seventy men of the ancients of Israel"]

VERS. 2.-- *And Moses alone shall come up to the Lord.* [n: (RAB.)] First it is commanded that Moses and Aaron, Nadab and Abiu, and the seventy elders should come up to the Lord. And at once it is subjoined that Moses alone should come up to the Lord, and that the rest should not draw near, but adore afar off. Where we are morally instructed to weigh our own strength, and not to reach the citadel of contemplation beyond our power, but so far as the divine clemency [0265B] calls, and the rule of Scripture invites.

[n: (GREG.)] Moses, that he might receive the sublime precepts of God, climbed on high; and that he might penetrate inward things, he is hidden from outward tumults: because holy men, who are compelled by necessity to serve outward offices, always flee back studiously to the secret places of the heart, and there ascend the summit of their inmost thought, and receive the law as it were upon a mountain, while, the tumults of temporal actions being set aside, they search out upon the height of their contemplation the sentence of the supernal will.

VERS. 3-5.-- *We will do [n: (AUG., quaest. 95.)]* It is to be noted that again the people, etc., *as far as* they laid hold of justice.

VERS. 6-9.-- *He took therefore,* etc. *[ID., quaest. 98.]* [0265C] It is to be noted that Moses now sacrificed for the first time, etc., *as far as* for that the decalogue was written on tables of stone appears afterwards.

*Bowls.* A *cratera*, or *crater*, is so called ἀπὸ τοῦ κρατεῖν, which is *to hold,* in that it is able to hold much wine.

VERS. 10, 11.-- *Under his feet.* God is not contained by place, nor does he set the members of a body in a posture, as it belongs to our body to sit, and to lie, to stand, and the like, which are of the body only. But God is a spirit. Therefore that he shows himself under a bodily appearance, or by signs bodily expressed, is not the appearing of his substance, which is what he himself is; rather the assumption of visible forms lies subject to all his power.

VERS. 12.-- *Come up to me,* etc. *(BED., lib. I de [0265D] Tabernac., cap. 11, tom. II.)* Being about to speak of the figure of the tabernacle, etc., *as far as* that the Church might be designated as about to speak throughout the world with the tongues of all nations.

*And I will give thee.* [n: (ID.)] Like to this is that: *Teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you [n: (Matth. XXVIII)]*. Therefore neither Moses, nor the apostles, nor anyone whatever, ought to teach other things than those which the Lord taught and prescribed by the decree of his counsel to be observed, in which he willed that we should have eternal life; and just as nothing of these ought to be passed over, so it is needful to commit to the hearers all things which he commanded.

VERS. 13.-- *Moses and Josue rose up. [n: (AUG., quaest. 103 in Exod.)]* Josue, not mentioned among those four, suddenly appears with Moses, etc., *as far [0266A] as* that at the time now recorded he was not yet called Jesus, but when it was written.

[n: (BED.)] Josue signifies Christ in name and in action; who is called the minister of Moses, because Christ *came not to destroy the law, but to fulfil [n: (Matth. V)]*. He follows the footsteps of Moses in all things, because in all that Moses wrote he is typically designated, and, as an inseparable companion, is found if he be rightly sought. Whence John V: *If you did believe Moses, you would perhaps believe me also.*

[n: (BED., de Tabernac., lib. I, cap. 1, tom. II.)] Aaron ( אהרן ) is interpreted *the mountain of strength,* Hur ( חור ) *fire* or *light,* etc., *as far as* and the mind burns toward the love of the Creator.

VERS. 15.-- *And when Moses was gone up, [n: (BEDA., ibid.)]* As the mountain the height of perfection, etc., *as far [0266B] as* he protects with heavenly blessing.

VERS. 16.-- *And the glory of the Lord dwelt. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* Not the loftiness only, but the very name of the mountain, etc., *as far as* on the seventh we are bidden to rest.

*Covering it with a cloud. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* For six days he is covered with the cloud and with the glory of the Lord, etc., *as far as* that he might come through to the loftier gifts of the divine vision and converse.

VERS. 17.-- *And the appearance was,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] The appearance of the glory of the Lord appeared as a burning fire, etc., *as far as* although they are not able to follow them fully.

VERS. 18.-- *And Moses entering in,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] Moses, having entered the midst of the cloud, went up into the mountain, etc., *as far as* that by the observance of them they may come to eternal salvation.



## CHAPTER XXV.

[0266C]

VERS. 2.-- *Speak to the children of Israel, that they bring me firstfruits,* etc. [n: (BED., de Tabernac., lib. I, cap. III, tom. II.)] We offer to God the firstfruits of our goods when, ascribing wholly to divine grace the good that we do, we profess with our whole heart that we cannot have even the beginning of a good action or thought save from the Lord; but that evils are always both begun and consummated by us at the instigation of the devil, and are not to be loosed save by the Lord's gift.

VERS. 3-6.-- *And these are the things you must take,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] All the things which the Lord commanded to be offered materially for the making of the sanctuary, we also, who desire to be spiritual children of Israel, that is, imitators [0266D] of the people that sees God, ought to offer spiritually: that by these voluntary oblations we may ourselves make a sanctuary within us, so that he may vouchsafe to dwell in our heart and to consecrate a habitation to himself.

VERS. 7.-- *Onyx stones,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] These we offer, etc., *as far as* are signified the merits of martyrdom added to good works.

VERS. 8.-- *And they shall make me a sanctuary. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* The Lord showed Moses the tabernacle on the mountain, etc., *as far as* it received the glorious multitude of holy souls.

VERS. 9-11.-- *According to all the likeness,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] We ought to imitate the angels by loving God [0267A] and our neighbour, etc., *as far as* and after this life may bring thee into that tabernacle which thou imitatest.

*Of all the vessels.* [n: (BED.)] The vessels of the tabernacle are the several persons of the angels, of whom Jerusalem, the mother of us all, consists in true peace and unity.

*And thus you shall make it. An ark,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid., cap. 4.)] The ark is commanded to be made, etc., *as far as* but he came in the likeness of sinful flesh.

*The length whereof* [n: (ID.)] It is asked of what size this cubit is, etc., *as far as* whence it appears that it designates that cubit which two palms make.

*The breadth. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* The breadth of one cubit for the dispensation of the Lord's charity, etc., *as far as* where we may love him with our whole heart, let us enter.

[0267B] [n: (ID.)] *Mystically.* The length of the ark the long-suffering of Christ, etc., *as far as* because he bore with long-suffering the slowness of the disciples.

*The height, a cubit,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] The ark has a cubit in height, etc., and *as far as* this little we have not save from him.

VERS. 12-15.-- *And four rings,* etc. [n: (GREG. Past., cap. 11, tom. III.)] The ark, the Church, etc., *as far as which is in you,* etc.

*Two rings.* [n: (BEDA, ut supra. )] Because in the figure of the four living creatures, etc., *as far as* they expressed the things which pertain to the glory of the life to come.

VERS. 16.-- *The testimony which I will give. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* Of which the Apostle, etc., *as far as* he taught the judge of all ages.

[0267C]VERS. 17.-- *A propitiatory.* [n: (ID.)] The propitiatory was a golden board of the same measure as the ark; so called because there God, appearing to Moses and speaking with him, was made propitious to the people; and it signifies mercy, whence it was fittingly above the ark, because his mercy is above all his works.

VERS. 18-21.-- *Two cherubim also,* etc. [n: (ID.)] Because of the fellowship of angelic peace, which cannot be had among fewer than two. Now the cherubim signify the angelic host; and the angels contemplate Christ in the highest concord and quiet; whence, with faces turned toward one another, they look upon the propitiatory, because they understand that they have that beatitude by the grace of God.

[n: (GREG., hom. 6 in Ezech.)] The two cherubim, which are called the *fulness [0267D] of knowledge,* etc., *as far as* if he promised one thing while he denied another.

[n: (BEDA, ibid.)] In the singular number it is called *cherub,* כרוב but in the plural *cherubim,* כרובים etc., *as far as* they are well designated by wings spread out and fitted for flying.

VERS. 22.-- *Thence will I give orders,* etc., *over the propitiatory. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* Over the propitiatory, etc., *as far as* whence Habacuc says: *In the midst of two living creatures thou shalt be made known [n: (Habac. III)]*. [var: Vulg. Hab 3:2 *in medio annorum notum facies*, "in the midst of the years thou shalt make it known"; the whole gloss rests on the two living creatures]

[n: (BED., ibid.)] In the ark the Church can be figured, etc., *as far as* wherein they themselves stand established upon the foundation of the highest truth.

[n: (BED., ibid.)] The table of setim wood, etc., *as far as* [0268A] whence Cant. II: *The king brought me into the wine cellar, and he set in order charity in me.* [var: Vulg. Ct 2:4 *Introduxit me in cellam vinariam, ordinavit in me caritatem*, without *rex*, "he brought me in"]

VERS. 23.-- *In height a cubit,* etc. [n: (BED., ibid.)] That the height has a cubit and a half, etc., *as far as* it is not figured by a full cubit.

VERS. 23.-- *And thou shalt make for it a lip.,* etc. [n: (BED., ibid.)] A golden lip is made on the table round about, etc., *as far as* according to the diversity of merits, the glory of each. [var: Vulg. these words stand at Ex 25:24; Migne's address repeats VERS. 23]

VERS. 24.-- *A crown,* etc., and *over it another crown. [n: (BED., ibid.)]* A golden crown is added, since through the Gospel to those who keep the commandments, etc., *as far as* that a loftier glory is kept for it in the receiving back of their bodies.

VERS. 26.-- *For every one,* etc. [n: (BED., ibid.)] The table [0268B] of the tabernacle has four feet, etc., *as far as* they may enter into the kingdom of heaven.

VERS. 27.-- *That the bars may be put through them. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* Through the rings the bars are put for carrying the table, etc., *as far as* they carry the table of the Lord with the loaves and the vessels in four rings.

VERS. 29.-- *Thou shalt prepare also dishes and bowls. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* The various vessels which are made for offering libations, etc., *as far as* whence: *Who, thinkest thou, is the faithful and prudent steward [n: (Luc. XII)]*?

VERS. 29.-- *Thou shalt prepare dishes,* etc. [n: (GREG., hom. 6 in Evang., tom. II.)] What is understood by the bowls and the cups but the measure of preaching? etc., *as far as* but also small and subtle things, which afford knowledge as it were by a taste.

[0268C] [n: (IDEM.)] One man, full of the doctrine of truth, inebriates his hearers, and as it were holds out a bowl; another is not strong enough to fill out what he perceives, yet, because he does declare it after some fashion, affords by a cup a taste. Those therefore who cannot minister bowls through the doctrine of wisdom, let them, so far as they suffice by the divine bounty, pour out to their neighbours cups of the word.

VERS. 30.-- *And thou shalt set upon the table the loaves of proposition in my sight always.* Josephus says that twelve loaves were made of fine flour, each of two tenths, which on the Sabbath were set upon the table, six on this side and six on that: upon each one a golden dish with a handful of incense, and thus they were kept until another Sabbath, on which the incense was burned; but the priests ate the loaves warm, putting back the incense.

[0268D] *And thou shalt set upon the table,* etc. [n: (BED. ubi supra. )] They are rightly called the loaves of proposition, because the word of salvation ought always to lie open to all the faithful, nor ought the word of comfort ever to be wanting in the Church: which word the Lord, proposing it to the world through the heralds of truth, willed to appear in his sight and to abound unceasingly, even unto the end, for those who hunger and thirst after justice.

VERS. 31.-- *Thou shalt make also a candlestick,* etc. [n: (GREG., hom. 6 in Ezech.)] The candlestick is Christ, in whom the nature of humanity shone with the light of the Godhead, etc., *as far as* the goblets therefore and the little spheres pertain to labour, the lilies to reward.

*Thou shalt make also a candlestick of beaten work of the purest gold.* [0269A] [n: (BEDA, ibid.)] The candlestick of the tabernacle, etc., *as far as* and by preaching and by doing he showed the brightness of the word to all.

*And the branches, the goblets and the little spheres. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* Namely, preachers proceeding from the shaft, etc., *as far as* which blooms for holy souls, that is, for eternal flowers.

VERS. 32.-- *Six branches,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] The number six, in which the world was made, etc., *as far as* whence Paul: *The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, and the charity of God, and the communication of the Holy Spirit, be always with you all.* [var: Vulg. 2Cor 13:13 *sit cum omnibus vobis*, without *semper*, "be with you all"]

*Three out of one. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* Three branches out of one side, etc., *as far as* he shall enjoy the vision of him.

[n: (ID., ubi supra )]. The goblets are made on the candlestick after the manner of a nut, etc., *as far as* because the continent of that [0269B] time had three orders of saints by the same distinction of times.

VERS. 33.-- *In the manner of a nut.* [n: (ID.)] The nut signifies the Church, whence: *He went down into the garden of nuts.* [var: Vulg. Ct 6:10 *Descendi in hortum nucum*, "I went down into the garden of nuts"; Migne's third person moves the words out of the Bride's mouth] For the nut has a sweet fruit within, which is not shown without unless the shell be broken: so the life of the saints keeps the sweetness of grace in the heart, which is not known outwardly, save when, the dwelling of the flesh being loosed, they behold themselves in the heavenly light.

[n: (ID.)] The third branch likewise has three goblets and little spheres, and three lilies: because many married persons were before the law, under the law and under the prophets, who rejoiced to hear the word of the Lord, and to run his way, and to look for the rewards of good works.

[n: (ID.)] Likewise from the other side the first branch, the second [0269C] and the third, three goblets and little spheres; three lilies also it had: because in both Testaments, among the doctors, among the continent, and among the married that are devoted to God, there were three orders by a distance of times. Namely in the primitive Church out of Israel, in our election out of the Gentiles, in the last gathering of the remnants of Israel: all of whom, inebriated in their own time with the word of life, desire to hasten in the way of peace, and to see the whiteness of perpetual light.

VERS. 34.-- *And in the candlestick itself,* etc. [n: (ID., ubi supra. )] We have said that the candlestick itself, etc., *as far as* but hearts renewed through the Spirit receive them.

VERS. 35.-- *And little spheres under every one, and lilies. Little spheres under two branches,* etc.

[n: (ID., ibid.)] There are under the several goblets little spheres [0269D] together with lilies, etc., *as far as* whence: *His left hand under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me.*

[n: (ID., ibid.)] The goblets, the little spheres and the lilies are under two branches, etc., *as far as all the fulness of the Godhead corporeally.*

VERS. 36.-- *The little spheres therefore and the branches. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* The whole work of the candlestick, etc., *as far as* whence: *In tribulation thou hast enlarged me,* etc.

VERS. 37.-- *Thou shalt make.* [n: (ID.)] The seven lamps are the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit, which always remained in Christ, and are distributed to the faithful according to his will: these are set upon the candlestick, because the Spirit of wisdom and understanding rested upon him [n: (Isai. XI)].

[0270A] *That they may give light,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] The lamps of the candlestick lighted up the tabernacle over against them, when Christ, full of grace and truth, bestowed on all out of his fulness; committing the word of the Gospel to the meek and the poor, conferring on the penitent the remedy of pardon, affirming that now is the time of sparing, in the time to come the time of the universal judgment.

VERS. 38.-- *Snuffers also, and where the things. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* The divine words, by which it is manifestly signified that in very many places not the letter of the law but the spiritual sense is to be kept: whence: *If you be circumcised, Christ shall profit you nothing.*

[n: (ID., ibid.)] The snuffers, and the vessels where the things that are snuffed off are extinguished, etc., *as far as* saying: *Whether prophecies shall be made void, or tongues shall cease,* etc.

[0270B] *Snuffers also,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] There are certain precepts in the Scriptures, etc., *as far as* and elsewhere: *Behold, I make all things new.*

VERS. 39.-- *The whole. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* This is the body of Christ together with the head itself, that is, all the elect, etc., *as far as* because by the desert of their crimes they fall down into the abyss of oppressing punishment.

VERS. 40.-- *Look. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* There was shown to Moses on the mountain the pattern of the candlestick which he should make, etc., *as far as* he showed the pattern to his hearers by the performance of the work and by the word of doctrine.



## CHAPTER XXVI.

VERS. 1.-- *And the tabernacle shall so be made,* etc. [n: (BED., lib. II de Tabernac., cap. 3, tom. II.)] [var: Vulg. Ex 26:1 *Tabernaculum vero ita facies*, "the tabernacle thou shalt make thus"; Migne's impersonal passive drops the command addressed to Moses] The tabernacle [0270C] which Moses made in the wilderness, and the temple which Solomon made in Jerusalem, etc., *as far as* and they may be joined by the bond of divine and of proper love.

[n: (ID., ubi supra. )] The figure of both sanctuaries can be distinguished thus, etc., *as far as* he depicts with a wonderful variety of figures.

*Of twisted fine linen, and of hyacinth and purple, and scarlet twice dyed, diversified. [n: (ID., ubi supra, cap. 2.)]* In these four colours of the curtains, etc., *as far as* the purple, of a bloody colour and dyed with the blood of shellfish, figures hearts devoted to God and ready to die for God.

VERS. 2.-- *The length of one curtain shall have twenty-eight cubits, the breadth four,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] [0270D] If from one up to seven thou number all the numbers in order, by a full order, *as far as* that through faith and good works it may tend toward rest.

*Of one measure,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] Although the curtains differed by a varying embroidery, yet they were all tempered to one measure of length and breadth, because although the elect have differing gifts, yet there is one Lord, one faith, one baptism, one God and Father of all.

[n: (ID., ubi supra. )] Josephus relates, etc., *as far as* the tabernacle is adorned as it were by the one connecting of ten curtains.

VERS. 4.-- *Loops of hyacinth in the sides,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] By loops of hyacinth the curtains are coupled to one another, [0271A] etc., *as far as* and they trust that they shall abide in it for ever.

[n: (BEDA, ibid.)] It was commanded also that fringes should be made at the corners of the cloaks, etc., *as far as* being gathered from the four winds, we shall be joined in an indivisible fellowship.

VERS. 5.-- *Fifty each,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] In the fiftieth year, etc., *as far as* and in the receiving of which there is truly rest and joy, it is rightly figured.

[n: (ID., ibid.)] The curtains have fifty loops, because it is brought about by the gift of the Holy Spirit that the elect are coupled to one another in the fellowship of peace, which is the bond of perfection: and the hope also and the memory of that fellowship and peace to come, which is had in this life, makes those who are separated by times or by places to be of one accord in the same piety.

[0271B] [n: (ID., ibid.)] The curtains have loops on either part, etc., *as far as* when the just are leagued together by a harmonious quality of virtues.

*Thou shalt make also fifty rings. [n: (ID., ibid.)]* This is more fully explained when it is said, etc., *as far as* nay rather, that out of all the elect the Church of Christ may be perfected.

VERS. 7.-- *Thou shalt make also tent-cloths,* etc. [n: (ID., ibid.)] This is found thus in what follows: *He made also eleven tent-cloths of goats' hair,* etc. The tent-cloths, with which the tabernacle is covered, etc., *as far as* while like tent-cloths they bear the storms without.
